A reliability study administering the ADIS-IV-L twice, independently, to 362 outpatients found good-to-excellent categorical reliability, with the most common source of disagreement being whether constituent symptoms met threshold for number, severity or duration — and, importantly for a treatment manual, found favourable interrater agreement specifically for the 0–8 dimensional severity ratings, meaning the CSR is more reliable than the categorical diagnosis it accompanies and worth recording alongside (not instead of) the yes/no diagnosis at intake and discharge. SCID reliability for social phobia is more modest and more variable across sites: earlier DSM-IV SCID-I/P work reported inter-rater κ of 0.63 and test–retest κ of 0.59 for social phobia ("fair" agreement), a Turkish SCID-5-CV validation in 185 patients found κ = 0.95 for SAD specifically, while a Persian SCID-5-CV validation found κ exceeded 0.80 for most diagnoses against a psychiatrist gold standard but fell to κ = 0.34 (moderate) for anxiety disorders as a class, with the authors explicitly recommending the interview be used "with caution" for anxiety diagnoses. That heterogeneity is worth stating to the reader rather than quoting a single reliability figure as if it generalised: which SCID reliability number applies depends heavily on site and translation. The large multi-centre DSM-5 field trials, testing 15 adult and 8 child/adolescent diagnoses across 11 academic centres with intraclass kappa targeted to a standard error below 0.1, found reliability spread across the full range — 5 diagnoses "very good" (κ 0.60–0.79), 9 "good" (0.40–0.59), 6 "questionable" (0.20–0.39), 3 "unacceptable" (< 0.20) — but did not separately report a kappa for SAD, so the ADIS/SCID figures above remain the disorder-specific reference. No dedicated psychometric validation of ADIS-5 for SAD could be located; the closest indexed evidence comes from a study comparing ADIS-5 classification against best-estimate clinical diagnosis in autistic adults, which found strong baseline concordance for depression and generalised anxiety but more variable agreement at outcome, and concluded explicitly that more research is needed to standardise clinical diagnostic interview practice. That gap should be stated plainly rather than papered over: ADIS-5 is in wide clinical use for SAD without a published SAD-specific reliability figure to cite.

### 3.6 The idiographic battery: what actually drives the session

The measures above answer "how severe is this patient's SAD." They do not, by themselves, tell a therapist what to do in session four. Four further tools carry that load.

**The fear-and-avoidance hierarchy.** Built collaboratively at the start of treatment, typically listing a set of concrete situations spanning both performance and interaction domains — the same two domains the LSAS itself samples — each situation is rated for predicted distress (a subjective units of distress, or SUDS, rating) and current avoidance, then ordered from least to most feared. This ordering is what sequences exposure: the therapist and patient agree to work up the list rather than starting at the top, and the hierarchy is revisited and re-rated as items are mastered, giving a running, patient-specific outcome measure that the standardised scales cannot provide.

**The behavioural assessment test or speech task.** No single standardised BAT protocol for SAD exists in the indexed literature, which is a genuine hole a clinician should know about rather than assume has been solved. The nearest thing to a validated observer-coding system is a scale that rates videotaped role-plays on five dimensions — gaze, vocal quality, speech length, discomfort, and conversation flow — validated across social phobia, other-anxiety-disorder and no-disorder comparison groups with excellent inter-rater reliability for both individual items and the total score, and convergent, divergent and criterion-related validity. In practice this means: script a brief standardised task (commonly a short impromptu speech to camera or a small audience, followed by a brief unscripted conversation with a confederate), video it, and score it on those five dimensions before and periodically during treatment. A related line of work in 105 adolescents found that even untrained, unfamiliar observers rating brief live interactions with unfamiliar peer confederates produced ratings that related to the patient's own self-report, distinguished clinically elevated social anxiety, tracked with trained observers' social-skills ratings, and correlated with self-reported arousal in the same interaction — useful evidence that informal behavioural observation carries real signal even without an elaborate coding apparatus, which matters when a full standardised BAT is not feasible in routine practice.

**Safety-behaviour inventories.** The Subtle Avoidance Frequency Examination (SAFE) resolves into three factors — active safety behaviours, subtle restriction of behaviour, and behaviours aimed at avoiding or concealing physical symptoms — with strong internal consistency, good construct validity, clear discrimination between clinical and non-clinical respondents, and demonstrated responsiveness to group psychotherapy. This is the instrument to track session-by-session during exposure work specifically because safety behaviours are the mechanism exposure without safety behaviours is designed to remove; a flat or rising SAFE score across sessions where exposure difficulty is nominally increasing is a direct signal that the exposures are not achieving their intended function.

**Post-event processing.** A German revision of the original post-event-processing questionnaire, refined across a 130-participant item-analysis study and validated in a further 268 participants, achieved excellent internal consistency and meaningful correlations with anxiety, depression and dysfunctional self-consciousness — and, structurally, a four-factor model fit the data better than the original one-dimensional conception, meaning post-event rumination is not a single construct and a single total score may obscure which facet is actually elevated in a given patient.

**Self-focused attention — and a genuine negative finding.** In a battery study administering a full Clark-and-Wells maintaining-factor set (belief measures, safety-behaviour and post-event-processing measures, and a self-focused-attention measure) to 220 adolescent students, social anxiety correlated with anticipatory processing (r = 0.33, p < 0.001), maladaptive social-evaluative beliefs (r = 0.58, p < 0.001), safety behaviours (r = 0.63, p < 0.001) and post-event processing (r = 0.28, p < 0.001) — but high- and low-socially-anxious groups differed significantly on every maintaining factor except the self-focused-attention measure (p = 0.235). That single negative finding is worth taking seriously in an idiographic battery: existing self-report measures of self-focused attention may be the weakest link in this set, and a clinician who wants to track self-focus reliably may get more mileage from a behavioural proxy (observed gaze aversion on the SPRS, for instance) than from a self-report scale. The same study is also the only indexed record naming the Social Phobia Weekly Summary Scale — the session-by-session tracking instrument used inside individual cognitive therapy on the Clark and Wells model — and no separate psychometric validation of it could be located; treat it as clinically established but psychometrically unverified.

**Belief-rating scales.** The Self-Beliefs related to Social Anxiety scale operationalises the three belief types the Clark and Wells model names as maintaining — high standards, conditional beliefs, and unconditional negative beliefs about the self — as a correlated three-factor structure, validated first in 235 undergraduates plus 33 patients with good internal consistency, test–retest reliability, and incremental and discriminative validity, then confirmed in a pooled sample of 284 patients with diagnosed SAD (plus 32 without). In that diagnosed sample, the total score and the high-standards and conditional-beliefs subscales related more strongly to social anxiety than to depression, but the unconditional-beliefs subscale did not discriminate — it related about equally to both — an interpretive caveat worth keeping in mind if that particular subscale is used to track progress. A parallel instrument, the Core Beliefs Questionnaire, comes in three parallel versions (Trait, Contingent, Other), validated in 269 clinical and 67 non-clinical respondents: the Trait version tracks more closely with depression, the Contingent version relates similarly to both constructs, and the Other version relates more strongly to social anxiety specifically — making it the version to choose when SAD-specificity matters — and all three versions fell from pre- to post-treatment with the magnitude of change predicting treatment outcome, supporting their use as process measures across a course of therapy and not only at intake.

### 3.7 Measurement-based care: assembling and using the battery

No SAD-specific randomised trial of measurement-based care could be located in the indexed literature — a real gap, and this manual should not overstate what routine progress monitoring has been shown to do specifically for this disorder. The transdiagnostic evidence is nonetheless substantial and directly applicable. A multilevel meta-analysis spanning 58 studies, 110 effect sizes and 21,699 patients found progress feedback reduced symptoms relative to control by a small but real margin (d = 0.15, 95% CI 0.10–0.20), with a larger effect specifically in patients flagged as not on track (d = 0.17, 95% CI 0.11–0.22) — precisely the population measurement-based care is designed to catch — and reduced drop-out (OR = 1.19, 95% CI 1.03–1.38). A separate meta-analysis of 24 studies using established feedback systems found that two-thirds showed feedback-assisted therapy outperforming treatment-as-usual delivered by the same practitioners, with feedback reducing deterioration rates and nearly doubling clinically-significant/reliable-change rates specifically among clients predicted to have a poor outcome. An international consensus process for children's and young people's anxiety and related disorders — 27 clinical, research and lived-experience experts, screening 70 candidate outcomes and 107 candidate instruments down to a final set of 7, validated against 487 respondents across 45 countries — is worth noting for what it did not do: no SAD-specific instrument made that final standard set, which should temper any assumption that a single "gold standard" tracking instrument for SAD has been internationally agreed.

Given that gap, the practical battery below is assembled from the instruments above rather than drawn from a single published recommendation, and is offered as a working structure rather than a validated protocol in its own right. At intake: a structured interview (ADIS-5 or SCID-5-CV) for diagnosis and subtype, with a 0–8 clinician severity rating recorded alongside the categorical diagnosis — repeat the CSR at discharge, since it is the more reliable of the two judgments. If diagnosis is not yet established and the setting is screening rather than treatment, mini-SPIN (cut-off ≥ 6) or full SPIN (cut-off 19, or 25 in some European validations) is efficient. For ongoing severity tracking, the full LSAS-SR every four sessions or so is the trial-grade instrument and the one with published response (≥ 28% reduction) and remission (≤ 35) cut-points; a shorter instrument — mini-SPIN, or SIAS-6/SPS-6 — is more practical for every-session tracking, with the explicit understanding that the short forms are validated for within-patient trend monitoring but not for between-treatment outcome comparison, where ceiling effects and inflated variance have been shown to obscure real differences. Mechanism-level tracking every two to four sessions should draw from the idiographic battery: BFNE-S for fear of negative evaluation, SBSA or the Other-version CBQ for maladaptive beliefs, SAFE for safety behaviours, and the individualised fear-and-avoidance hierarchy re-rated as items are attempted. At discharge, report the LSAS-SR against both its remission (≤ 35) and response (≥ 28% reduction) cut-points explicitly, rather than a raw change score alone, since no anchor-based minimal clinically important difference has been published for the LSAS and these bootstrap-derived cut-points are the best available substitute. Because no published Reliable Change Index exists for any of the SAD-specific scales either, a single session's fluctuation should not be over-interpreted — the feedback literature's own "not on track" signal is built from a trend across several observations, not one data point, and the same discipline should govern how a clinician reads week-to-week LSAS-SR or SIAS-6 movement.

## 4. Differential diagnosis and comorbidity at intake

Two questions structure the intake hour: which near neighbor does this presentation belong to, and which comorbidities change what happens in session one. Neither is answered by a single sign; each is answered by a pattern the interviewer can elicit directly, checked against numbers.

### Avoidant personality disorder

Size the overlap first. In a national US survey of over 43,000 adults, lifetime generalized social phobia (GSP) ran 2.8% and avoidant personality disorder (AvPD) 2.4%; 36.4% of GSP cases also carried AvPD, rising to 57.3% among those fearing all 13 assessed social situations — overlap tracks fear *breadth*, not a fixed proportion — and roughly 40% of AvPD carried GSP.

The continuum question splits the evidence. For it: a Norwegian twin study of 1,427 female–female pairs found the *same* additive genetic factors drove both disorders, with *uncorrelated* environments. Against it: social-phobia-alone patients had higher global functioning than AvPD-alone patients in a 2,192-patient sample, and AvPD tracked eating disorders far more strongly where social phobia tracked panic disorder; 15 of 91 patients in a dedicated comparison had AvPD *without* SAD at all; and AvPD patients reported more severe childhood neglect, especially physical, surviving control for temperament and concurrent abuse. AvPD is not simply severe SAD with a different label. Three intake questions do more work than a symptom count: a pervasively negative self-concept; rejection construed as a verdict on global worth rather than one bad interaction; and not-fitting-in dating from early childhood, before any specific trauma. The single most informative item in one large sample was AvPD criterion 3 — difficulty being open even with people one is close to — tracking disability and quality of life once symptom *count*, not a bare diagnosis, entered as predictor.

**Does it moderate which psychotherapy to run?** The trial built to test this randomized 154 outpatients carrying BOTH SAD and AvPD as principal diagnoses to 30 weekly group sessions (roughly nine months) of cognitive behavioral therapy (n = 79) or schema therapy (n = 75), with the Liebowitz Social Anxiety Scale and an Avoidant Personality Disorder Severity Index as co-primary outcomes. Intention-to-treat analysis found no significant between-group difference at 3-month or 1-year follow-up on either measure, or on depression or quality of life; both arms produced large within-group change (Liebowitz d 1.07–1.50). The only significant difference was completion — schema therapy retained more patients — and a companion moderator analysis of the same 154, testing trauma, depressive load, personality and self-esteem, found none. Smaller older trials converge on a shape: a 28-study review and a 244-patient trial of the Clark and Wells cognitive therapy protocol both found comorbidity and severity predict worse **end-state functioning**, not a smaller **degree of improvement** — comorbid patients start further back and finish further back moving the same distance. A paroxetine/cognitive-therapy/combination/placebo trial, 54% AvPD, found cognitive therapy alone still beat both paroxetine and combination at 12 months (recovery 68% vs 40% vs 24% vs 4%).

**Intake rule:** name AvPD when present, but do not delay exposure work to "treat the personality first," and do not switch protocols on an AvPD diagnosis alone — track completion risk, not efficacy risk.

### Panic disorder and agoraphobia

The operative distinction is the *object* of fear: in SAD, panic-like surges are cued by evaluative exposure and are not themselves what is feared; in panic disorder the sensations and the attack itself are the feared object, and avoidance generalizes to any setting where escape feels restricted, regardless of an audience. Ask what the patient fears if no one were watching — panic disorder's fear survives that question, SAD's largely does not. Social phobia associated more strongly with panic disorder than AvPD did in one clinical sample, and comorbid agoraphobia is not neutral for treatment selection: in an 80-patient residential trial, patients with concurrent agoraphobia did better with residential cognitive therapy than residential interpersonal therapy. Comorbidity itself neither predicted nor moderated outcome in a metacognitive-versus-cognitive-therapy trial of 74 patients with primary PTSD, social phobia or panic disorder ± agoraphobia.

### Generalized anxiety disorder

The boundary is content and scope: GAD worry roams across health, finances, family and multiple domains; SAD's fear is anchored to scrutiny and evaluation in social or performance contexts specifically. Dedicated discriminant-validity work is sparse, but where GAD has been tested as a comorbidity it behaves like depression: comorbid GAD predicted the *level* of social anxiety across individual CBT for SAD in a 93-patient cohort, but not the *amount of change* achieved.

### Autism spectrum disorder — the hardest boundary

Base rates in youth: a meta-analysis of 31 studies (2,121 children and adolescents with ASD) found 39.6% carried at least one comorbid anxiety disorder, social anxiety disorder specifically at 16.6%. In adults, a direct comparison of 28 people with ASD against 26 with SAD-no-ASD found 50% of the ASD group met full SAD criteria — social anxiety in autism is frequently a bona fide, independently diagnosable disorder, not merely secondary insight into social difficulty. An 83-article meta-analysis found ASD anxiety exceeds that of typically developing children, with the gap *widening with IQ* — higher-functioning autistic people are, if anything, more anxious, not less — and also exceeds clinically-referred non-ASD children, a gap that widens with age.

The intake risk runs the other direction too: autism can be entirely undiagnosed and hiding inside a "treatment-resistant social anxiety" presentation. A study of 667 university students found 0.7–1.9% could meet criteria for high-functioning autism depending on ascertainment method, and none of the five who met full criteria had ever been previously diagnosed; those above the autism-symptom threshold self-reported *more* social anxiety than matched peers.

The single most useful discriminator concerns what the patient can actually tell you. In 191 autistic children with anxiety disorders, those with impaired theory of mind were significantly *less* likely to spontaneously express fear of negative evaluation — a distinct presentation that will not surface if the clinician relies on the standard "are you worried what people think of you?" probe; negative-evaluation fear must be inferred from behavior and situational specificity rather than elicited by self-report alone. This tracks a broader pattern: ASD adults score higher than non-ASD comparisons on social-anxiety measures but significantly *lower* than a dedicated SAD sample — a severity gradient, not equivalence. An eye-tracking study adds a signature observable in the room: social anxiety alone affects only the *first* fixation duration on another person's eyes (vigilance then avoidance, inside a second), while autistic traits produce eye avoidance persisting across *later* time points; people high in both show early avoidance plus sustained avoidance once fixated.

**Treatment:** both diagnoses can and should be given together where present; anxiety-focused CBT adapted for autistic youth is an active field. The only randomized signal specific to social-anxiety pharmacotherapy in autistic adults is a 12-person MDMA-assisted-psychotherapy pilot (8 active, 4 placebo) across two 8-hour sessions plus non-drug integration sessions; the Liebowitz improvement reached significance (p = 0.037) with a placebo-subtracted effect size of 1.4, but the confidence interval crossed zero at both 1- and 6-month endpoints — hypothesis-generating only.

### Selective mutism

Treat selective mutism as social anxiety expressed through a speech-specific avoidance operant, not "very shy child." Overlap is close to total: 94% of 95 children with selective mutism also met criteria for social phobia, versus a 74-patient comparison group. But the two diverge — more separation anxiety (45% vs 26%), more oppositional defiant disorder (22% vs 5%), less generalized anxiety and depression — and self-report doesn't confirm the intuitive story: mutism children did *not* rate themselves as more socially anxious than social-phobia children, though clinicians rated them more distressed and higher on oppositional behavior. Fear *content* is nearly identical (59% of spontaneously reported fears are social; the groups matched on 33 of 34 fear cognitions), and physiology suggests silence itself is avoidance: elevated resting arousal, dropping in *anticipation* of a verbal task before it starts. Family loading points toward the SAD spectrum: parents of mutism children carried generalized social phobia at more than double the control rate (37.0% vs 14.1%, OR 3.6) and AvPD at nearly four times the rate (17.5% vs 4.7%, OR 4.3).

A 5-year follow-up of school-based CBT for selective mutism found the large majority in full or partial remission, but 23% of the cohort met social phobia criteria at follow-up — screen for autism features too, since one synthesis places selective mutism at the SAD/ASD junction, with restricted/repetitive behaviors implicated in maintaining the difficulty. **Intake rule:** graduated exposure to verbal communication is the active ingredient; expect continuing social-phobia risk after successful mutism treatment, and plan for continuity of care rather than discharge.

### Body dysmorphic disorder

The two run bidirectionally comorbid (BDD present in 4.8–12% of SAD cases across studies; SAD in 12–68.8% of BDD cases), and BDD ranks fourth most common comorbid disorder in SAD, after simple phobia, alcohol abuse and depression. Temporal order separates BDD from depression and substance use: social phobia usually *began before* BDD onset in a 293-patient cohort, while depression and substance use typically developed *after*.

The discriminating question is the *locus* of the feared consequence: in BDD, public attention to a specific perceived physical defect; in SAD, negative evaluation across social or performance situations for any of several reasons. Ask what specifically the patient believes observers will notice — a named body part answers BDD. Delusionality separates the disorders further than social anxiety does: BDD carries referential thinking and, in a meaningful subset, frank delusional conviction with poorer insight than OCD, needing serotonergic medication regardless of psychotherapy. Within BDD, elevated social anxiety tracks comorbid AvPD, not comorbid social phobia — its own construct, not a SAD formulation by another name. **Intake rule:** do not treat appearance preoccupation as a social-anxiety cognition; BDD carries a different suicide risk and needs mirror/perceptual retraining and ritual response prevention standard SAD protocols do not contain.

### Paranoid ideation and the psychosis prodrome

Rates are substantial enough that this boundary belongs at every intake, not only where psychosis is suspected: a meta-analysis of 25 studies (92,522 people with a psychotic disorder) found pooled SAD prevalence of 21% (95% CI 16–26%), higher outpatient (25%) than inpatient (9%) — and comorbid SAD tracked *greater*, not lesser, insight. In clinical-high-risk (CHR) populations the rate approaches the schizophrenia-spectrum figure: a review of 124 studies found social phobia in 14.4% of CHR individuals versus 0.36% of controls; the one study testing social anxiety as a predictor of transition to first-episode psychosis found no predictive power.

The cleanest experimental discriminator comes from a virtual-reality study of 200 general-population adults in a neutral social environment: perceptual anomalies — things not quite seeming right — *increased* the risk of a paranoid reaction but *decreased* the risk of social anxiety, while ordinary anxiety, depression and interpersonal sensitivity predicted both similarly. Ask about anomalous perception specifically; its presence pushes the differential toward paranoia, its absence keeps you in SAD territory even with high suspiciousness. This holds in first-episode psychosis directly: social anxiety was unrelated to PANSS positive symptoms including suspiciousness in an 80-patient sample, and the *majority* of socially anxious first-episode patients had no concurrent persecutory delusions — social anxiety in psychosis needs its own treatment target, not paranoia's.

**Prognostic significance:** a 2-year trajectory study of 764 CHR individuals found a subgroup whose social anxiety stayed elevated *despite* recovery in positive symptoms, and that subgroup had the poorest long-term functional outcomes of the trajectories identified — sustained social anxiety in CHR is a functional-outcome marker in its own right.

### Ordinary shyness

Self-report instruments cannot separate the constructs: across ten shyness and social-anxiety questionnaires in 801 adults, everything loaded onto a single factor. That does not mean the disorder is a medicalization of a trait — impairment data settle the question the other way. Social phobia was present in 18% of self-identified shy people versus 3% of non-shy people: 82% of shy individuals were *not* socially phobic. A finer-grained study of 2,202 people found social phobia in roughly half the "highly shy" (90th-percentile) group versus 18% "normatively shy," and generalized social phobia specifically at 36% versus 4% — but *non*generalized social phobia was identical, 14% versus 14%: trait shyness tracks the generalized presentation only.

The threshold that matters is impairment, and it scales continuously: a national survey found comorbidity, role impairment and treatment-seeking all showed a dose–response relationship with the *number* of social fears, no natural break by content or count — no bright categorical line, but the diagnosis is defended by accumulating impairment, not kind. Shyness and social anxiety as traits also associate with *lower* adolescent substance use, while diagnosable SAD associates with *higher* use — a direction reversal a pure "more of the same trait" account cannot produce.

### Comorbidity as a treatment-planning problem

The recurring shape below is the same: comorbidity depresses where a patient *ends up*, far more consistently than it slows how fast they move. Ask "does this predict a worse endpoint" separately from "does this mean I sequence treatment."

### Depression

Onset order runs SAD-first more often than not, because social anxiety is an adolescent-onset disorder and depression is not: a 10-year prospective study of 3,021 people found SAD's incidence peaked narrowly between ages 10 and 19 and consistently predicted subsequent depression regardless of when SAD itself began. In non-depressed adolescents and young adults, SAD prospectively predicted new-onset depression at odds ratio 3.5 (95% CI 2.0–6.0), and where already comorbid at baseline, depression course was worse — persistence-or-recurrence OR 2.3 (1.2–4.6), attempted-suicide OR 6.1 (1.2–32.2). This is not universal: a 2-year cohort of 2,038 Finnish ninth-graders found the arrow reversed by sex — boys' social phobia predicted later depression (OR 3.6), girls' depression predicted later social phobia (OR 7.8). Nor does remitting SAD create a symptom-substitution problem: SAD carried the *lowest* odds of recurrence of any mood/anxiety/substance disorder after remission (adjusted OR 0.2, 95% CI 0.1–0.6).

**Does comorbid depression predict worse psychotherapy outcome, and does it change what to treat first?** Comorbid depression predicted the *average level* of social anxiety across individual CBT for SAD but not the *amount of change*, in a 93-patient cohort; a three-arm CBT/ACT/waitlist trial (n = 87) found comorbid depression associated with poorer outcomes across all active treatments. Treating depression first is not a solution: a transdiagnostic internet-CBT trial for 290 people with depression found depression symptoms fell by at least 45% and comorbid GAD by at least 43%, but comorbid social anxiety by only 29% — SAD needs its own work even after depression responds. **Sequencing rule:** treat concurrently unless depression is severe enough to block the exposure homework SAD treatment depends on, or carries acute suicide risk; where personality pathology and a long treatment history stack on depression, expect more sessions to remission, not a different treatment (a manualized psychodynamic protocol in routine practice reached only 9% remission versus 16% treatment-as-usual in this population, despite large within-group change over 12 months).

### Alcohol use disorder and the self-medication pathway

Cross-sectionally the association is real and specific to *dependence*, not abuse: SAD carried OR 2.8 for alcohol dependence but only 1.2 for abuse in a national survey, and preceded alcohol-dependence onset in 79.7% of comorbid cases. A 14-year prospective study found baseline SAD predicted 4.5-times the odds of subsequent alcohol dependence (not abuse), surviving control for other anxiety disorders — sex-specific in at least one cohort, significant in women but not men across 3 years.

**But the strongest single prospective test of this pathway is a genuine null, and a manual that presents self-medication as settled misrepresents the evidence.** An individual-participant meta-analysis pooling two large US national samples found *neither* subclinical social fears *nor* a full 12-month SAD diagnosis predicted incident alcohol use disorder at follow-up. A second, independent contradiction: a Finnish adolescent cohort found social-phobia symptoms did *not* elevate substance-use incidence at all (general anxiety did), and comorbid social phobia *decreased* the persistence of frequent drinking. Part of the discrepancy resolves along the trait/disorder line: trait shyness and social anxiety associate with *lower* adolescent substance use in a 50-article review, while diagnosable SAD associates with *higher* tobacco and cannabis use — self-medication may be a disorder-level, not trait-level, phenomenon.

**What happens to drinking when social anxiety is treated — the trials disagree, and integration versus addition explains it mechanically.** A negative trial added a social-anxiety CBT module onto standard alcoholism CBT (concurrent n = 49 vs alcoholism-only n = 44, 12 weeks): the concurrently-treated group did *worse* on three of four drinking measures, with no social-anxiety benefit. A positive trial built a genuinely *integrated* protocol instead — CBT plus motivational enhancement across 10 sessions (integrated n = 61 vs alcohol-focused control n = 56): both groups reduced drinking by roughly five drinks/day with no between-group difference, but integration achieved significantly greater social-anxiety improvement (mean difference −14.9, 95% CI −28.1 to −1.6, d = 0.60) and quality-of-life gain (d = 0.80) at 6 months. *Adding* a poorly-integrated module dilutes alcohol treatment; *integrating* gains social-anxiety ground without losing alcohol ground. Comorbid SUD more broadly does not sabotage anxiety-disorder CBT (prevalence rose with anxiety-disorder count, 22–29% with one or two to 46% with three, without moderating outcome). Do not wait for sobriety to assess: roughly a third of alcohol-dependent inpatients scored in the generalized-social-phobia range at admission, and only half remitted after 5 weeks abstinence. **Sequencing rule:** concurrent but not merged — integrate rather than bolt on, keep an explicit alcohol-focused component, reassess social anxiety after 4–5 weeks of abstinence, not during acute withdrawal.

### Other substance use

Cannabis follows the alcohol signature: in a national sample split into a cannabis-use-disorder-without-SAD group (n = 2,957), a SAD-without-cannabis-use-disorder group (n = 1,643) and a comorbid group (n = 340), SAD was related more strongly to cannabis *dependence* than abuse, SAD onset preceded cannabis use disorder in most comorbid cases, and the combination carried more impairment than either alone. No dedicated randomized trial of integrated SAD–cannabis treatment appears in this evidence base — treat it as a coverage gap, extend the concurrent-not-merged logic used for alcohol by extrapolation only, and do not assume it carries the same trial-level support.

### ADHD

Comorbid childhood ADHD tracks a rougher clinical picture: in a 123-patient SAD sample, those with childhood ADHD carried more emotional trauma, more impulsivity, and more severe depression, anxiety and social anxiety than those without. The mechanism looks like accumulated social failure rather than amplified fear: in generalized SAD with versus without adult ADHD, the ADHD group carried significantly more avoidant personality features, a childhood-ADHD symptom scale predicted AvPD criterion count in regression (β = 0.305, p = .007), and *current* social-anxiety severity did not correlate with AvPD criterion count at all. **Discriminator:** ADHD-driven social difficulty is impulsivity- and inattention-driven, pervasive across low- and high-stakes situations alike; SAD's avoidance is anticipatory, evaluation-contingent and situation-selective. Screen the *childhood* history specifically (a retrospective ADHD scale, not current-inattention ratings) when social anxiety is severe, trauma history is present, or impulse-control problems complicate the picture. **No trial evidence in this harvest tests whether comorbid ADHD moderates SAD-psychotherapy outcome** — a genuine coverage gap, not a reassurance.

## 5. What works, how well, and how we know

Everything that follows in this manual rests on one evidence base, and a clinician who has not seen its architecture cannot properly weigh anything built on top of it. This section sets out that architecture: what the largest synthesis actually compared, what happens when the comparator is tightened from an inert waitlist to a matched placebo, where later and competing analyses land differently and why, and what the guidelines conclude once all of this is weighed. Every effect size below is a standardised mean difference (SMD) or Hedges' g on validated social-anxiety symptom scales; by convention in this literature, a negative number favours the active treatment.

### 5.1 The anchor network: scale, method, and reference condition

The single largest synthesis in this field is a systematic review and network meta-analysis commissioned to build the evidence base for the UK national guideline. It searched 21 databases from 1988 to September 2013 with no language restriction, screened 168 potentially eligible reports down to 101 included trials, and analysed **13,164 of 14,229 randomised participants** (some trials did not report outcomes on everyone randomised). Those 101 trials contributed **41 distinct interventions or control conditions, collapsed into 17 treatment classes**, with per-study sample sizes ranging from 18 to 839 and a median treatment duration of 12 weeks (range 2–28). The analysis was a Bayesian random-effects network meta-analysis, pooling each study's effect as a mean Hedges' g across all validated social-anxiety scales it reported. The reference condition against which every class was first anchored was **waitlist** — a methodological choice this section returns to at length in §5.12, because it turns out to matter more than almost anything else in this literature.

The trial population is worth holding in mind throughout: median-of-means baseline severity on the Liebowitz Social Anxiety Scale (LSAS) was 78 across the 65 studies that reported it, median-of-means age 36, 80% white, 52% women. In 44 trials participants were drug-free; in the other 27, a median-of-means 27% were already taking medication at randomisation — a reminder that "treatment-naïve" is not the modal trial participant even in foundational studies.

### 5.2 Class effects against waitlist

Measured against waitlist, both drug and psychotherapy classes produced large effects, with individual cognitive therapy at the very top of the entire network:

| Class | SMD vs waitlist | 95% credible interval |
|---|---|---|
| **Individual CBT** | **−1.19** | −1.56 to −0.81 |
| MAOIs | −1.01 | −1.56 to −0.45 |
| Benzodiazepines | −0.96 | −1.56 to −0.36 |
| Group CBT | −0.92 | −1.33 to −0.51 |
| SSRIs + SNRIs | −0.91 | −1.23 to −0.60 |
| Exposure + social skills training | −0.86 | −1.42 to −0.29 |
| Self-help, with therapist support | −0.86 | −1.36 to −0.36 |
| Anticonvulsants | −0.81 | −1.36 to −0.28 |
| Self-help, unsupported | −0.75 | −1.25 to −0.26 |
| Mirtazapine (NaSSA) | −0.80 | −1.64 to 0.01 (crosses zero; n=30) |
| Psychodynamic psychotherapy | −0.62 | −0.93 to −0.31 |
| Promotion of exercise; "other" (supportive, mindfulness, IPT) | not superior to waitlist | — |

Within the individual-CBT class, the single named protocol driving the top-line number is the **Clark and Wells model**, which on its own scored **SMD −1.56 (95% CrI −1.85 to −1.27)** against waitlist — the largest effect of any of the 41 nodes in the network, and the figure this manual's whole approach is anchored to. A generic, non-manualised individual CBT (−1.19), Heimberg's group protocol (−1.02), and internet- or book-supported self-help (−0.85 to −0.88) all trail it. On the pharmacological side, **phenelzine** scored −1.28 (−1.57 to −0.98), comparable to Clark and Wells CT, but off only 125 people across five trials — the authors themselves flag this as likely inflated by small-study effects. Its MAOI classmate moclobemide scored a much more modest −0.74 (−1.03 to −0.44); the two drugs share a class but not an effect size. Combined psychotherapy-plus-medication scored the single largest class effect of all, −1.30 (−1.73 to −0.88), but that number is built from **five different combinations, one trial each, 156 participants total** — too thin a base to license any general claim about combination, a topic this manual takes up separately.

### 5.3 The decisive test: effects against matched placebo

Waitlist tells you a treatment beats doing nothing. It does not tell you the treatment beats the expectation of help, therapist attention, and the passage of time — which is what a matched placebo is built to control for. When the anchor network re-ran the comparison against the *appropriate* placebo instead of waitlist, most of the field's apparent effects collapsed toward zero, and only two classes survived:

- **SSRIs + SNRIs vs pill placebo: −0.44 (95% CrI −0.67 to −0.22)**
- **Individual CBT vs psychological placebo: −0.56 (95% CrI −1.00 to −0.11)**
- MAOIs vs pill placebo: −0.53 (−1.06 to −0.01) — barely excludes zero
- Individual CBT vs IPT/mindfulness/supportive therapy pooled: −0.82 (−1.41 to −0.24)

**These are the only two classes in the entire 17-class network that beat their own matched placebo.** Everything else that looked impressive against waitlist — group CBT, exposure with social skills training, self-help, benzodiazepines, anticonvulsants, psychodynamic therapy — has no comparison against its own matched inert control in this dataset, either because no such trial exists or because the class did not clear it.

The arithmetic this licenses is blunt and needs to be said plainly: individual CBT's effect against waitlist is −1.19, and against psychological placebo it is −0.56 — a fall of roughly 53%. SSRIs go from −0.91 against waitlist to −0.44 against pill placebo — a fall of roughly 52%. **Both treatments lose about half their apparent effect once the comparator stops being an inert waitlist and starts being something that controls for expectation and attention.** This is not a criticism unique to psychotherapy or unique to medication — it is symmetrical, and it is the central empirical fact this whole evidence chapter turns on. It does not mean individual CBT or antidepressants "don't work" — a genuine, specific effect of roughly half a standard deviation against a credible placebo is a real and clinically useful effect. It means the other half of what a waitlist-referenced trial reports is common to any credible-seeming intervention, and should not be marketed as unique to the treatment being tested.

### 5.4 How much to trust it: sparsity, heterogeneity, risk of bias, and published critique

The network's own architecture limits what it can say with confidence, and a clinician using its numbers should know the limits going in. **Of 820 mathematically possible pairwise comparisons among the 41 nodes, only 84 were ever tested directly** — 76 trials compared an intervention against a control, 58 compared two active interventions against each other, and of those 58, **only four trials directly compared any psychological intervention against any pharmacological one.** Nearly the entire psychotherapy-versus-medication ranking in this network is therefore an *indirect*, model-derived comparison, not an observed one, borrowed transitively across shared waitlist and placebo arms. Treat any precise-looking psychotherapy-vs-drug ranking from this network as a modelling inference, not a trial finding.

Between-trial heterogeneity was moderate: posterior median SD 0.19 (95% CrI 0.14–0.25). The class-level model fit the data slightly better than a model treating every individual intervention as distinct (posterior mean residual deviance 165.3 vs 176.3 against 148 treatment groups; DIC 364.8 vs 371.0), and only 9 of 44 closed evidence loops showed potential inconsistency between direct and indirect evidence, with no substantial divergence detected overall. Risk of bias was mixed: sequence generation was rated adequate in 74 of 101 trials and allocation concealment in 69; only 37 trials were judged low risk for selective outcome reporting; and — excluding masking, which is not achievable in psychotherapy trials by design — only **28 of the 101 trials were rated low risk of bias on every other Cochrane domain**. The full report could not be located for 8 studies, 20 reported no usable outcome data, and 2 reported implausible results. One further, more mundane data-quality flag: the published paper itself contains an internal inconsistency in one of its headline numbers — the individual-CBT-versus-psychodynamic-therapy credible interval is printed as −1.03 to −0.11 in the abstract and −1.00 to −0.11 in the results text. Neither materially changes the conclusion, but it is the kind of thing worth checking before quoting a network estimate to two decimal places.

The network has also drawn a formal published critique arguing it carries a **heightened risk of false positives from unadjusted multiple comparisons across a 41-node network** — testing enough pairwise contrasts inflates the chance that some "significant" differences are noise rather than signal. The original authors published a reply. Neither correspondence overturns the headline findings, but both are worth knowing exist before treating any single narrow-CrI comparison in this network as settled.

### 5.5 Later and competing syntheses — where they disagree, and why

Three later analyses revisit this territory with different scopes, and where they disagree with the anchor network, the disagreement is traceable to a specific methodological choice rather than to a genuine change in what works.

A 2025 Bayesian network meta-analysis restricted to psychotherapies alone, covering 92 studies (90 RCTs) to June 2024 — roughly eleven more years of trials than the anchor network — reproduces the same rank order at the top but with different magnitudes. **The Clark and Wells protocol scored SMD 1.42 (95% CrI 1.14–1.70)** in this later network, smaller than the anchor network's −1.56, consistent with what usually happens as a network accumulates more, later, generally larger trials: early small-study effects attenuate. **Internet-delivered CBT following the Andersson and Carlbring protocol scored 1.15 (0.87–1.42)**, and — the more striking disagreement — **psychodynamic therapy scored 0.98 (0.61–1.36)**, the best-performing non-CBT approach and nearly 60% larger than the anchor network's 0.62. The anchor network's own placebo analysis had shown individual CBT beating psychological placebo but had nothing directly comparable for psychodynamic therapy; its 0.62 versus-waitlist estimate sat only marginally above the 0.63 psychological-placebo effect, an implicit suggestion that psychodynamic therapy might be barely distinguishable from non-specific support. The later, larger psychotherapy-only network does not reproduce that implication. This is a genuine, currently unresolved disagreement between the two best available syntheses, not a case where one is simply wrong; a clinician should read any single confident number for psychodynamic therapy's effect size with that tension in mind.

A 2026 frequentist network meta-analysis of non-pharmacological interventions, 104 RCTs and 10,708 participants with a formal GRADE appraisal, reports **CBT at SMD −0.80 (95% CI −0.92 to −0.68, SUCRA 86.4%)** — visibly smaller than the anchor network's individual-CBT figure of −1.19. Three mechanical reasons explain essentially all of the gap. First, this network pools individual, group, and internet-delivered CBT into a single node, so the strongest-performing format (individual, Clark and Wells) is diluted by weaker-performing formats sharing its label. Second, its comparator is pooled "control conditions" rather than waitlist specifically — folding in placebo and active-control arms alongside waitlist arms pulls the reference condition's own effect upward and the treatment-vs-reference gap downward, the same mechanism worked through in §5.3 and §5.12. Third, its inclusion criteria admit "clinically relevant social anxiety symptoms" as well as formally diagnosed social anxiety disorder, broadening and likely diluting the sample. None of this makes either number wrong; they are answering different questions — "how large is the Clark and Wells protocol's effect" versus "how large is the average effect of CBT-labelled therapy across formats and comparators" — and a clinician should ask which question a given cited number is actually answering before using it to argue for or against a specific protocol.

Two smaller, more specialised networks complete this picture without changing its shape. A 2026 Bayesian network of digital interventions across 42 RCTs found internet-delivered cognitive therapy the strongest performer for combined social anxiety and depression outcomes, with robust effects for various internet-CBT formats and the largest effect for virtual reality on quality of life specifically — evidence that digital delivery, done well, is not a lesser substitute but a format with its own comparative strengths. And a network meta-analysis restricted to children and adolescents, 30 RCTs and 1,547 participants, found internet CBT ranked highest for social anxiety symptoms (SUCRA 71.2%), ahead of group CBT (68.4%) and individual CBT (66.0%) — an **inversion of the adult finding** that individual delivery outperforms group. This manual is written for adult psychotherapy, but the inversion is a useful caution: "individual beats group" is a finding about format interacting with a specific, adult-derived protocol, not a context-free law of psychotherapy delivery.

### 5.6 Individual versus group: protocol held constant, then pooled

Whether individual delivery genuinely outperforms group delivery depends entirely on whether the comparison holds the *protocol* constant. A trial that randomised participants directly to individual or group delivery of the identical Clark and Wells cognitive therapy protocol (n=71 randomised, 65 completing post-treatment, 59 completing 6-month follow-up) found **individual CT superior to group CT on several social-phobia measures at both post-treatment and 6-month follow-up**; the authors' own interpretation is that group delivery *diminishes* the effectiveness of this specific protocol, not merely that it is less studied. This is consistent with the anchor network's own class estimates (individual CBT −1.19 vs group CBT −0.92), although those two credible intervals overlap heavily and should not be read as a formal significance test.

Pooled meta-analyses reach the opposite conclusion, and the reconciliation is mechanical rather than contradictory. A meta-analysis of group psychotherapy specifically for social anxiety disorder (36 RCTs, 2,171 patients, predominantly cognitive-behavioural group therapy) found group treatment beat waitlist on SAD-specific symptoms at g=0.84 (95% CI 0.72–0.97) and on general psychopathology at g=0.62 (0.36–0.89), superior to common-factor control conditions on SAD symptoms but not on general psychopathology — and **found no difference against individual psychotherapy and no difference against pharmacotherapy.** A broader meta-analysis of group psychotherapy across anxiety disorders generally (57 studies, 76 comparisons, 3,656 participants spanning GAD, SAD, and panic) found the same pattern: g=0.92 (0.81–1.03) against no-treatment control, but only g=0.24 (−0.09 to 0.57, not significant) against individual psychotherapy and g=−0.05 (−0.33 to 0.23, not significant) against pharmacotherapy — and flagged **researcher allegiance as a significant moderator of head-to-head findings**, a warning sign about whose hands built which comparison. The reconciliation: these pooled analyses average across *heterogeneous* group protocols compared against *heterogeneous* individual protocols, so protocol-level differences wash out in both directions. The single trial that held the protocol fixed and varied only the format found a real individual advantage for that specific protocol. Format-versus-brand is therefore not a single fact but a question that only makes sense asked *within* a named protocol — a theme this manual returns to throughout. One further data point on format: a trial of intensive, massed-format cognitive-behavioural group therapy (two weeks of daily sessions with a week of homework between, n=26 against a 6-month waitlist) produced effect sizes that grew over time rather than fading — 0.56 at post-treatment, 0.68 at 3 months, 0.81 at 6 months and 1 year — evidence that compressed scheduling is at minimum not obviously inferior to weekly delivery, though this trial has no active treatment comparator arm.

### 5.7 Cognitive therapy versus exposure

Across anxiety disorders broadly, a meta-analysis directly comparing cognitive therapy against exposure therapy (20 RCTs, n=1,308) found **no difference for PTSD, OCD, or panic disorder — but for social phobia specifically, a statistically significant superiority of cognitive therapy over exposure**, both short-term (Z=3.72, p=0.0002) and long-term (Z=3.28, p=0.001). Social anxiety disorder is therefore something of an outlier within the anxiety family on this specific question; whatever mechanism separates cognitive restructuring from behavioural exposure appears to matter more here than it does elsewhere. A landmark single trial makes the same point with response data a clinician can hold onto directly: cognitive therapy (the Clark and Wells protocol) was compared against exposure plus applied relaxation and against waitlist (n=62). Both active arms beat waitlist on every measure, but **cognitive therapy also beat exposure-plus-applied-relaxation on social-phobia-specific measures**, and the gap showed up starkly in diagnostic outcome: **84% of the cognitive-therapy group no longer met diagnostic criteria at end of treatment, versus 42% of the exposure-plus-relaxation group and 0% of the waitlist group.** The advantage persisted at one-year follow-up, and patients who had received exposure-plus-relaxation were more likely to have sought additional treatment afterward — a durability signal in the therapy's favour, not merely an acute-phase one.

### 5.8 Head-to-head against medication

Three primary trials put individual or group cognitive-behavioural treatment directly against a specific drug, without a combination arm complicating the read (combination trials are covered separately in this manual). In the first and largest, group CBT was compared against phenelzine, pill placebo, and an educational-supportive group therapy control (n=133, two sites, 12-week acute outcome). Both active treatments beat both controls, but **phenelzine responded faster — evident by six weeks — and was superior to group CBT on some measures at the 12-week endpoint**, with no meaningful site-by-treatment (allegiance) interaction. Following the same cohort into a maintenance and then treatment-free follow-up phase, phenelzine responders entered maintenance more improved than CBT responders and kept that advantage through the maintenance period, relapse during maintenance did not differ between arms, but **phenelzine showed a trend toward greater relapse once treatment was withdrawn entirely, especially in generalised social phobia** — the authors' interpretation is that group CBT may confer a more durable response once treatment stops, even where the acute-phase drug advantage is real.

The second trial compared cognitive therapy against fluoxetine plus self-exposure and against placebo plus self-exposure (n=60, 16 weeks, then a three-month booster phase, with 12-month follow-up). **Cognitive therapy was superior to both the fluoxetine and the placebo arms at mid-treatment and post-treatment, and fluoxetine did not outperform placebo at all** — meaning the SSRI added nothing measurable to self-directed exposure in this trial. Cognitive therapy's advantage over the fluoxetine arm was maintained through the end of the booster phase and at 12-month follow-up.

The third relevant head-to-head is technically psychotherapy against psychotherapy rather than psychotherapy against medication, but belongs here because it is the best-powered comparison of CBT against a genuine competing modality: the SOPHO-NET trial randomised 495 outpatients to CBT (n=209), psychodynamic therapy (n=207), or waitlist (n=79), with blind-rated outcome. **Remission was 36% for CBT, 26% for psychodynamic therapy, and 9% for waitlist; response was 60%, 52%, and 15% respectively.** CBT was statistically superior to psychodynamic therapy on remission but not on response, and the between-group effect sizes for both outcomes were small. Followed to 24 months, the picture changed materially: **response reached roughly 70% and remission roughly 40% in both arms, with no significant between-group difference after the six-month mark** — the strongest single piece of evidence in this literature that a CBT-over-psychodynamic advantage, where it exists, is an acute-phase effect that closes with time rather than a durable structural superiority. A 30-month societal-perspective economic evaluation run alongside the same trial found the probability that CBT is more cost-effective than psychodynamic therapy rises with willingness to pay: **65% at €0 additional willingness-to-pay per anxiety-free day, 83% at €10, and 96% at €30** — CBT is favoured, but the probability is not overwhelming at the lowest cost threshold, consistent with two treatments whose long-term outcomes converge.

One further comparison belongs in this section because it is CBT against a bona fide alternative psychotherapy rather than against medication: cognitive therapy against interpersonal psychotherapy and waitlist (n=117 randomised, 16 individual sessions plus one booster). **Response by blinded rating was 65.8% for cognitive therapy, 42.1% for interpersonal psychotherapy, and 7.3% for waitlist; at one-year follow-up, 68.4% versus 31.6%** for the two active arms, cognitive therapy remaining ahead on the LSAS throughout, with no site interaction.

### 5.9 Response, remission, and the reminder this is a hard disorder

Two cross-disorder meta-analyses of CBT for anxiety establish the benchmarks a clinician should hold this disorder against. Pooling 100 articles, overall mean remission across anxiety disorders treated with CBT was **51.0%**, with remission defined most favourably when measured as good end-state functioning or loss of the primary diagnosis rather than symptom-score cutoffs — and critically, **social anxiety disorder and OCD had the lowest remission rates of any anxiety disorder in the pooled dataset**, with PTSD the highest. A companion analysis of 87 studies found overall response rates of **49.5% at post-treatment and 53.6% at follow-up**, but showed response rates move substantially with how "response" is defined: multi-criterion definitions, combinations of reliable-change-index and clinical-cutoff, and intention-to-treat analyses all produced lower rates, while **blinded independent assessors produced systematically higher response rates than unblinded ones** — a methodological detail that should make a clinician read any single response percentage with an eye to how it was measured, not just what number it reports.

Set against those cross-disorder benchmarks, the SAD-specific rates from the trials in §5.6–§5.8 read as broadly consistent, not exceptional: 60% response / 36% remission for CBT at the SOPHO-NET post-treatment endpoint, rising to roughly 70% response / roughly 40% remission by 24 months for both CBT and psychodynamic therapy; 65.8% response for cognitive therapy against interpersonal psychotherapy's 42.1%; 84% no longer meeting diagnostic criteria for cognitive therapy against 42% for exposure plus relaxation. None of these clear even 70% remission at any acute-phase endpoint, and most sit well below it. The clinical implication is not that these treatments fail — a majority of patients respond, and a substantial minority remit — but that a clinician should set expectations with the patient accordingly: social anxiety disorder does not resolve as reliably or as quickly, even with the best-supported protocol delivered well, as some other anxiety presentations do.

### 5.10 Dropout and acceptability

There is no meta-analysis of psychotherapy dropout specific to social anxiety disorder — a genuine gap in this evidence base — so the best available figures come from adjacent anxiety-disorder and delivery-format analyses. Across 140 internet-delivered anxiety treatment trials (199 treatment arms, N=11,021), only **46% (95% CI 42–50%) of people who expressed interest were ultimately eligible and included**; among those who started, **98% began treatment, 81% completed assigned treatment modules, and 21% dropped out by post-treatment** as measured by questionnaire non-completion, with clinician guidance and the specific anxiety disorder both acting as significant moderators. A meta-analysis of virtual-reality exposure therapy (46 studies, N=1,057) found **16% overall attrition, with no significant difference in dropout odds against standard in-vivo exposure**, and between-session homework completion as a significant moderator favouring retention. In generalised anxiety disorder specifically (45 studies, 2,224 participants), **weighted mean dropout was 16.99% (95% CI 14.42–19.91%)**, unmoderated by age, sex, severity, comorbidity, treatment type, session count, or therapist experience — evidence that roughly one in six is a reasonably stable baseline dropout rate across delivery formats rather than a marker of a specific protocol's tolerability. One trial-level predictor study in social anxiety disorder itself (n=74, comparing virtual-reality and group exposure) found that **stereotype-confirmation concerns predicted dropout while race, age, gender, and baseline severity did not, and group treatment was associated with higher dropout than individual delivery** — a small but directly relevant convergence with the individual-versus-group findings in §5.6.

### 5.11 The GRADE picture

The most methodologically rigorous appraisal in this literature is a Cochrane systematic review of pharmacotherapy, covering 66 RCTs and 11,597 participants (63 contributing to meta-analysis), which applied formal GRADE ratings outcome by outcome. **SSRI treatment response was rated VERY LOW quality of evidence** despite a precise-looking pooled risk ratio of 1.65 (95% CI 1.48–1.85, 24 trials, N=4,984); MAOIs, RIMAs, and benzodiazepines were each rated **LOW quality**; the GABA-analogue anticonvulsant class was the one outlier among symptom-severity outcomes at **MODERATE quality** (RR 1.60, 1.16–2.20, 3 trials, N=532); and SSRIs for relapse prevention were the only other outcome in the whole review rated MODERATE — and the only class with any evidence on that endpoint at all. On tolerability, SSRI treatment withdrawal carried RR 2.59 (1.97–3.39, LOW quality) and venlafaxine withdrawal RR 3.23 (2.15–4.86, MODERATE quality), though absolute withdrawal rates were low in both arms. The review's blunt summary judgement: **most of the symptom-severity evidence across every drug class was of very low quality.** An earlier version of the same Cochrane review (36 RCTs, 4,268 participants) had already flagged **funnel-plot evidence of publication bias** on the pharmacotherapy side, alongside a pooled LSAS weighted mean difference of −15.56 (95% CI −17.95 to −13.16) and a relapse-prevention risk ratio of 0.33 (0.22–0.49) — publication bias that a purely later, larger sample size does not by itself correct.

No comparably rigorous GRADE appraisal exists for psychotherapy in social anxiety disorder specifically. The nearest available is the 2026 frequentist network meta-analysis of non-pharmacological interventions discussed in §5.5, which rated certainty of evidence **predominantly low to moderate** across its pooled comparisons. The asymmetry is itself worth naming: pharmacotherapy for this disorder has been GRADE-appraised in detail and found to rest substantially on very-low-to-low-quality evidence; psychotherapy has not been appraised with comparable rigour at all. Neither fact should be read as "so the other side is better" — both point toward the same conclusion, that confident-sounding point estimates in this field sit on thinner methodological ground than their precision suggests.

### 5.12 The waitlist-inflation problem

This is the single fact most necessary to correctly read every other number in this chapter. Within the anchor network itself, pill placebo beat waitlist by SMD −0.47 (95% CrI −0.71 to −0.23) and psychological placebo beat waitlist by SMD −0.63 (−0.90 to −0.36) — meaning **a trial that uses waitlist rather than a credible placebo control inflates its apparent treatment effect by roughly half a standard deviation to two-thirds of a standard deviation before the active treatment has done anything specific at all.** This is precisely the arithmetic behind §5.3: individual CBT's effect falls from −1.19 against waitlist to −0.56 against psychological placebo, and SSRIs fall from −0.91 to −0.44, as the comparator tightens.

The same phenomenon has been independently measured, at larger scale, in a pre-post meta-analysis spanning panic disorder, generalised anxiety disorder, and social phobia together (234 RCTs, 37,333 patients): **pre-post effect sizes of the control conditions themselves were d=0.20 for waitlist, d=0.83 for psychological placebo, and d=1.29 for pill placebo** — a roughly six-fold difference in how much a control arm appears to "improve" depending purely on which control was chosen, with nothing about the active treatment changed. And a meta-analytic comparison in a related disorder — major depression, not SAD, so the magnitude does not transport directly but the mechanism plainly does — found waitlist-controlled psychotherapy trials produced a pooled effect of g=0.95 (95% CI 0.85–1.04) versus g=0.63 (0.55–0.71) for trials using a care-as-usual comparator (p<0.001, robust to meta-regression adjustment for every measured difference between the two sets of trials). Critically, the mechanism identified was not that the treatment arm did *better* under waitlist conditions — it was that **the waitlist control arm improved less**: within-group pre-post change was g=0.37 in waitlist arms versus g=0.64 in care-as-usual arms. Waitlist does not merely fail to help; people randomised to it appear to do measurably worse than people simply left in ordinary care, which mechanically inflates whatever is compared against it. **The practical rule for reading any individual trial in this literature: check what the control condition was before trusting the effect size, and discount any waitlist-controlled effect size by roughly half a standard deviation before comparing it to a trial that used a placebo or active control.**

### 5.13 What the guidelines recommend, and why

The UK national guideline's evidence base is the anchor network meta-analysis itself, together with a linked decision-analytic economic model built on the same network (28 interventions modelled, UK NHS and personal social services perspective, quality-adjusted life years as the outcome, efficacy inputs drawn directly from the network). The guideline's stated position, as summarised in the network's own discussion, is that **individual CBT is the treatment of choice**; for patients who decline it, an SSRI is offered if the patient prefers medication, or CBT-based supported self-help if they prefer a psychological alternative; psychodynamic psychotherapy sits third-line; other drug classes are reserved for non-response to initial treatment. The stated rationale rests on exactly the specificity findings worked through in §5.3 — individual CBT is one of only two classes that beat its own matched placebo, and it does so alongside a lower side-effect burden and, per the acute-phase trial evidence, better-maintained gains than medication once treatment stops (a durability claim, it is worth noting, that the network's own authors state was *not* derived from within their own dataset, since they explicitly excluded long-term follow-up data for lack of adequately controlled results — a limitation that should temper how strongly that particular argument is deployed). The economic model's own finding was that **individual cognitive therapy was the most cost-effective option, followed by generic individual CBT, then phenelzine, then unsupported book-based self-help**, with other drugs and group-delivered psychological interventions ranking below all four — a result driven substantially by the model's assumption that psychological treatment retains its benefit for longer, and one that did not even incorporate medication side effects, which the authors note would have favoured individual CBT still further.

Three other major guideline processes converge on largely the same first-line recommendation via independent routes. A 2022 international consensus guideline, built from 33 experts across 22 countries reviewing 1,007 RCTs across the whole anxiety-disorder family — and, new in that revision, grading psychotherapies by the same formal methodology previously applied only to drugs — recommends **SSRIs and SNRIs as first-line medication and CBT as the first-line psychotherapy** for social anxiety disorder specifically. A Canadian consensus guideline covering anxiety, PTSD, and OCD, built from a structured MEDLINE and PsycINFO search from 1980–2012 and a modified periodic-health-examination rating scheme, gives social anxiety disorder its own dedicated section and — a structural point worth flagging for prescribers — explicitly weights **side-effect profile**, not efficacy alone, into its recommendation grade, which is part of why medication does not automatically outrank psychotherapy in its algorithm despite broadly comparable acute effect sizes. And the German S3 guideline, built by 36 experts from 20 professional societies reviewing 403 RCTs in its original form and revised by 35 experts from 29 societies with 92 further RCTs, recommends **CBT as carrying the highest level of evidence among psychological treatments, with psychodynamic therapy reserved for patients for whom CBT is ineffective or unavailable, or who prefer it after being informed of the better-supported alternative** — an explicitly hierarchical framing — alongside SSRIs and SNRIs as first-line medications, continued for six to twelve months after remission. None of these guidelines resolves the acute-versus-durable tension the trial evidence itself raises (§5.8); all four converge on individual CBT, and specifically the Clark and Wells protocol, as the recommended starting point, while treating that recommendation as provisional on the patient's own preference, access, and tolerance for side effects rather than as a closed question.

One further piece of scaffolding belongs here because everything that follows in this manual about switching, augmenting, or escalating treatment depends on it: a 2024 Delphi consensus process (36 multidisciplinary experts, three survey rounds, a ≥75% agreement threshold) produced 14 formal recommendations defining what counts as treatment resistance in anxiety disorders, covering both pharmacological and psychotherapeutic non-response and setting out a staging model. First-line failure is not a vague judgement call in a well-run algorithm; it has an operational definition, and later sections of this manual that discuss what to do when individual CBT alone is not enough build directly on it.

## 6. Individual cognitive therapy: the core protocol

### 6.1 The shape of a course

CT-SAD runs over **up to 14 weekly sessions across 3–4 months**, plus **up to three booster sessions in the first three months of follow-up**. Sessions are **up to 90 minutes**, not the standard hour, specifically to leave room for in-session behavioural experiments — including ones run outside the consulting room — and to debrief them properly. In the definitive randomised trial, patients attended a mean of **12.8 sessions (SD 2.1)** — 18.4 therapist hours — plus **2.3 boosters (SD 0.9)** — 2.8 hours — roughly 21 hours total, completing a mean of **13.5 in-session behavioural experiments (SD 6.1)**. Hold that dose against the reality of routine services: the average patient in England's NHS Talking Therapies system receives only **7.5 sessions in total**, the documented reason uptake of the full 14×90-minute format is often poor.

Every session opens with a fixed measure set. The **LSAS**, **SPIN**, and **SCQ** are completed every session, and the SCQ's frequency-and-belief ratings on specific negative cognitions set that session's focus — sessions target whichever thoughts carry the highest belief rating that week. The **SPWSS**, also weekly, tracks avoidance, self-focused attention, anticipatory anxiety, and post-event rumination. The **SBQ** and **SAQ** are given only at start, middle, and end, identifying safety behaviours to target and tracking excessive standards and conditional/unconditional beliefs. Patients audio-record every session to review between visits, and sessions are **routinely video-recorded from the start** — so being filmed becomes unremarkable rather than an anxiety trigger in its own right.

Eleven components divide into those used with essentially every patient — formulation, the self-focused-attention/safety-behaviours experiment, video and photograph feedback, attention training, behavioural experiments progressing to decatastrophising, and the blueprint — and those used as indicated: surveys, virtual-audience practice, anticipatory-processing/rumination work, memory work, and techniques for entrenched unconditional beliefs. The canonical sequence: **Session 1** builds the individualised model, no belief change attempted; **Session 2** the self-focused-attention/safety-behaviours experiment; **Session 3** video feedback on both recordings; **Session 4** formally introduces attention training, after which behavioural experiments dominate sessions and homework; memory work later if needed; the blueprint closes the course.

### 6.2 Building the idiosyncratic formulation

Session 1's only job is to construct, live and collaboratively, the patient's own version of the Clark and Wells maintenance cycle — situation → negative automatic thoughts → self-focused attention and a negative self-image → safety behaviours and somatic symptoms → confirmation of the original thoughts — drawn on a whiteboard using the patient's own recent bad experience, their own words for the self-image and safety behaviours. No belief change is attempted; the aim is shared understanding of the mechanism. This is not inert: in the trial that isolated this week, LSAS did not change significantly across the roughly two weeks to assessment, and the formulation session itself functioned as an attention-and-contact control (p = .7) — therapy's active ingredients start in Session 2, not here.

### 6.3 The self-focused-attention and safety-behaviours experiment

Session 2 is the procedure that launches treatment, and its power is entirely in the contrast it manufactures. The patient has **two brief social interactions**, typically one-to-one conversations with a stranger (two short presentations, for public-speaking fears), chosen to activate the core fear without being overwhelming. In the first, attention turns **on the self** — monitoring performance — with habitual safety behaviours **used** (rehearsing what to say, avoiding eye contact). In the second, attention goes **externally** — onto the other person, the content, the room — getting absorbed rather than evaluating, with safety behaviours deliberately **dropped**. Immediately after each, the patient rates anxiety, self-focus, safety-behaviour use, and perceived performance on 0–100% scales into a two-column comparison table; ratings must show a genuine separation between conditions. The conversational partner gives written feedback on each conversation without being told the patient's specific fears, so it reflects an ordinary interlocutor — banked for the video-feedback session that follows. The intended discovery: **less anxious and came across better** in the externally-focused, safety-behaviour-free condition. From Session 2 onward, the same format tests the patient's deepest unconditional beliefs ("I am fundamentally boring"), entered as explicit falsifiable predictions rather than deferred.

This is not untested theatre. The original single-case series — 8 socially phobic patients, counterbalanced within-subject, one session of exposure alone versus one session of exposure plus deliberately reduced safety behaviours — found the added safety-behaviour reduction produced significantly greater falls in both within-situation anxiety and belief in the feared catastrophe; this is the empirical root of the entire behavioural-experiments-are-not-exposure distinction used throughout this protocol. A dedicated demonstration study isolated both signature single-session procedures (this experiment and video feedback) and found each achieved its aim **and** reduced symptoms on its own, though per-arm n and effect sizes are not in the retrieved record. A separate study found high-socially-anxious patients used more safety behaviours than low-anxious controls, more often, in more situations — and that **both** groups rated their own safety behaviours as helpful; a second experiment manipulated safety-behaviour use and self-focus directly and showed both unhelpful regardless of belief. Factor analysis of the safety-behaviours questionnaire replicated **two subtypes** — avoidance and impression management — both elevated versus PTSD and controls; a dyadic follow-up found **both** subtypes raised the performer's own anxiety, but only the **avoidance** subtype measurably degraded the partner's experience — a useful priority when choosing which behaviour to drop first. The procedure back-translates to adolescents: 35 with SAD in an internet-delivered trial showed lower anxiety and more positive self-appraisals in the external-focus condition on every comparison (all p < .0025), with video-feedback gains larger specifically for the internal-focus condition — where the model predicts the distortion should be greatest.

### 6.4 Video feedback — and why skipping the preparation makes it fail

Session 3 is built around watching both Session-2 recordings, and it is the procedure most vulnerable to being run badly. Done well, in 47 patients from a major trial, **98% rated themselves less negatively than predicted** after viewing, and self-rated LSAS fell from **73.4 to 65.5 in the following week (p = .003)** — against no change across the roughly two weeks to assessment (p = 1.00) or in the week after the purely psychoeducational formulation session (p = .7); the overall comparison across these intervals was itself significant (F(2.84, 124.82) = 15.37, p < .001). Video feedback, not formulation or the passage of time, is where measurable change begins.

That result depends on **cognitive preparation**: two published studies that ran video feedback without adequate preparation found **no benefit**, both explicitly having spent little time preparing patients or discussing the viewing experience afterward. The canonical three steps: **(1) predict in detail** what will be seen and heard; **(2) form a mental image** of the feared self-presentation; **(3) watch as though watching a stranger** — judging only what is visible, not re-experiencing the anxiety. In the original trial, prepared patients rated their performance more favourably than an unprepared group and than their own pre-video ratings.

Preparation defeats five processing biases: **feelings-as-evidence** (re-experiencing anxiety, mistaking it for what is on screen — countered by "watch as a stranger"); **selective search for negative behaviour** (a global "I come across badly" belief hunting any flaw — countered by sharp, falsifiable predictions in advance); **discounting the video's accuracy** ("the camera didn't catch it" — countered by calibrating the recording so a feared sign would be visible); **reading a safety behaviour's trace as an intrinsic deficit** (countered by asking whether the patient was deliberately holding back); and **reactivated self-criticism** (countered by a compassionate third-person stance, or covering their own image first for appearance-focused patients). Setup: record **both** people, never zoomed tightly on the patient alone; a colour chart in shot unexplained for blushing fears; patients demonstrate the feared sign against the footage; a still frame of disconfirmation becomes a flashcard.

The dismantling literature on preparation is dense. In 40 high-socially-anxious participants, preparation enhanced the benefit **and generalised to a second, unprepared speech thirty minutes later**.
