In 95 speech-anxious undergraduates, preparation produced **strong** effects on self-perception, **weak** effects on anxiety, and **no** effect on avoidance behaviour. The first controlled test in **diagnosed, treatment-seeking** SAD found the benefit concentrated in patients with the largest self–observer discrepancy — moderated, not universal. Applied to **audio** feedback (n = 40), the same sequence reduced implicit social anxiety; a later trial (n = 41) found **step 3 alone** — listening as though to a stranger — sufficient, suggesting the perspective shift may be the truly active ingredient. A four-arm trial of 140 students found only the condition combining preparation, video feedback, **real audience feedback, and a structured post-viewing review** significantly reduced state anxiety and raised positive self-perception — the justification for the confederate's written feedback in CT-SAD; audio review supplements video wherever the feared attribute is vocal, since patients specifically underestimate their own auditory performance. Comparing internet and face-to-face delivery: **92%** and **96%** respectively thought they looked less anxious than predicted; self-perception change was larger face-to-face, but symptom impact a week later did not differ by format.

### 6.5 Attention training and the shift to external focus

Formally introduced in Session 4: graded exercises shifting attention from internal monitoring to **external auditory and visual** stimuli — sounds, colours, reflections, then music aloud, culminating in the **therapist reading aloud to the patient**, the closest in-room analogue to real interaction. Standardised videos support homework; the therapist angles their chair away and closes their eyes when the patient does, to reduce self-consciousness.

The rationale is causal: in 52 high- and 52 low-socially-anxious participants, experimentally raising self-focused evaluative attention **increased** estimates of the percentage of people "looking at you" in a face-matrix task, in **both** groups, with no effect on a non-social control task. But attention training's status as a *discrete* ingredient is genuinely mixed. In 65 patients with fear of blushing/trembling/sweating, task concentration training outperformed applied relaxation (each followed by cognitive therapy) on bodily-symptom fear at its own within-phase assessment — but that advantage **disappeared once cognitive therapy was added**, only to **re-emerge at one-year follow-up** (d ≈ 3, and superior on self-focus at every assessment point). An intensified weekend-group version in 82 patients found the two equally effective, both superior to waitlist, with 69–73% remission at six months. Adding the formal Attention Training Technique to group CBT in 81 patients produced **no potentiation on any outcome**, and a full factorial dismantling trial (Section 6.15) found attention training **not superior to its absence** at post-treatment. The honest reading: mechanistically justified, clearest benefit combined with cognitive therapy or at longer follow-up, weak evidence it adds anything alone.

### 6.6 Behavioural experiments — and why they are not exposure

From Session 4, behavioural experiments carry most session time and homework, and the distinction here is conceptually load-bearing: **exposure** requires only remaining in a feared situation until anxiety falls — it says nothing about what the patient attends to or does. A **behavioural experiment** tests a pre-specified, idiosyncratic, belief-level prediction with a stated belief rating and an observable, falsifiable criterion, run with safety behaviours dropped and attention external. Two patients can sit through an identical feared situation for identical duration; only the one running a behavioural experiment is doing something the model predicts will change belief. The single-case series above is the direct demonstration: exposure plus dropped safety behaviours beat exposure alone, in the same patients.

Setup runs in sequence: identify the target belief from the model and recent SCQ ratings; loosen it first (evidence for/against, or a survey); state the prediction with a belief rating and observable criterion ("if I drop my safety behaviours and speak spontaneously on webcam they will think I am boring, 60%, and I'll know because they'll look away or leave the call early"); run it with safety behaviours dropped and attention external; explicitly **update the negative self-image**, not just the belief rating; generalise the learning. Every experiment is recorded on a record sheet, completed by the patient wherever possible, with video/photo/other-person feedback folded in.

Experiments escalate: early ones simply drop safety behaviours and shift attention externally; only once the patient has repeated success with those do experiments move to **decatastrophising** — deliberately producing the feared sign itself (something deliberately unremarkable, a long pause, blusher on the cheeks, water under the arms to simulate sweat, a trembling hand holding a full glass, dropping keys in public) with attention held on others' actual reactions, not predicted ones. Held back this way, decatastrophising reads as an escalation of a method that has already proven itself, not a stand-alone ordeal — the most direct possible test of the catastrophic prediction itself. This is CT-SAD's version of shame-attacking, but belief-targeted and evidence-gathering, built around a full appraisal-by-experiment grid (stared at / boring / stupid / nothing to say / blushing / sweating / shaking), each with an in-session and a homework version.

The only randomised dismantling trial **within** CT-SAD itself compared cognitive therapy incorporating behavioural experiments against cognitive therapy relying more on verbal, discussion-based interventions, in 60 patients (30 per arm), clinician-administered LSAS primary, assessed at baseline, post-treatment, and six-month follow-up. At post-treatment there was **no significant between-group difference on any measure**, with a trend favouring behavioural experiments (p = .072) and between-group effect sizes of **d = 0.55 (LSAS) and d = 0.41 (SPIN)** — sizeable but under-powered. Response probability was **97.7% versus 74.9%**; within-participant effect sizes reached **d = 2.59** versus **d = 1.02**. The one clearly significant finding emerged only at **six-month follow-up**: a large advantage for behavioural experiments on emotion-regulation skills (p < .001, d = 1.19) — whatever this component adds may take months to surface, and surfaces first in emotion regulation, not symptoms. Separately, in 100 adults randomised to relationally-augmented CBT, graduated exposure plus applied relaxation, or waitlist across 14 group sessions, the relational arm beat exposure-plus-relaxation on relationship satisfaction and the symptom composite, and **change in safety-behaviour use partially mediated** that superiority on both.

### 6.7 Systematically dropping safety behaviours

Tracked on the SBQ at start, middle, and end, identifying which behaviours remain to target. The subtype finding above is directly actionable: **avoidance-type** behaviours (leaving early, no eye contact, staying silent) measurably degrade the partner's own experience and should generally be prioritised for early removal; **impression-management** behaviours (over-rehearsing, over-grooming) cost the patient but are largely invisible to the other person — both are worth dropping, but only avoidance is actively sabotaging the interaction itself.

### 6.8 Surveys

Used **as required**, subordinate to behavioural experiments, which must remain the core of every course. Surveys loosen a belief before it is tested or normalise a feared physiological sign. The patient poses a question to a defined group; the **therapist reads every response before sharing it**, to filter for genuine understanding of the question; results are shared by email or screen-share. Curated images of well-known people visibly sweating or blushing normalise feared physical signs in discussion. A genuine gap: repeated targeted searches returned **no randomised evidence that surveys, as a discrete technique, add anything** — an under-studied piece of an otherwise well-evidenced protocol.

### 6.9 Anticipatory processing and post-event rumination

Tracked every session on the SPWSS; a dedicated intervention triggers only when these processes are **not** improving with standard technique. The intervention: an advantages/disadvantages analysis of the worrying or dwelling itself; a shift from worrying to **testing** ("how can I find out if that's actually true?"); and, for social-media-driven rumination, a flashcard or screensaver cueing a deliberate shift into an absorbing unrelated task. A qualitative study of 21 patients found post-event processing experienced simultaneously as "the only, safe and useful way to improve myself" and as something that "hurts more than it helps" — occasional makeshift insights found *during* rumination act as intermittent reinforcement sustaining the positive belief, so the advantages/disadvantages discussion must engage that belief directly, not just list costs. Direct trial evidence is thin: a brief single pre-speech intervention (24 vs 23 patients) affected some cognitive processes but **left rumination levels unchanged**; adding the Attention Training Technique to group CBT (Section 6.5) did **not** improve anticipatory or post-event processing. This is the softest-evidenced standard technique in the protocol.

### 6.10 Imagery rescripting of early memories

Rationale rests on two findings. Interviews with 22 SAD patients found **all** reported recurrent negative spontaneous self-images, stable across time and situations, mostly multisensory, mostly traceable to memories of adverse social events clustered around disorder onset — the negative self-image is typically an old memory being replayed, not a free-floating distortion. A counterbalanced experiment then established **causality**: patients held either their habitual negative self-image or a less-negative control image during a conversation with a stranger. Under the negative image patients were more anxious, rated themselves worse — and **a blind assessor also rated their anxiety as more evident and behaviour as less positive**, with patients under the negative image significantly underestimating their own performance and overestimating symptom visibility.

Two stages, the second reserved for need. **Discrimination training** first: spotting differences between the traumatic memory and the present ("THEN I was 10, bullied by classmates; NOW I am 34, in a work meeting, not being bullied"), written into a THEN/NOW table carried as a flashcard — usually sufficient alone. **Imagery rescripting**, for memories that keep intruding, follows four steps: identify the key memory linked to the current self-image; rate the negative meaning drawn from it ("I am dull and will be ridiculed again — 80%"); explore alternative evidence via Socratic questioning from an adult perspective; bring the new understanding back into the memory via rescripting — picturing it and narrating in the present tense across three phases, so the adult evidence is woven into the remembered scene itself.

The evidence is small but consistent. An own-control design in 11 patients ran a control session (memory discussed, not modified) one week before an experimental session (restructuring then rescripting): **no change after control**, significant improvement after the experimental session across beliefs, distress, vividness, and situational anxiety. A companion study in 14 patients found significant within-session change plus persistence at one week. A randomised trial in 14 patients tested rescripting **without** the preceding restructuring step against a neutral reading task and still found significant improvement at one week — questioning whether restructuring is strictly necessary, though the authors flag that n = 14 limits the conclusion.

### 6.11 Updating the self-image, unconditional beliefs, and self-criticism

Self-image updating is not a scheduled session but a standing requirement inside every experiment debrief and video-feedback session — "how does this fit with the way you saw yourself?", rated 0–100% for the patient's own idiosyncratic images (a silly smirk, looking detached, coming across like a small child), which no standard questionnaire captures.

Persistent unconditional beliefs ("I am unlikeable / boring / weird / foolish / inferior / inadequate") carry a specific warning: treating them as a separate "low self-esteem" problem, an end-of-course add-on, produces **therapist drift** away from the core techniques that actually carry the effect, and such digressions are described as having limited value at that cost. The recommended approach is to formulate unconditional beliefs **as part of the standard model** and target them **from Session 2 onward** — entered as predictions in the self-focused-attention/safety-behaviours experiment, folded into video-feedback debriefs, tested in behavioural experiments — with **decatastrophising experiments carrying particular weight**, since they test the belief in its most extreme form ("even when I show the flaw, I am still acceptable"). If beliefs persist: a positive data log, memory work on the linked early trauma, and — for habitual self-criticism — spotting and labelling the internal self-critic and responding as one would to a friend.

### 6.12 The blueprint and relapse prevention

Built collaboratively toward the end of treatment: the key learning from the course, in the patient's own words; concrete steps to build on it; and a specific plan for recognising and managing a future setback, rather than treating any relapse as proof the treatment failed. Typically emailed to the patient to complete as homework after the final substantive session, then reviewed together, with an explicit instruction to save it somewhere retrievable months or years later.

### 6.13 How homework is set and reviewed

Homework is specific, not generic "practice exposure," and is reviewed against the same weekly measures that set the session agenda. From Session 4, behavioural experiments are set for homework in most sessions, each with its own written prediction and record sheet, so the next session's review checks a falsifiable claim rather than "how did it go." Weekly LSAS/SPIN/SCQ/SPWSS completion is itself homework, since it drives the next session's focus. Attention-training practice uses the standardised video library, and patients listen back to their own recorded sessions. Structured presentations to virtual audiences — nine pre-built options varying in size and difficulty, some with deliberately distracted-looking members — are set for public-speaking fears (maximise to full screen, keep the patient's own video feed out of view, drop safety behaviours, hold attention on the audience); this is one of the few dose-response findings in the literature — **frequency of virtual-audience practice significantly predicted better outcome** in the internet-delivered protocol (standardised β = −0.420, p = .003). Flashcards from video-feedback still frames, THEN/NOW tables, and positive data logs round out the set, each reviewed the following session against the belief rating it targets.

### 6.14 The trials

#### 6.14.1 Against medication

The founding trial randomised 60 patients with generalised social phobia three ways — cognitive therapy, fluoxetine plus self-exposure, pill placebo plus self-exposure — over 16 weeks, after which the medication blind was broken and the cognitive-therapy and fluoxetine arms entered a three-month booster phase, followed to 12 months. Cognitive therapy was superior to **both** other arms on social phobia measures at mid- and post-treatment, while fluoxetine-plus-self-exposure **did not outperform placebo-plus-self-exposure** at all; cognitive therapy's superiority over fluoxetine held at end of booster and at 12 months. Per-arm n, effect sizes with CIs, and remission rates are not in the retrieved record — but the directional finding is unambiguous and replicated in every later comparison below.

A later four-arm trial in 102 patients (54% also meeting criteria for avoidant personality disorder) randomised paroxetine, cognitive therapy, cognitive therapy plus paroxetine, and placebo across a 26-week medication phase, followed to 12 months. Cognitive therapy alone beat paroxetine and placebo at end of treatment, but **was not superior to the combination** at that point. The picture changes at follow-up, and this is where this manual's caution about combining medication with good psychotherapy comes from: at 12 months cognitive therapy **maintained** its benefit over both placebo and paroxetine alone, while there were **no significant differences among the combination, paroxetine, and placebo arms** — the combination had fallen back toward medication or placebo level. Twelve-month recovery: **68% cognitive therapy, 40% combination, 24% paroxetine, 4% placebo**. Adding medication to good cognitive therapy produced **less** durable benefit than cognitive therapy alone — genuinely counter-intuitive, and not smoothed over here.

The reverse question — does adding CBT help once antidepressants have already failed — gets a cleaner answer. In 21 patients receiving CBT plus usual psychiatric care versus 21 on usual care alone over 16 weeks, adjusted mean LSAS reduction was **−40.87 points (CBT) versus +0.68 (usual care)**, a between-group difference of **−41.55 points (95% CI −53.68 to −29.42, p < .0001)**; response **85.7% versus 10.0%**; remission **47.6% versus 0.0%** (both p < .001). The two findings are not in tension: one asks whether medication adds to cognitive therapy (it does not, durably), the other whether cognitive therapy adds to medication that has already failed (it clearly does).

#### 6.14.2 Against exposure plus applied relaxation

In 62 patients randomised to cognitive therapy, exposure plus applied relaxation, or waitlist, both active arms beat waitlist on every measure, but on social phobia measures **cognitive therapy produced significantly greater improvement than exposure plus applied relaxation**. Diagnostic remission: **84% cognitive therapy, 42% exposure plus applied relaxation, 0% waitlist**, no longer meeting criteria at end of treatment/wait. These differences **persisted at one-year follow-up**, and exposure-plus-relaxation patients were significantly more likely to have sought additional treatment in that year — the gap was not simply absorbed by further help-seeking. Therapist effects were small and non-significant, arguing against a few unusually skilled therapists explaining the result. Per-arm effect sizes with CIs and dropout are not in the retrieved abstract.

#### 6.14.3 Against interpersonal psychotherapy

Of 254 patients screened, 117 with primary SAD were randomised to 16 individual sessions of cognitive therapy or interpersonal psychotherapy, each plus one booster, assessed 20 weeks after randomisation (106 completed treatment or wait). Primary outcome was treatment response on the Clinical Global Impression–Improvement scale, rated by **independent evaluators masked to allocation**. Response: **65.8% cognitive therapy, 42.1% interpersonal therapy, 7.3% waitlist**, cognitive therapy significantly ahead on both response and independently-rated LSAS, both active arms ahead of waitlist. At **one-year follow-up** the gap widened: **68.4% versus 31.6% response**, correspondingly better LSAS for cognitive therapy. Per-arm n and effect sizes with CIs were not in the retrieved abstract.

A residential adaptation of both treatments — 10 weeks, integrated group-and-individual format, 80 chronic, highly comorbid patients who had mostly failed other treatments — found **no significant between-treatment difference** on primary outcomes; residential cognitive therapy beat residential interpersonal therapy only on social role security, and its own authors noted it performed less well than individual cognitive therapy in other, non-residential trials — consistent with adapting the protocol away from individual delivery costing some of its effect. Weekly process analyses from the same trial found self-focus, estimated cost, and safety-behaviour use each predicted the following week's change in anxiety **bidirectionally**, with early alliance predicting outcome only **indirectly, through the cognitive-process variables**.

In the interpersonal-therapy trial, sudden, large, stable symptom drops occurred in **22.4%** of 67 patients tracked weekly, similar in magnitude and timing across both treatments but with **lower post-treatment symptoms specifically in the cognitive-therapy arm**; notably, **the gains preceded the cognitive change**, not the reverse — a complication for any simple "cognition changes first" account.

#### 6.14.4 Individual versus group delivery

In 71 patients randomised to individual cognitive therapy, a group version of the identical treatment, or waitlist (65 completed post-treatment, 59 at six-month follow-up), both formats improved significantly, but **individual therapy was superior to group therapy on several measures at both post-treatment and six-month follow-up**; effects on general mood/psychopathology were smaller than on social-phobia measures in both formats — evidence, in the trial's own reading, that this is a specific treatment whose efficacy is genuinely reduced, not merely diluted, by group delivery.

A three-arm trial in 100 patients compared **intensive group** treatment (16 sessions over 3 weeks), standard **individual** therapy (16 weekly sessions over 4 months), and **treatment as usual** (an indicated SSRI plus sessions as needed, over a year). Individual therapy beat **both** other arms, which did not differ from each other — the compressed three-week group format performed no better than a full year of medication-based usual care. At five-year follow-up, 48 of the original 67 patients (72%) were re-contacted; **75% (36/48) had sought no further treatment**, and patients from **either** treatment showed further improvement — though the two arms were pooled, so it cannot say whether individual therapy's advantage persisted.

In adolescents the pattern repeats: 57 of 279 assessed, aged 13–16, randomised to individual cognitive therapy, group CBT, or an attentional placebo, found individual therapy significantly ahead of **both** other arms at post-treatment and 12 months, while group CBT **did not differ from the attentional placebo** — the sharpest single data point for format mattering more than the CBT label.

#### 6.14.5 Effectiveness and dissemination in routine care

A cluster-randomised trial across 48 private practitioners compared practitioners trained in the manualised protocol against practitioners delivering CBT-as-usual with no such training, in 162 SAD patients in routine private practice (107 completers: 57 manualised, 50 as-usual), assessed at treatment-hours 8, 15, and 25, at end of treatment, and at 6 and 12 months. Within-group effect sizes on independently-rated LSAS were large and durable in **both** arms — **d = 1.91 manualised, d = 1.80 as-usual** — with **no significant between-group difference**; the trial's own authors call this unexpected. This qualifies "protocol identity matters more than brand": it matters enormously against a genuinely diluted format (group therapy, another active treatment, medication) but adds little against generically competent CBT already delivered by experienced practitioners, once baseline therapist skill exists. The effective dose here, roughly 25 sessions over 30 weeks, was considerably longer than the 14×90-minute research format.

Dissemination into new settings looks more positive when delivery hews to the manual. Three therapists trained in the internet-delivered protocol treated 6 Hong Kong patients to completion with no dropouts; mean LSAS fell 53.8 points (SD 39.5), within-group **d = 2.06 (95% CI 0.66–3.46)**, sustained at three-month follow-up. A translated, culturally adapted internet version, single-arm in 15 Japanese patients already on unimproved usual care, added 14 weeks plus a three-month follow-up with up to three boosters: within-group **d = 3.66 (p < .001)**, maintained at follow-up, **80% (12/15) reliable improvement, 60% (9/15) remission**, dropout **7%**, no serious adverse events. Audit data pooling two routine-care samples (271 patients) found the change processes look **similar between trials and routine practice** — reassuring outside a university trial unit (Section 6.15).

Training itself has been tested experimentally: 94 fifth-year clinical psychology students undertook a 16-hour CT-SAD course, randomised to behavioural rehearsal (demonstration plus role-play) against demonstration plus discussion alone, rated blind on post-training videotapes. Rehearsal produced higher competence on all three rated variables, **d = 1.23–1.40 (95% CI 0.78–1.85)**, with no effect on self-report outcomes at post-training or six-month follow-up. Sudden-gain rates across format in 99 patients: **64% (face-to-face) and 51% (internet)**, each preceded by reductions in negative social cognitions and self-focus, not depression — "therapy content appears to play a more important role than the medium of delivery," the same lesson the individual-versus-group data teach.

### 6.15 What actually carries the effect

The mediation evidence produces a genuine, live disagreement, reported here rather than resolved by fiat. In the main internet-versus-face-to-face trial, a composite of negative social cognitions, safety-behaviour use, attitudes, and self-focus mediated the effect for **both** formats, accounting for roughly **60% of the improvement**. A weekly multilevel mediation analysis of 29 patients receiving individual therapy found **shifts from self-focused to externally focused attention mediated improvement one week later**, while change in negative automatic thoughts **did not** predict next-week anxiety — a direct instruction to prioritise attention over disputing specific thoughts. The 271-patient routine-care audit found self-focused attention, negative social cognitions, and depressed mood all significant mediators, negative social cognitions strongest. Pooling two earlier CBT studies (86 patients), change in both cost- and probability-appraisal biases mediated outcome, but against the strength of the alliance only mid-treatment change in probability bias independently predicted outcome.

Against this, a full factorial mediation analysis of an internet self-help programme informed by, but not identical to, the Clark and Wells protocol found the **opposite** pattern: knowledge gain mediated a psychoeducation component, reduced safety-behaviour use mediated an exposure component, avoidance did **not** mediate exposure — and **self-focused attention was, in the authors' own words, consistently identified as an inert mechanism of change**. A real contradiction, not laundered into one confident answer here. It plausibly reflects structure, not error: mediator instruments differ, and "exposure" in that self-help package is self-directed practice, not the CT-SAD behavioural experiment (prediction-testing with safety behaviours dropped, attention held external by design); the package has no video feedback, surveys, or memory work, and its own authors are explicit it dismantles their self-help programme, not CT-SAD. A clinician delivering the full therapist-guided protocol should weight the therapist-delivered mediation evidence more heavily, while recognising every one of these analyses establishes temporal precedence within a treatment arm, not experimental manipulation of the mediator — evidence consistent with causation, not proof of it.

The one true experimental test of a discrete component is the behavioural-experiments trial in Section 6.6: under-powered, null at post-treatment, large and significant on emotion regulation at six months. A large factorial trial found attention training **not superior to its absence** at post-treatment, consistent with its mixed record elsewhere. The competence literature adds a more decisive kind of evidence: rated therapist competence on a validated scale (inter-rater reliability 0.81, internal consistency 0.89, test-retest 0.86) significantly **predicted** outcome on both CGI-Improvement and LSAS, while neither patient difficulty nor manual adherence improved that prediction. Doing the right things skilfully predicts outcome; following the checklist does not — echoed by behavioural rehearsal producing a large jump in exactly this rated competence, a trainable target. Finally, patients who completed a full course, asked what changed them, converged on the same two mechanisms the model itself nominates: **reducing internal focus of attention, and reducing safety behaviours and avoidance**.

## 7. Group CBT, exposure, and social skills training

This section covers the treatment most patients actually receive: group-delivered cognitive-behavioural therapy, and the exposure procedure inside it that also stands as its own treatment. It then covers social skills training, applied relaxation, intensive formats, and format — group versus individual — as a question of capacity and cost, not which treatment a clinician should reach for first when both are available.

### 7.1 Heimberg's cognitive-behavioural group therapy: the delivered package

The most extensively trialed manualized group protocol for this disorder is Heimberg's cognitive-behavioural group therapy, CBGT — ancestor of what the network meta-analysis anchoring this manual labels the Hope, Heimberg and Turk model. Dose is consistent across the pivotal trials: **12 weekly sessions over 12 weeks**. CBGT is conventionally staffed by two co-therapists running a group of roughly six patients; that ratio, and the session-by-session template, come from Heimberg's published manual, not the trial reports themselves, which specify dose and outcome but not staffing.

Four components recur across every description: **psychoeducation** about the cognitive-behavioural model; **cognitive restructuring** — identifying automatic thoughts and generating rational responses; **in-session simulated exposure** — role-played social encounters staged inside the group, using other members and the co-therapists as interaction partners; and **homework** — structured in-vivo assignments between sessions. A formal CBGT dismantling trial defines the exposure-only comparator as "an exposure-based treatment without formal cognitive restructuring" — fixing restructuring as the one separable ingredient, the basis for the dismantling evidence in 7.6.3.

**The worksheet logic.** The CBGT literature does not reproduce a verbatim worksheet, but the identical three-stage loop is documented step by step in an open-access exposure case description, and the sequence — goal set *before* the simulation, not during or after — is the central operational claim about how to run one.

- **Before.** The therapist elicits a specific, behaviourally observable prediction, written on a standard worksheet: "vague hypotheses are exceedingly difficult to support or refute." Worked example: *if he expressed an opinion to a coworker, he would be regarded as incompetent, as evidenced by quizzical stares, raised eyebrows, and avoidance.* The behavioural goal is a concrete act — express an opinion, ask a question — never an anxiety-level target like "SUDS below 40."
- **During.** The patient performs the behaviour and records the actual response in the same objective language used for the prediction.
- **After.** The therapist runs a Socratic comparison: *"Is the evidence consistent with what you predicted?"* and *"Did you learn anything about your coworkers' responses to you?"* As therapy progresses the patient runs this set of questions on himself.

**Homework is a measured ingredient, and its relation to outcome is not linear.** Greater compliance in the **first** and **final** blocks of the 12-week course predicted lower post-treatment social interactional anxiety — the expected direction — but compliance in the **middle** sessions was positively related to post-treatment fears of scrutiny and criticism, an inversion the original authors called "surprising" and could not explain via locus-of-control measures. A modern replication (105 patients, twelve sessions of imagery-enhanced or standard group CBT) found homework engagement predicted lower anxiety **only mid-treatment** — the opposite of the original study's mid-treatment finding — and found that **group cohesion**, not therapeutic alliance, was what tracked end-of-treatment symptoms.

**The week-by-week arc:** early sessions build rationale and worksheet mechanics on lower hierarchy items; middle sessions carry the bulk of escalating in-session simulations; late sessions push the hardest items, shift weight toward in-vivo homework, and close with relapse-prevention planning as scaffolding is withdrawn.

### 7.2 Exposure as a treatment in its own right

#### 7.2.1 Hierarchy, modality, and dose

The literature delivers exposure through several modalities: **simulated, role-played exposure inside a group** (CBGT's engine); **in-vivo exposure**, as homework and as a standalone treatment; **flooding** — intense, non-graduated exposure without a stepwise hierarchy, tested over **3 months** with 6-month follow-up in 72 patients against a beta-blocker and placebo, where it was consistently superior to placebo and beat the beta-blocker on behavioural measures; and **imaginal plus in-vivo exposure combined**, the comparator arm in the adult SST trial in 7.3.

Dose parameters vary by protocol: **2-hour sessions, 12 weekly** in a contemporary community-clinic trial; **14 weekly sessions** in the comprehensive group CBT arm reviewed in 7.6.2; and for intensive formats, **daily sessions over 2 weeks separated by a week of homework**, or **16 sessions over 3 weeks** versus the same 16 spread over 4 months (7.5). No retrieved trial specifies a minimum minutes-per-trial rule for this disorder — and the inhibitory-learning literature explicitly rejects duration as the right way to decide when a trial ends.

#### 7.2.2 Two philosophies of exposure delivery

Two competing accounts govern what should happen during a trial, giving the therapist different instructions for the same fifteen minutes. Both belong in the manual, because the comparative evidence here does not settle the question as cleanly as the newer framing's popularity suggests.

**Habituation / SUDS.** Stay in the situation until distress declines — conventionally until Subjective Units of Distress roughly halve. A trial ends when fear has come down; the hierarchy is ascended in strict order, easiest first; the goal is fear reduction itself; cognitive restructuring lowers anticipatory anxiety before or during exposure; the patient is coached mid-exposure toward coping and toward the situation's positive elements. Fear that fails to decline smoothly within a session is read as a sign it isn't working.

**Inhibitory learning.** The founding claim is direct: across animal and human laboratory work, "neither the degree by which fear reduces nor the ending fear level predict[s] therapeutic outcome." Exposure does not erase the original fear association; it lays a **second, inhibitory** association on top, which is why fear returns — spontaneous recovery, renewal on context change, reinstatement after an unsignalled aversive event, rapid reacquisition after one re-pairing. Anxious patients show deficits specifically in this inhibitory learning. The rules invert the habituation model point for point: a trial ends when conditions have most effectively **violated the patient's expectancy**, not when fear has declined; trial number is set by how many occasions it takes to weaken the **expectancy**, not a fixed count; hierarchy order follows what the patient most needs to learn, not ascending difficulty — in the anchoring social-phobia case, predictions were recorded but **not used to order exercises**, and treatment began with the single most feared item (attending social events with his wife) ahead of lower-fear ones (a grocery clerk); the goal is explicitly **not** fear reduction but a "personal scientist" hypothesis test; cognitive work is **confined to after the exposure** — deploying it beforehand, the named bad example being *"Does it really make sense to be afraid of next weekend's party? What's happened at the past few parties you've attended?"*, shrinks the prediction–outcome mismatch the learning depends on; mid-exposure attention to coping or to positive elements is forbidden as "such a pernicious safety behavior"; and in-session variability, far from a bad sign, is **desirable** — sustained rather than smoothly declining arousal is associated with less fear at retest.

The most recent update re-tiers these into a checklist — **fundamental** (expectancy violation, sustained attention to the feared stimulus, removing safety signals, mental rehearsal after exposure), **advanced** (deepened extinction, occasional reinforced extinction), **generalization** (retrieval cues, multiple contexts, variability, positive affect) — with the following SAD-specific detail behind each: **expectancy violation** requires a real-world predicted outcome, not an emotion — "I will get anxious" doesn't qualify, "I will be ignored or rejected" does — and the post-exposure question probes the **degree of surprise**. Not just theory: a single-session trial in 37 undergraduates with high public speaking anxiety pitted a component targeting perceived **probability** against one targeting perceived **cost** of a negative outcome; cost won, mediated by change in social cost estimates — the same construct that, in 7.6.3, explains why cognitive work extends CBGT's gains after treatment ends. **Deepened extinction** extinguishes two or more fear cues separately before combining them, both predicting the same outcome, without ever needing the patient to state an expectancy beforehand. **Removal of safety signals** — another person, a phone, food, and, specific to this disorder, fear reduction itself as a safety signal for a patient who fears being seen anxious — should be phased out gradually only to protect against attrition; where the patient is willing, **immediate** removal is preferred: no fidgeting, earphones, or a "just in case" magazine, and no **post-event processing** afterward, since replaying the interaction for flaws interferes with consolidating that the predicted outcome did not occur. **Variability** means selecting hierarchy items out of order and lengthening and randomizing the interval between sessions later in treatment; the acknowledged cost is higher arousal that visibly fails to habituate within-session, despite better long-term outcomes. **Occasional reinforced extinction** — letting the feared outcome occur now and again — carries the manual's most direct line: *"the addition of occasional social rejections and 'shame attacks' in exposures to social situations… We routinely conduct such reinforced exposure and even encourage clients to seek the opportunity for occasional negative outcomes."* Rationale: fear can be re-acquired after one pairing with genuine rejection, so treatment that never lets one happen leaves that vulnerability untested.

**Retrieval cues** — an instructional cue such as "mentally reinstate what you learned here" beats a physical object — should be used sparingly and **late**, for relapse prevention, since early use can blunt the expectancy violation itself. A trial of 65 patients with public-speaking anxiety found two multisensory cues produced **no contextual renewal** and **reduced spontaneous recovery** of fear on behavioural and physiological measures — but patients using a cue as a reminder they had coped benefited more, while those who found it merely **comforting**, converting it into a safety signal, benefited less. **Video feedback**, similarly, only works if cognitively prepared beforehand — predict what the recording will show, form a mental image of performing, then watch as though watching a stranger — which produced more favourable self-ratings than unprepared viewing.

**What the comparative evidence actually shows.** The theoretical case is strong and several individual strategies have real component-level support — variability, retrieval-cue timing, expectancy-targeting — but a direct head-to-head trial of an inhibitory-learning-optimized protocol against a traditional graduated, SUDS-governed one, in this disorder specifically, does not appear in this evidence base. A critical review of the framework catalogues real limitations in the evidence for several individual tenets even while endorsing the overall shift. The honest summary: this is the best-argued account of how to run a trial and should be the default framing taught here, but it is not a settled, head-to-head-proven replacement — that trial has not been done.

### 7.3 Social skills training: what it adds, and to whom

Social skills training (SST) targets three domains: **pragmatic** skills (managing a conversational topic), **paralinguistic** skills (motor movement, facial orientation, posture), and **speech/prosodic** skills (volume, inflection). In adults it is delivered as half of **Social Effectiveness Therapy (SET)**, paired with exposure; in the paediatric version, **SET-C**, combined with exposure to enhance skill and reduce anxiety together; in an integrated European protocol it joins rational emotive therapy and in-vivo exposure; and in Heimberg's 16-session adolescent CBGT adaptation it sits alongside problem-solving and assertiveness training.

**In adults, SST buys observable skill and clinician-rated global status, not extra remission.** A trial of 106 adults, randomized to exposure alone (imaginal plus in vivo), SET (SST plus exposure), or waitlist, with blinded clinician ratings and blinded coding of recorded behaviour, found loss of diagnosis at post-treatment of **67% for SET versus 54% for exposure alone — not significant**; SET was significantly superior on coded social skill and general clinical status, and both active arms improved on self-report and observed-behaviour measures. A trial of 34 patients found the mirror result: integrated RET-plus-SST-plus-exposure was, contrary to the authors' own expectation, **not superior to exposure in vivo alone**, with equal long-term effectiveness. A qualitative review of 30 RCTs judged CBT efficacious and specific and exposure efficacious, but stated flatly "there was little evidence to support the use of social skills training." The network meta-analysis pools exposure and SST into one class and finds a real but modest, imprecisely estimated effect against waitlist — SMD −0.86, 95% CrI −1.42 to −0.29 — not one of the interventions shown to beat psychological placebo.

**In children, SST earns a clearly differentiated place, including against medication.** In a trial of children aged 7–17 (registered enrollment 139) randomizing SET-C, fluoxetine, or pill placebo, both actives beat placebo on social distress, avoidance, and functioning, but **SET-C beat fluoxetine on each**, and SET-C was the **only** treatment superior to placebo on social skills, situation-specific anxiety, and social competence; fluoxetine peaked around week 8, SET-C kept improving through week 12. A mechanistic follow-up on the same sample showed why: SET-C children showed better pragmatic and paralinguistic behaviour than either comparator, fluoxetine showed **no more** skill than placebo, and only SET-C improved on all three domains pre- to post-treatment. A separate trial of 55 children aged 8–14, comparing individual exposure plus group SST with or without added parent education, found no difference between the active arms, both beating a 12-week waitlist, gains maintained at 1-year follow-up.

In adults chasing remission, SST is not load-bearing — exposure alone gets most patients there, and skills training chiefly buys observable social performance. In children, it is differentiated from both waitlist and an SSRI specifically on skill acquisition, and belongs as first-line, not optional, when the presentation includes a genuine skills deficit and not anxiety alone.

### 7.4 Applied relaxation as an active comparator

Applied relaxation functions in this literature as the exposure-based control arm precisely because it is genuinely active, not inert — which is what makes the trials that beat it informative. The most decisive randomized 62 patients to cognitive therapy on the Clark and Wells model, exposure plus applied relaxation, or waitlist: both actives beat waitlist, cognitive therapy beat exposure-plus-relaxation, and loss of diagnosis at post-treatment was **84% for cognitive therapy versus 42% for exposure-plus-relaxation versus 0% for waitlist** — persisting at 1 year, with the relaxation group more likely to have sought further treatment.

A second trial of 65 patients whose main complaint was fear of blushing, trembling, sweating, or freezing randomized task concentration training or applied relaxation, each followed by cognitive therapy. Task concentration beat relaxation at a within-test measurement taken before cognitive therapy was added; the gap closed once cognitive therapy was layered on both; but at **1-year follow-up** the task-concentration-plus-CT sequence again beat relaxation-plus-CT on fear of bodily symptoms, with effect sizes reaching roughly **d = 3**, and beat it throughout on self-focused attention. A modern self-help trial of 63 adults found self-guided self-compassion training beat waitlist (d = 0.12–0.33) but **not** self-guided applied relaxation (d = 0.01–0.05) — confirming relaxation is a real comparator, not a strawman. A meta-analysis of 108 trials across eleven conditions found benzodiazepines and SSRIs the most consistently effective, dropout similar across active conditions, and psychological gains moderate but **continuing to grow at follow-up** — durability the same review could not assess for medication given sparse drug-trial follow-up data.

### 7.5 Intensive and massed formats

Compressing the same dose into a shorter calendar window is a live option, and the evidence is more encouraging than intuition suggests. A trial of 26 patients randomized to intensive CBGT — **two weeks of daily sessions separated by one week of homework** — versus a 6-month waitlist found the treated group superior at 3 weeks, 3 months, and 6 months, with average effect size **growing** over time: 0.56 at post-treatment, 0.68 at 3 months, 0.81 at 6 months and 1 year.

A trial of 100 patients randomized three ways — intensive group cognitive treatment (16 sessions over 3 weeks), individual cognitive therapy (the same 16 sessions over 4 months), or treatment-as-usual (an indicated SSRI plus therapy as needed over a year) — found individual therapy superior to both other arms, but **no difference in overall effectiveness between the 3-week intensive group format and the year-long usual care**: the intensive format, in the authors' words, "lasts only 3 weeks, and is as effective as more protracted treatment as usual." At 5-year follow-up, 48 of the original 67 patients were reassessed; 75% had sought no further treatment, and both arms showed **further** improvement from post-treatment to 5 years. The lesson for a service under access pressure: a massed 2-to-3-week format is not an obvious efficacy compromise — matched against a full year of usual care in the one trial that tested it, and still gaining a year after the block ended in the trial against waitlist.

### 7.6 The pivotal trials, in full depth

#### 7.6.1 CBGT versus phenelzine: acute, maintenance, and treatment-free relapse

The defining comparative trial randomized 133 patients across two sites to CBGT, phenelzine, pill placebo, or educational-supportive group therapy over **12 weeks**. The CBGT control was not an inert waitlist but educational-supportive group therapy, an attention-placebo the authors describe as of **equal credibility** to CBGT — the strongest available design against the possibility that CBGT's advantage is really just an advantage over a control patients didn't believe in. Sites were chosen to test allegiance: one known for pharmacological treatment of anxiety, one for cognitive-behavioural treatment. At 12 weeks, **both phenelzine and CBGT beat both controls** on response and dimensional measures; phenelzine's response was evident earlier, by week 6, and phenelzine beat CBGT on some measures by week 12; but there were **few differences between sites** — both worked regardless of the delivering facility's orientation, evidence against the allegiance effect the design was built to detect. Per-arm response rates and effect sizes with confidence intervals sit in the paper's tables rather than its abstract and are not reproduced here.

Acute responders then entered a **6-month maintenance phase**, and those still responding entered a further **6-month treatment-free phase**; placebo and educational-supportive patients did not continue, so this long-term comparison is phenelzine versus CBGT in acute responders only — a selected sample. **Phenelzine patients entered maintenance more improved**, and non-relapsers held that advantage throughout maintenance; relapse **during** maintenance did not differ between treatments; but once treatment was withdrawn, **phenelzine showed a trend toward greater relapse**, particularly in the **generalized subtype**. The authors' own reading: the two treatments "may differ in their long-term effects," and CBGT "may lead to a greater likelihood of maintaining response after treatment has terminated." Exact relapse percentages per arm and phase are, again, in the tables and not asserted here.

#### 7.6.2 The fluoxetine / comprehensive group CBT trial

A five-arm, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial at two academic centres screened **722** patients and randomized **295** to intention-to-treat analysis — 156 (52.9%) male, 226 (76.3%) white, mean age 37.1, all generalized subtype. Arms: fluoxetine (10–60 mg/day equivalent) alone, pill placebo alone, group comprehensive CBT alone (**weekly, 14 sessions**), CBT plus fluoxetine, and CBT plus placebo, scored on the Brief Social Phobia Scale and Clinical Global Impressions by a blinded evaluator. CGI response at 14 weeks: **fluoxetine 29/57 (50.9%), CBT alone 31/60 (51.7%), CBT plus fluoxetine 32/59 (54.2%), CBT plus placebo 30/59 (50.8%), placebo 19/60 (31.7%)** — all four active arms beat placebo. Trajectory mattered: at **week 4**, fluoxetine alone and CBT alone each outpaced the combination arms, but **by the final visit none of the four active treatments differed from each other** — only from placebo. Combining did not help: "combined treatment did not yield any further advantage." On a secondary videotaped behavioural assessment, fluoxetine and CBT-plus-fluoxetine beat placebo **only at one of the two sites**. All treatments were well tolerated, but the authors' caution stands: **many patients remained symptomatic after 14 weeks of any active treatment.**

The companion recruitment analysis: the leading exclusion was **comorbid depression**, so this sample was depression-depleted relative to a real clinic population; the leading reason for refusal among eligible patients was **unwillingness to take medication**, with few objecting to group CBT or placebo — support for group CBT as the more broadly acceptable arm where both modalities are offered.

#### 7.6.3 Does cognitive restructuring add to exposure? The dismantling evidence

The question was first tested three decades ago in a trial of guided exposure with and without cognitive restructuring for severe social phobia, the historical anchor for this literature; it carries no PubMed abstract, so no numbers from it are asserted here. Three more recent, fully documented sources answer it directly, and they do not all point the same way at the same time horizon.

A direct CBGT dismantling — full protocol versus exposure-based group treatment with restructuring stripped out, versus waitlist — found both actives beat waitlist. At post-treatment, full CBGT showed an advantage on subjective anxiety during an individualized behavioural test, but it had **disappeared by 6-month follow-up**; the authors flagged that CBGT underperformed its own prior trials in this run, a caveat worth keeping attached to the null.

The cleanest test comes from a trial of 90 patients: CBGT, exposure-group therapy without cognitive intervention, or waitlist. Both actives beat waitlist and **did not differ at post-treatment** — replicating the null — but **only CBGT continued improving** from post-test to 6 months, associated with how much estimated **social cost** — perceived severity of a negative outcome, distinct from its perceived probability — had already dropped by post-treatment. Social cost mediated the initial gain equally in both arms; what differed was who kept improving afterward.

A meta-analysis of 20 RCTs comparing cognitive therapy against exposure across 1,308 patients found **no difference** in PTSD, OCD, or panic disorder — but social phobia was the exception in both directions: cognitive therapy significantly beat exposure short-term and long-term. One caveat: the pooled exposure arms were largely pure exposure, while the applied-relaxation trial in 7.4 bundles relaxation into its exposure arm — a node-definition mismatch that can make two honest analyses look like they disagree.

Reconciling this is the chapter's central argument. Adding restructuring to exposure does **not** reliably improve end-of-treatment outcome — the CBGT dismantling and the cleaner mediation trial both show it, matching the SST dismantling in 7.3. It does appear to improve **maintenance**: the mediation trial's continued gain, and the phenelzine trial's finding that CBGT held its ground better once treatment stopped, point the same way. And when "cognitive therapy" means the full Clark and Wells package rather than restructuring bolted onto exposure, it **does** beat exposure head to head — 84% versus 42% loss of diagnosis, 7.4 — and across the pooled literature above. The decisive comparison is manual against manual, not component against component: stripping restructuring out of CBGT costs little at the endpoint, but swapping it for the fuller Clark and Wells model changes the outcome substantially. Inhibitory learning (7.2.2) predicts this null and re-specifies the real question as **when** cognitive work belongs — after exposure, for consolidation — not **whether**.

### 7.7 Format: group versus individual as an access decision

Two lines of evidence point different directions, and the disagreement is mechanical, not genuine, once each comparison is made explicit. A trial holding the **same** protocol constant — Clark and Wells cognitive therapy, delivered individually or in a group — and randomizing only format found individual delivery superior on several measures at post-treatment and 6-month follow-up, in 71 patients randomized (65 post, 59 at 6 months). The authors' own conclusion: individual therapy's effectiveness "may be diminished by delivery in a group format."

Against this, a meta-analysis of 36 RCTs and 2,171 patients, restricted to CBGT because that is what nearly every trial used, found it superior to waitlist — **g = 0.84 (95% CI 0.72–0.97)** for specific symptoms, **g = 0.62 (0.36–0.89)** for general psychopathology — and superior to common-factor controls on specific symptoms, but **no significant difference against individual psychotherapy or pharmacotherapy on direct comparison**. A broader meta-analysis across the major anxiety disorders — 57 studies, 3,656 participants — reproduced the pattern: g = 0.92 (0.81–1.03) vs no treatment, g = 0.29 (0.10–0.48) vs common-factor controls, but no significant difference against individual therapy (g = 0.24, −0.09 to 0.57) or pharmacotherapy (g = −0.05, −0.33 to 0.23), with researcher allegiance a significant moderator.

The mechanics of the disagreement: the individual-versus-group trial randomizes **one protocol** to two formats — the cleanest causal test, and individual wins. The meta-analyses pool **dozens of trials**, each pairing whatever protocols those studies happened to use — so "individual" and "group" in the pooled comparison are different protocol pools, not the same treatment delivered two ways, and protocol variance between the pools can wash out a real format effect. The rule: **when protocol is held constant, individual beats group; when protocols are pooled, format differences vanish.**

The network meta-analysis is consistent with the within-protocol result. Group CBT as a class beats waitlist — **SMD −0.92 (95% CrI −1.33 to −0.51)** — but does **not** beat psychological placebo, where individual CBT does (**−0.56, −1.00 to −0.11**); of the four psychological interventions that individually clear the placebo bar, only group **enhanced** CBT is group-format, and the Hope, Heimberg and Turk model sits at **−1.02 (−1.42 to −0.62)** against waitlist — smaller, and non-overlapping, next to the Clark and Wells model's **−1.56 (−1.85 to −1.27)**. A cost-effectiveness model on this same network ranked individual cognitive therapy most cost-effective, then generic individual CBT, then phenelzine, then unsupported book self-help: "other drugs, group-based psychological interventions and other individually delivered psychological interventions were less cost-effective" — driven partly by psychotherapy's better-documented long-term durability.

None of this makes group CBT a poor treatment: it produces large, durable gains, replicates at trial-grade effect sizes in a real public community clinic, and an intensive 3-week format has matched a full year of usual care (7.5). It means **group delivery is the right default when the question is access — treating six or eight patients in the time it takes to treat one or two individually — not when the question is which treatment gives a given patient the best chance of full remission.** Where individual Clark and Wells therapy can be resourced, it remains the treatment of choice on every efficacy, remission, and cost metric this literature produces; where it cannot, group CBT on the Heimberg model is a well-evidenced, durable, considerably more efficient way to reach more patients — chosen for capacity, not as a second-best choice made in ignorance of the difference.

## 8. Third-wave and acceptance-based approaches

The third-wave treatments entered social anxiety with a specific and falsifiable promise: that the disorder can be dismantled by changing the patient's *relationship* to anxious internal events rather than their frequency, content or credibility. Two facts frame the whole domain, and they pull against each other. Every head-to-head randomised trial of a third-wave protocol against CBT in social anxiety disorder has failed to find a difference on the primary outcome — but not one of them was powered as a non-inferiority trial, and only one pre-specified a margin, which it then exceeded. Meanwhile the anchor network meta-analysis (101 trials, 13,164 participants, 41 interventions in 17 classes) placed the class containing mindfulness *outside* the set of classes efficacious against waitlist altogether, and found individual CBT superior to it by SMD −0.82 (95% CrI −1.41 to −0.24), a credible interval excluding zero. Reconciling those two observations is the work of this section.

### 8.1 Acceptance and commitment therapy

#### 8.1.1 The reference protocol

The best-specified ACT protocol for this disorder is the 12-session individual protocol used in the UCLA ACT-versus-CBT trial (NCT00872820): twelve weekly one-hour individual sessions plus monthly 20–35-minute booster telephone calls across the following six months, with most patients finishing in 12–16 weeks (range 11–18). Session 1 is psychoeducation, experiential exercises and an initial framing of acceptance and valued action. Sessions 2–3 are *creative hopelessness*: an explicit audit of what the patient's anxiety-control efforts have actually achieved and what they have cost in valued living. Sessions 4–5 introduce mindfulness, acceptance and **cognitive defusion** — learning to experience anxiety-related language as part of the ongoing stream of experience rather than responding to its literal meaning. Sessions 6–11 continue acceptance, mindfulness and defusion work while adding values exploration and clarification, and deploy behavioural exposures — interoceptive, in vivo and imaginal — as practice in making room for, mindfully observing and accepting anxiety *while engaging in valued activities*. Session 12 is review and forward planning.

The comparator in that trial was matched on the number of sessions devoted to exposure and differed in the framing of exposure's intent: cognitive restructuring of overestimation and catastrophising about negative evaluation plus hypothesis testing and breathing retraining across sessions 2–4, exposure introduced at session 5 and emphasised through session 11 with restructuring and breathing retained as coping tools and **the explicit goal of eventual fear reduction**, then relapse prevention.

#### 8.1.2 What the ACT therapist actually does differently

Take one patient and one avoidance: she declines the team presentation, and reports the thought *they will see my hands shaking and conclude I am pathetic*.

The cognitive therapist treats that thought as a testable prediction. She specifies it ("how many people, how visible, what will they conclude"), rates its credibility, designs a behavioural experiment with a stated disconfirmation criterion, drops the safety behaviours that would contaminate the test, and afterwards compares what the patient predicted with what happened — often against video feedback. The unit of change is belief credibility; the experiment has succeeded when the prediction fails.

The ACT therapist never adjudicates the thought's truth. In sessions 2–3 she builds the inventory instead: rehearsing sentences in the car, two drinks beforehand, scanning faces for disapproval, gripping the glass so the tremor does not show, declining the invitation. Then two questions, held open and not answered for the patient — *did it work?* (did anxiety go down, and stay down) and *what did it cost you?* The therapist deliberately withholds an alternative at this stage; the unworkability has to be the patient's own conclusion, not the therapist's argument. In sessions 4–5 the thought *I am pathetic* is handled as an event rather than a proposition — repeated until it becomes sound, prefaced with "I am having the thought that…", watched as it passes [unverified for the specific exercise names, which are conventional ACT practice rather than trial-reported]. The aim is not to make the thought less believable; it is to make it less binding. Acceptance work runs the same logic on the body: interoceptive exercises are used as *willingness* practice — deliberately making room for the racing heart and the heat in the face — not as habituation trials with a downward arc to demonstrate.

Values clarification then supplies the rationale that CBT gets from fear reduction. The therapist elicits a concrete domain — being the kind of colleague who contributes, being reachable to friends — and the presentation becomes a step in a named direction. Committed action is the exposure, with two changes to the instruction that matter operationally: anxiety is explicitly invited along rather than managed down, and the success criterion is that the action occurred, not that distress fell.

The sharpest single tell comes in the following session, when the patient reports that she gave the presentation, it felt terrible, and anxiety stayed at 80 out of 100. The cognitive therapist asks what the prediction was, whether it came true, whether safety behaviours crept back, and treats persistent distress as information that the experiment needs re-running more cleanly. The ACT therapist asks whether the patient did what she said she would do, and treats the 80 as neither a failure nor evidence of anything — the whole point is that she went, *with* the 80. Everything that follows from that difference is visible in the session: no SUDS-based homework logs, no coping-skill rehearsal, no breathing retraining, no restructuring worksheets.

That separation was achieved in the room, and it was measured. Blind ratings of 45 taped sessions from 24 patients on the Drexel adherence scale gave cognitive-therapy adherence of 0.55 (SD 0.29) in CBT versus 0.05 (0.06) in ACT, t(42)=7.87, p<.001, and ACT adherence of 0.87 (0.17) versus 0.04 (0.08), t(42)=−20.19, p<.001, with therapist competence equal (3.06 vs 3.21 on a 1–5 scale, p=.449) and treatment credibility equal (4.2 vs 4.83, p=.311). One matching failure is important: **behavioural-exposure adherence was 0.61 (0.29) in CBT and only 0.16 (0.23) in ACT**, t(42)=5.68, p<.001, despite the design's intent to equate exposure dose. Exposure adherence nonetheless predicted no post-treatment outcome (all ps>.233), and therapist-rated homework compliance across sessions 6–12 predicted nothing either (ps>.302).

#### 8.1.3 What ACT achieves in social anxiety

In that trial 100 patients were randomised (ACT 34, CBT 40, waitlist 26; modified-ITT 87 who began treatment) with DSM-IV generalised social phobia and ADIS-IV clinician severity ratings ≥4. On the composite social anxiety outcome (z-averaged LSAS-SR, SIAS and SPS) both treatments beat waitlist — CBT β=0.80 (95% CI 0.40 to 1.2), ACT β=0.88 (95% CI 0.49 to 1.3), both p<.001 — and **did not differ from one another: β=−0.09, 95% CI −0.49 to 0.32, p=.675**. Clinically significant improvement, defined as a clinician severity rating ≤3 *plus* reliable change on SPS or SIAS, was 52.3% (11/21) for CBT, 40.9% (9/22) for ACT and 4.3% (1/23) for waitlist at post (CBT vs ACT p=.45); on intention-to-treat with last observation carried forward the twelve-month figures were **47.8% for CBT and 33.3% for ACT**. Session completion favoured ACT non-significantly (83% vs 67%, p=.149). Quality of life on the QOLI beat waitlist in neither arm, and self-rated distress during a three-minute videotaped speech fell in all three groups including waitlist (p<.001) — a repeated-assessment artefact the investigators explicitly warn against treating as outcome.

Two moderator findings run *against* ACT theory. The AAQ (psychological flexibility) × group × time interaction was significant, χ²=4.04, p=.044, and at twelve months patients one standard deviation *below* the AAQ mean — the most experientially avoidant, the group ACT is theorised to suit — **did better with CBT: β=1.03, 95% CI 0.055 to 2.01, p=.038**. Behaviourally measured public-speaking avoidance moderated in the same direction: more avoidant patients benefited more from CBT by twelve-month follow-up. One contamination signal deserves recording because it recurs: between six and twelve months four ACT participants started a new psychotherapy and no CBT participant did (none of the four achieved reliable change), and the same asymmetry appeared in the mixed-anxiety parent trial.

#### 8.1.4 Group, self-help and digital delivery

Mindfulness and acceptance-based group therapy (MAGT) — abbreviated MBCT mindfulness exercises plus didactic and experiential ACT components plus in-session and in-vivo exposure, over 12 weekly group sessions — was tested against cognitive-behavioural group therapy and waitlist in 137 patients (53/53/31). Both treatments beat waitlist; MAGT and CBGT did not differ on social anxiety or most secondary variables. The hypothesis was stated in advance as "both superior to control but not significantly different from one another" — a non-inferiority claim tested with a superiority design — and **no between-group effect sizes or confidence intervals were reported**, which is a real and consequential reporting gap. In the same sample both arms reduced self-focused attention and safety behaviours relative to control with no difference between them.

Self-help works. An ACT-based mindfulness and acceptance workbook against waitlist in 117 community help-seekers produced between-group effect sizes of **0.74 to 0.79** on social anxiety outcomes, with change also in self-compassion, mindfulness, acceptance and depression; latent change-score modelling in an extended sample supported a unidirectional path in which increases in acceptance preceded decreases in social anxiety rather than the reverse. Internet-delivered ACT with a smartphone app (NCT01963806, n=152, social anxiety and/or panic) gave **d=0.70 for social anxiety** against waitlist, with **no difference between therapist-guided and unguided** delivery. In an eight-module internet acceptance-based CBT (n=42), therapist support bought retention and not outcome: attrition 20% with support versus 50% without, with no between-group symptom difference.

#### 8.1.5 The meta-analytic verdict on ACT

Pooled across 60 randomised trials and 4,234 participants, ACT's mean effect size across all comparisons is **0.42**, and **against any cognitive or behavioural comparator it is 0.16, non-significant**. The pooled estimate had *deteriorated* from 0.68 in the previous version of the same review with no accompanying improvement in methodological quality, and ACT was rated **"experimental" for social anxiety** — a rung below "possibly efficacious". The mediation literature reinforces this reading rather than rescuing it: in the mixed-anxiety parent trial, cognitive defusion changed substantially in both ACT and CBT (d=0.90–1.93) and **mediated quality of life, behavioural avoidance and depression across both treatments** (R² change 0.06–0.13); within the SAD trial itself, the curvature of experiential-avoidance decline mediated outcome in ACT only, but the curvature of negative-cognition decline predicted outcome in *both*. The authors of one such analysis concluded that the two treatments "share common treatment mechanisms, making them more similar than distinct."

### 8.2 Mindfulness-based approaches

MBSR is delivered here in its standard Kabat-Zinn form: eight weekly 2.5-hour classes plus a one-day meditation retreat plus daily home practice — breath focus, body scan, open monitoring, brief informal practice and Hatha yoga — with instructors of substantial experience (mean 15.7 years in the exercise-comparison trial).

The first head-to-head, 53 patients with generalised SAD randomised to an 8-week MBSR course or 12 weekly sessions of CBGT, **found CBT superior**: significantly lower clinician- *and* patient-rated social anxiety, and significantly greater response and remission (full remission 8/18 with CBGT versus 2/22 with MBSR), with mood, functioning and quality of life improving comparably. The confound cuts both ways — unequal dose (12 versus 8 weeks), no control group, permitted concurrent psychotropics, no adherence measurement, no follow-up.

The Stanford dose-matched trial (NCT02036658) removed the dose confound, and the CBT advantage disappeared. From 724 online screens, 108 unmedicated patients with generalised SAD and LSAS-SR >60 were randomised to CBGT, MBSR or waitlist, **both active arms receiving 12 weekly 2.5-hour sessions = 30 hours** (the MBSR retreat day was converted into four extra weekly classes between sessions 6 and 7). A trained rater sat in every session: adherence 4.92±0.27 for CBGT versus 4.81±0.17 for MBSR (p=.43); completion (≥9/12 sessions) 33/36 in each arm; a mean of 4.5 (SD 0.84) in-session exposures per CBGT patient. LSAS-SR fell **44 points (48%, within-group d=1.56) with CBGT and 36.5 points (40%, d=1.43) with MBSR** against 12.5 points with waitlist; both beat waitlist (ts>5.36, ps<.001) and **CBGT versus MBSR gave t=1.36, df=68, p=.18**. Clinically significant improvement — reliable change of >13.83 LSAS-SR points *plus* crossing below 47.68 into the functional range — was 44.4% with CBGT and 38.9% with MBSR (χ²=0.23, p=.63, φ=.06) against 11.1% with waitlist.

The process data from that trial are the strongest evidence in the domain that the two treatments move nearly identical machinery. Reappraisal frequency, cognitive distortions, mindfulness, attention focusing and attention shifting all changed to statistically indistinguishable degrees; **CBGT increased mindfulness on the FFMQ by d=1.67, larger than MBSR's own d=1.44**. The *only* differential process effect in the entire trial was greater reduction in subtle avoidance and safety behaviours after CBGT (−20.1 points, d=1.27, versus −9.8, d=0.64; t=2.30, df=66, p=.025). Six processes mediated both treatments against waitlist; only reappraisal self-efficacy and reduced safety behaviours mediated the CBGT-versus-MBSR contrast. Weekly-trajectory analysis found the expected specialisations (CBGT better at disputing anxious thoughts and reappraisal success; MBSR better at acceptance of anxiety) but a partly *crossed* mechanism pattern — in MBSR, weekly increases in "disputing anxious thoughts and feelings", a CBT process, predicted subsequent symptom reduction, while in CBGT they did not. Moderator work in the same sample points one way clinically: **high anger suppression predicted significantly greater social anxiety reduction with CBGT than MBSR**, and high anger expression predicted lesser reduction in MBSR only.

The most deflating mindfulness datum is the exercise comparison. Fifty-six patients with generalised SAD were randomised to MBSR (n=31) or aerobic exercise (n=25) — a two-month gym membership with at least two individual moderate-intensity sessions plus one group class weekly for eight weeks, with **intensity and heart rate not monitored**. There was **no group-by-time interaction for any clinical measure (all p>.16) or well-being measure (all p>.33)**, at post or at three-month follow-up, with practice time equated at roughly 3.4 hours per week in both arms. Both improved substantially on time (LSAS-SR F=67.37, p<.001, η²p=.64 pre-post; η²p=.69 to three-month follow-up). Clinically significant change on LSAS-SR was **22.5% with MBSR and 29.5% with aerobic exercise**, all comparisons non-significant — and the investigators' own verdict is the honest one: about a quarter reached clinically significant change, "lower than what has been found in previous studies (ranges from 31–75%)", and these interventions require refinement "before these can be recommended as standalone interventions". Severity moderated in a clinically usable way: MBSR was most effective at *lower* baseline severity, aerobic exercise at *higher*.

MBCT proper has one small crossover pilot: 26 young adults received 8 × 2-hour group MBCT and 12 × 2-hour group CBT in reversed order, with pre-post effects of **d=0.78 for MBCT and d=1.15 for CBT** — "not significantly different from, although numerically lower than" CBT, in a design with carryover confounding and no power to support any equivalence claim. An uncontrolled 8-week MBSR trial in 53 students with SAD gave ITT effects of d=0.80 on SAD symptoms and d=0.61 on distress, with 37% achieving clinically significant change; notably the **largest effects were on self-compassion (d=1.49) and mindfulness (d=1.35)** — the process moved further than the symptom.

### 8.3 Compassion, shame and self-criticism

The only compassion-augmented protocol with a randomised active comparator in adult SAD is the MBI-SAD: 12 weekly group sessions combining standard MBSR elements, **explicit self-compassion training**, and **mindful exposure** — practising a mindful response to the internal experiences evoked by feared social situations. Against waitlist (21 versus 18) it was feasible (81% attended ≥75% of sessions) and superior on social anxiety severity (p≤0.0001), depression, social adjustment and self-compassion, with gains held at three months. Against CBGT in 97 patients, **CBGT fared better on clinician- and self-rated social anxiety severity; the difference on the Liebowitz Social Anxiety Scale exceeded the pre-specified non-inferiority margin, but the confidence interval extended in both directions around that margin, leaving the result inconclusive rather than negative**. This is the textbook signature of an underpowered non-inferiority trial: it established neither non-inferiority nor inferiority. The MBI-SAD compared favourably on depression, self-esteem, life satisfaction and social adjustment — and, contrary to its own theory, **did not produce greater change in mindfulness or self-compassion than CBGT**. The investigators' conclusion was that CBGT should be considered first-line. Patient preference is real but does not convert: pre-randomisation, twice as many patients preferred the mindfulness arm, and being allocated to one's preference affected only perceived credibility — not response, initiation, attendance, homework compliance or satisfaction.

Self-compassion training as a standalone was tested in 63 adults with diagnosed SAD randomised for six weeks to waitlist, self-guided self-compassion training, or self-guided applied relaxation. Self-compassion training **beat waitlist with between-group effect sizes of only d=0.12 to 0.33, and did not beat applied relaxation at all (ps>.05, ds=0.01 to 0.05)** — while producing greater clinically significant gains in self-compassion and in fear of self-compassion than both comparators. The process target moved specifically; the symptom did not beat a credible active control. Formal compassion-focused therapy for SAD remains unread: NCT04979676 (four arms — cognitive therapy, CFT, ACT, waitlist — in 92 adolescents, 10 weekly individual online sessions plus two boosters) has **no results posted** and, by the registry's own description, filled its arms sequentially across three project years rather than randomising them concurrently; NCT07244198 (49 adolescents, online CFT versus waitlist with heart-rate-variability endpoints) has no results posted; NCT05671419 (Mindful Self-Compassion, n=40) is recruiting. Experimental work is more encouraging than the trials: a single 30-minute self-compassionate writing task directed at maladaptive self-beliefs reduced endorsement of those beliefs, expected anxiety and expected safety behaviours across samples of 442 and 229 — but a separate self-compassion-induction study found that in a live videoconference interaction (n=114), state self-compassion and safety behaviours did not differ from control.

### 8.4 Metacognitive therapy

Metacognitive therapy targets the cognitive attentional syndrome — anticipatory processing, self-focused attention and post-event processing — and the metacognitive beliefs that sustain it, deliberately **not** the content of social-evaluative cognitions and **without** relying on exposure for disconfirmation. **There is no adequately powered randomised trial of it in a SAD-only sample.** The direct evidence is an A-B replication series across three patients spanning performance-type SAD, generalised SAD, and generalised SAD with comorbid avoidant personality disorder — all three responded with gains largely maintained at six months — plus a four-patient case-control against applied relaxation.
