The largest randomised trial containing SAD patients is a mixed-diagnosis residential study of 90 inpatients with primary PTSD, social phobia or panic ± agoraphobia: MCT was **significantly better than disorder-specific CBT at post-treatment, d=0.7, with no difference at one-year follow-up** because the CBT group continued to improve during follow-up. It cannot be quoted as a SAD result. Correlational work in 52 SAD patients from a cognitive-therapy/paroxetine trial found that change in metacognitions uniquely predicted improvement in interpersonal problems once the overlap with social-phobic cognitions and symptoms was controlled — support for a target, not for an efficacy claim. The registry offers little relief: NCT03514225 is a five-patient replication series and NCT07382323 is a 30-participant *qualitative* study not yet recruiting.

### 8.5 Imagery rescripting

Imagery rescripting targets the aversive autobiographical memory encoded in the recurrent negative self-image, in three phases: re-experience the memory from the original perspective; observe it from the present-day adult perspective and **intervene** on behalf of the former self; re-experience it with the changes in place. All randomised evidence in SAD is single-session and small. Rescripting without any cognitive restructuring beat a reading task on memory distress, image distress, fear of negative evaluation and social interaction anxiety at one week (n=14). A single session against no intervention (n=25) produced substantial symptom reduction plus shifts in the content, validity and accuracy of memory-derived core beliefs about self and others — but not about the world. The cleanest dismantling (n=33, rescripting versus imaginal exposure versus supportive counselling) found that **rescripting increased positive and neutral memory details only, imaginal exposure increased both positive/neutral and negative details, and supportive counselling changed nothing**; memory appraisals did not differ across conditions, but rescripting participants were more likely to update negative memory-derived core beliefs. A 77-patient randomised comparison with continuous physiological monitoring showed the predicted activation-then-transformation signature: an initial rise in negative feeling into phase 1 with higher mean heart rate, then a larger and more stable fall across phases 2 and 3 with significantly higher HRV in phase 3. The only multi-session data are an uncontrolled multiple-baseline open trial of eight sessions delivered by videoconference (n=35): negligible change across an eight-week baseline (SIAS-6 d=0.22; SPS-6 d=−0.03), then **pre-post SIAS-6 d=0.81 (95% CI 0.32–1.29) and SPS-6 d=0.80 (0.30–1.27)**, holding at three months (d=0.85 and d=0.90), with **66% no longer meeting SAD criteria at post and 74% at three months**. A registered head-to-head of one-session imaginal exposure versus rescripting (NCT04635904, n=100) has an unknown status.

### 8.6 Emotion-focused therapy

The retrievable EFT-for-SAD evidence base is a **12-patient non-concurrent multiple-baseline study** — patients randomised to a 4-, 8- or 12-week baseline, then up to 28 sessions using an empathic therapeutic relationship, two-chair work for self-criticism, empty-chair work for unresolved feelings toward significant others, and focusing. LSAS did not change during baseline, improved during treatment with **Cohen's d=−2.37**, and held through follow-up; **7 of 11 completers no longer met SAD criteria**. Self-criticism followed the same trajectory as symptoms, while self-reassurance improved significantly only during the follow-up phase. In the same twelve patients, across 1,008 coded segments, more than half showed a rupture–resolution alliance pattern and cross-lagged analysis found that **symptoms predicted alliance while alliance did not predict symptoms**. A PubMed search for a randomised comparative trial of EFT versus CBT in SAD returns nothing. Treat the d=−2.37 as a hypothesis, not an effect size.

### 8.7 Adjudicating the disagreement

The network meta-analysis and the individual trials disagree for mechanical reasons that are worth naming. The network's "other therapies" class is a *class*, pooling heterogeneous and largely small mindfulness-adjacent protocols with a shared reference structure; the individual trials are single, well-delivered, dose-matched protocols in specialist hands, each with an active comparator rather than a network of them. The class-level estimate answers "does the average intervention of this kind, as tested, separate from waitlist across the whole evidence base" — and it does not. The trial-level estimates answer "did this protocol, delivered this well, match CBGT in this sample" — and they did, in samples that could not have detected the difference that matters.

That last point is decisive and should govern practice. With roughly 30 per arm, 80% power detects only d≈0.73; with 36 per arm, d≈0.67; with 53 per arm, d≈0.55. **None of these trials could have detected a small-to-moderate true CBT advantage.** The reference ACT trial's own response gap — 52.3% versus 40.9% at post, 47.8% versus 33.3% on ITT at twelve months — is precisely the size of difference these designs are blind to; detecting an 11–14 percentage-point difference in response rate at 80% power requires roughly 250–350 per arm. "No significant difference" in this literature is a statement about sample size, not about equivalence. Against that, three findings point the other way with data rather than absence of data: CBGT beat MBSR on clinician- and patient-rated severity and on remission (8/18 versus 2/22) in the unequal-dose comparison; CBGT beat the MBI-SAD by a margin the trial could not resolve; and at 1.5-year follow-up in a mixed anxious/depressed sample, cognitive therapy maintained gains better than ACT, with one-third more patients in the normative depression range and **more than twice as many in the normative functioning range**.

The practical conclusion is narrow and defensible. No third-wave protocol has earned first-line status for social anxiety disorder, and the treatments that have shown they can beat their own matched placebo remain individual CBT and the antidepressants. Third-wave approaches earn a place as second-line or by indication: where a patient refuses cognitive therapy or has failed it; where a comorbid mood disorder is present, since ACT outperformed CBT in that subgroup; where broader well-being outcomes are the priority, since the MBI-SAD compared favourably to CBGT on depression, self-esteem, life satisfaction and social adjustment while losing on social anxiety itself; and where self-help is the realistic option, since an ACT-based workbook produced between-group effects of 0.74–0.79. Two cautions travel with that recommendation. Most third-wave SAD protocols are group-delivered, and the health-economic model built on the same network found group psychological interventions *less* cost-effective than individual cognitive therapy, generic individual CBT, phenelzine and book-based self-help without support. And the theory has not survived its own tests: the most experientially avoidant patients did better with CBT, CBGT raised mindfulness more than MBSR did, the MBI-SAD raised self-compassion no more than CBGT, and the acceptance mediator in the group trial did not behave as predicted. Choose a third-wave treatment for the patient in front of you, on grounds of preference, retention, comorbidity or access — not on the belief that it works through a different and better mechanism, because that belief is currently unsupported.

## 9. Psychodynamic therapy, interpersonal therapy, and supportive approaches

The field's shorthand — cognitive therapy works for this disorder and the alternatives do not — is half right, and the wrong half matters clinically. Manualized short-term psychodynamic psychotherapy is a genuinely efficacious treatment, tested in one of the largest psychotherapy trials ever run in social anxiety disorder, and by two years indistinguishable from CBT on remission and response. Interpersonal psychotherapy is a weaker case, and this manual will say so plainly. And supportive therapy is not the straw man of an introduction paragraph — it is the yardstick, because the effect size of a credible non-directive comparator is what tells you how much of any waitlist-controlled result is treatment and how much is contact, expectancy and time.

### 9.1 The psychodynamic model: the core conflictual relationship theme applied to social anxiety

The treatment is an adaptation of Luborsky's supportive-expressive therapy, organised around the **core conflictual relationship theme (CCRT)** — a repeating three-part pattern comprising a **Wish (W)**, an anticipated **Response from Others (RO)**, and the patient's own **Response of Self (RS)**.

The disorder-specific move is elegant: **in social anxiety disorder the RS component simply *is* the symptom.** The wish is for attention, recognition and affirmation; the anticipated response from others is humiliation and contempt; the response of self is anxiety, blushing, self-monitoring and withdrawal. Shame is positioned as the disorder's central affect and formulated in the same grammar — an RS directed toward devaluing internal objects, sustaining a self-reinforcing cycle of anxiety and avoidance — with low self-esteem theorised to shape both the anticipated RO and the RS.

Note the convergence with the Clark and Wells model: both make anticipatory catastrophic expectation of others' judgement the engine and the patient's self-directed response the maintaining mechanism, differing over whether that expectation is a belief to be disconfirmed by experiment or a relationship template to be recognised across settings. A clinician fluent in one will not find the other alien.

### 9.2 What a session actually contains

The manual retains four generic supportive-expressive elements — explicit goal setting; sustained focus on the symptom-linked CCRT; **expressive (interpretive) interventions** building insight into the theme; and **supportive interventions**, of which establishing a secure helping alliance is named the single most important. Onto these it layers five disorder-specific additions: psychoeducation about the disorder and the treatment model; an explicit focus on **shame**; an explicit focus on the **unrealistic demands** the patient places on themselves; active **encouragement to confront anxiety-producing situations**; and frankly directive interventions, including **specific prescriptions to stop persisting self-devaluation**. The last two matter for reading the comparative trials: this manual contains a graded-confrontation instruction and a behavioural prescription, so the contrast with exposure-based CBT is not insight versus action.

A linking (expressive) intervention as written in the manual reads: *"As we have seen, you are not only afraid of exposing yourself (response from the self), but you sometimes wish to be at the center of attention and to be affirmed by others (wish). However, you are afraid that they will humiliate you (response from others)."* The theme is then worked through in three domains in parallel — present relationships, past relationships, and the relationship with the therapist.

**Dose.** Up to **25 individual sessions of 50 minutes** plus up to **5 preparatory sessions** — a ceiling of 30 contacts — designed for six months. In the pivotal trial it was delivered over a mean of **37.4 ± 18.0 weeks**, with **25.67 ± 9.61 sessions completed** against **25.84 ± 9.13** in the CBT arm (p = .51): dose was equal across arms, removing the commonest confound in this comparison.

**Therapist preparation.** The 53 psychodynamic therapists averaged **8.0 ± 9.0 years** of clinical experience, were trained by the manual's authors, each completed **two pilot cases** before admission, received 90-minute group supervision fortnightly for six months then monthly, and had **every session videotaped** for supervision and integrity rating. That is the training intensity behind the results below — and §9.4 shows what happens when it is reduced.

### 9.3 SOPHO-NET Study A: the pivotal trial in full

**Design and flow.** A multicentre three-arm parallel-group randomized **superiority** trial across five German university outpatient centres, recruiting 11 April 2007 to 15 May 2012, independently monitored. Randomization was **3:3:1** (CBT : psychodynamic : waitlist), the small waitlist arm a deliberate power trade. Allegiance was controlled architecturally: at each centre one clinic delivered the psychodynamic treatment and a *different* clinic delivered CBT, with experts in both modalities as local investigators at every site. 1,450 screened → 514 initially randomized → **495 assigned: CBT n = 209, psychodynamic n = 207, waitlist n = 79.**

**Population.** Ages 18–70, DSM-IV social anxiety disorder by structured interview, **LSAS > 30**, social anxiety the primary and most severe disorder. Excluded: psychosis, acute substance disorders, **cluster A and cluster B personality disorders**, self-harm risk, and **any concurrent psychotherapy or psychotropic medication**. Mean age 35.2 ± 12.2, about 55% female, baseline LSAS anxiety 39.7 ± 11.0 and avoidance 33.2 ± 12.0 (total ≈ 73).

**Outcomes and blinding.** **Remission = LSAS ≤ 30**; **response = ≥ 31% LSAS reduction** (chosen for comparability with a CGI-I of 2 or better). Twenty-three specifically trained assessors, masked to condition, rated all outcomes; interviews were videotaped; LSAS inter-rater reliability was **Kendall's W = 0.98**.

**Primary results, end of treatment, intention-to-treat:**

| | CBT (n = 209) | Psychodynamic (n = 207) | Waitlist (n = 79) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Remission (LSAS ≤ 30) | **36%** | **26%** | 9% |
| Response (≥ 31% LSAS reduction) | **60%** | **52%** | 15% |

Both active treatments beat waitlist on both endpoints. **CBT was significantly superior for remission but not for response** — a ten-point remission gap and an eight-point response gap. Between-group effect sizes were small, and the trialists recorded that the CBT-versus-psychodynamic differences fell **below the trial's a priori threshold of clinical significance**. On secondary measures CBT was superior on social phobia and interpersonal-problem scales, with **no difference on depression**.

**Attrition.** Completers were **159/209 (76.1%)** in CBT and **149/207 (72.0%)** in the psychodynamic arm — roughly 24% and 28% acute dropout, not significantly different by arm. Dropouts were sicker and more socially disadvantaged: LSAS **79.46 ± 22.85 versus 71.35 ± 21.06**; 44.4% versus 69.5% held a high-school certificate; 30.2% versus 43.1% were partnered; 1.32 versus 0.90 comorbid diagnoses. Baseline LSAS predicted dropout but explained only **4.8% of variance** (p = .015).

**The two-year follow-up — and how to read it.** With assessments at 6, 12 and 24 months post-treatment, **response converged at approximately 70% and remission at approximately 40% in both arms**, stable or rising, with **no significant between-treatment differences after the six-month point**. This is the most-quoted finding in the psychodynamic literature for this disorder, and it is real — but it rests on **48% attrition at 6 months and up to 81% attrition at the 2-year assessment**. The analysis was intention-to-treat with imputation, and imputation across four missing patients in five is a modelling assumption, not an observation. The honest statement: *the acute advantage of CBT was not detectable at two years in a dataset in which most patients were no longer observed.* Nor can the null be upgraded to equivalence. The published standard for non-inferiority — set out by the psychodynamic manual's own principal investigator — requires an a-priori, empirically justified margin, a pre-registered sample size powered for non-inferiority, and a non-active control; this trial satisfies the last condition only, having tested superiority with no margin defined. The interpretation was contested at the time in published correspondence, worth reading in the original.

**Health economics.** The incremental cost-effectiveness ratio was **€3,615 per responder for CBT versus €4,958 for psychodynamic therapy**, and **€5,788 versus €10,733 per remitted patient** — the remission gap roughly doubles the cost of the most clinically meaningful outcome. At a 97.5% certainty threshold CBT was cost-effective at a willingness-to-pay of **≥ €16,100 per responder and ≥ €26,605 per remitter**, whereas for psychodynamic therapy cost-effectiveness was certain only for response, and only at **≥ €27,290**. Over **30 months** from a societal perspective, using anxiety-free days, the probability that CBT is cost-effective relative to psychodynamic therapy was **65%, 83% and 96%** at additional willingness-to-pay of €0, €10 and €30 per anxiety-free day. The clinical difference vanishes by two years; the economic difference does not.

**Mechanism findings that embarrass the model.** Across 474 patients with valid attachment ratings, CBT reduced attachment anxiety (ES −.30, p = .005) and avoidance (ES −.23, p = .034) relative to waitlist, while the psychodynamic arm moved **neither** (anxiety ES −.12, p = .254; avoidance ES −.01, p = .976), CBT superior on avoidance in the direct contrast (−.24, p = .009) — the investigators state they had expected the reverse. Within the psychodynamic arm, baseline LSAS explained **39%** of endstate variance and **27.2%** of remission, and while **shame and self-esteem correlated with outcome bivariately, neither survived multivariate adjustment** — the manual's two named targets add nothing beyond symptom severity. Against this, both treatments improved implicit and explicit self-esteem and every facet of self-concept with no between-treatment difference: the model's constructs move, they simply do not move preferentially or predict. One usable moderator emerged — patients in the **highest attachment-avoidance quartile** did better with CBT at post-treatment and at one year.

### 9.4 The rest of the psychodynamic evidence, and what happens outside a trial

The **adolescent** replication (ages 14–20; N = 107: CBT 34, psychodynamic 34, waitlist 39) found both treatments superior to waitlist on masked-rated LSAS-CA — CBT d = 0.61 (95% CI 0.14–1.08), psychodynamic d = 0.53 (0.06–1.00) — with response **66% / 54% / 20%** and remission **47% / 34% / 6%**, stable to 12 months: the same ordering, the same non-significant gap. Its economic companion put CBT at **€18,824 per QALY** versus waitlist with 63% probability of falling under €50,000/QALY, while **psychodynamic therapy did not prove cost-effective**.

Smaller trials mostly find nothing between the modalities. A Dutch trial (N = 47; psychodynamic 22, CBT 27) found large within-subject effects, remission above 50% and **no difference on any measure** — while delivering a mean of 31.4 psychodynamic against 19.8 CBT sessions, a 1.6-fold dose advantage to the arm that tied. A 2×2 factorial trial found **group CBT and group psychodynamic therapy indistinguishable** (12.33% versus 22.54% improvement, p = .11) while sertraline beat placebo (25.73% versus 9.46%). Adding psychodynamic group therapy to clonazepam improved global impression (p = 0.033) with **no difference on LSAS, quality of life or depression**. The earliest dynamic trial, in 42 patients judged unsuitable for insight-oriented work, found dynamically oriented supportive therapy and prolonged exposure both superior to relaxation and to the basal condition of information, self-exposure instructions and medication alone.

Crucially, **only one psychodynamic trial in this disorder has ever used a credible placebo comparator**: 12-session psychodynamic group therapy against a lecture-discussion and support control, n = 30, favouring the active treatment on LSAS, F(1,28) = 4.84, p = 0.036. Thirty patients in a group format is the entire placebo-controlled evidence base.

Internet delivery performs best. Affect-focused internet psychodynamic therapy beat waitlist at **d = 1.05 (0.62–1.53)** with gains extending to 24 months; a guided-versus-unguided trial (N = 181) gave guided **d = 1.07 (0.72–1.43)**, unguided **d = 0.61 (0.25–0.98)**, guided over unguided **d = 0.46 (0.11–0.80)** at post-treatment but not at 6 or 12 months. A counterpoint: an AI-delivered comparison (N = 102) found **neither** psychodynamic nor CBT chatbot arms superior to waitlist, because **the waitlist itself improved d = 0.54**.

**Meta-analysis.** The best current synthesis — 11 RCTs, n = 1,167 — gives **g = −0.89 (−1.18 to −0.60)** versus waitlist after trim-and-fill, **g = 0.01 (−0.14 to 0.15)** versus any active comparator, **g = 0.00 (−0.23 to 0.23)** versus CBT specifically, **no dropout difference** (logOR 0.10, −0.34 to 0.54) and no long-term between-group difference (g = −0.01, −0.25 to 0.24). Two subsidiary findings deserve more attention than the headline: study quality **significantly moderated effects against passive controls** (R² = 72.8%, p = .013), with **lower-quality studies reporting larger effects**, and only 2 of 11 studies were at low risk of bias on all four Cochrane domains. A competing meta-analysis of 12 RCTs found psychodynamic therapy **weaker** than active comparators (SMD 0.15, 95% CI 0.02–0.28); it has been contested in published rebuttals alleging inclusion and exclusion errors, and should be quoted only with that caveat. Across anxiety disorders generally, psychodynamic therapy beats controls at g = 0.64 but sits at g = 0.02 against alternative treatments post-treatment, drifting to **g = −0.26 beyond one year**; the Cochrane review likewise notes effect sizes growing at long-term follow-up while **some lost statistical significance**. The most allegiance-proof estimate comes from a formal equivalence test run by adversarial collaboration between psychodynamic and CBT representatives: **g = −0.153 (90% equivalence CI −0.227 to −0.079)** against a pre-set margin of 0.25 — equivalence supported, though across disorders rather than in social anxiety specifically. A pre-registered umbrella review grades the anxiety-disorder evidence **moderate quality** and the recommendation **"strong", not "very strong"**.

**And then there is the transfer trial.** When the same manual moved into routine practice — 49 therapists cluster-randomized to the manual or to psychodynamic treatment as usual, trained with **two workshops of about five hours** instead of pilot cases and intensive supervision — **remission after 25 sessions was 9% with the manual versus 16% without it** (adjusted OR 0.75, 95% CI 0.19–2.93, p = .681) and response **33% versus 28%** (OR 1.24, 0.49–3.16). Treatment was not shortened: 36.35 ± 34.48 versus 33.86 ± 23.52 sessions, **35.4% exceeding 25 sessions**. Therapist allegiance did not differ (8.61 ± 1.11 versus 8.17 ± 1.38, p = .108), and adherence separated only on the disorder-specific technique subscale — the supportive (p = .415) and expressive (p = .755) dimensions did not differ at all. The authors' verdict: *"These results can be seen as being disappointing."* The efficacy of this manual is inseparable from the training and supervision that delivered it.

### 9.5 Interpersonal psychotherapy: the honest verdict

Interpersonal psychotherapy was built as a time-limited manualized treatment for major depression and adapted to social anxiety by retaining its four problem areas — grief, role disputes, role transitions, and interpersonal deficits or sensitivity — while reframing social anxiety as a **maintained interpersonal-behaviour pattern** rather than a maladaptive belief system. The target is the pattern of behaviour in relationships, not the prediction about them.

An open trial of 14-week modified IPT in nine patients yielded **7/9 (78%) much or very much improved**, and the authors rightly called for a controlled comparison. Three controlled trials followed; the results are consistently unfavourable.

**Against cognitive therapy, in outpatients.** A two-site trial randomized 117 patients with primary social anxiety disorder to cognitive therapy, IPT, or waitlist, delivering **16 individual sessions plus one booster**, with post-treatment assessment 20 weeks after randomization and masked-evaluator CGI-I response as the primary outcome. **Response was 65.8% for cognitive therapy, 42.1% for IPT and 7.3% for waitlist.** Cognitive therapy was significantly superior to IPT on both response rate and independently assessed LSAS; both actives beat waitlist. At one year the gap had widened, not closed: **68.4% versus 31.6%**, IPT response *falling* by ten points while cognitive therapy rose slightly. There were **no significant treatment × site interactions**, so this is not a site-allegiance artefact. (Per-arm sizes are arithmetically consistent with roughly 38 per active arm and 41 on waitlist, but that is inference, not a reported figure: treat as `[unverified]`.) Companion analyses sharpen rather than rescue: 22.4% of patients had a sudden gain, equally frequent, large and timed in both arms, but **sudden gains left lower post-treatment symptoms in cognitive therapy**; and session-by-session common-factor ratings found cognitive therapy higher on behavioural coping focus, resource activation and motivational clarification, with **no difference in problem activation or in the therapeutic relationship** — outcome was predicted by resource and problem activation, common factors rather than modality.

**Against a residential cognitive package.** Eighty chronic, highly comorbid, medication-free patients — most having already failed other treatments — were randomized to 10 weeks of residential cognitive or residential interpersonal therapy, both modified for an integrated group-plus-individual residential format. Both improved; **there were no significant between-treatment differences**, cognitive therapy superior on one of three weekly secondary measures ("social role security"), with continued improvement to one year. Then the trialists said the thing that matters: the residential cognitive arm **"was associated with less improvement compared to individual CT in other recent trials."** This is a tie at a lower altitude, not parity with the reference treatment. Process analysis found cognitive-model variables (self-focus, estimated probability and cost of negative social events, safety behaviours) *and* the interpersonal variable "perceived acceptance by others" each predicted subsequent weekly change, with **no process × treatment interactions** and cognitive variables falling similarly in both arms — the interpersonal treatment appears to have worked partly through cognitive change.

**Against supportive therapy.** Seventy patients were randomized to 14 weekly individual sessions of IPT or supportive therapy. Both improved; **IPT was not superior**. LSAS fell from **67.7 to 46.9 with IPT and 64.5 to 49.8 with supportive therapy** — roughly 30.7% and 22.8%, neither arm reaching a mean 31% response threshold — with **no difference in the proportion of responders**, and IPT superior on exactly one measure, the Brief Fear of Negative Evaluation Scale. A blind adherence audit of 133 videotapes from 53 participants found statistical separation but **"higher than expected overlap"**, attributed to the crossed design in which the same therapists delivered both; greater use of IPT techniques in early sessions predicted better outcome in *both* arms. The trial cannot cleanly separate "IPT does not beat supportive therapy" from "the supportive arm was partly IPT" — but neither reading helps IPT.

**And in the network.** IPT is not modelled as its own node in the anchor network meta-analysis; it is pooled with mindfulness and supportive therapy into "other psychological therapies", and **that class is not superior to waitlist**, with individual CBT beating it by **SMD −0.82 (95% CrI −1.41 to −0.24)**. There is still **no adequately powered IPT trial in this disorder carrying both a waitlist and a credible placebo arm.** The precise statement: IPT produces real symptom reduction, has never separated from a supportive control, loses substantially to cognitive therapy at post-treatment and by more at one year, and has no evidence supporting it as a first- or second-line choice here.

### 9.6 Supportive and non-directive therapy: the comparator that sets the scale

The most consequential number in this domain is not a treatment effect. It is **psychological placebo versus waitlist, SMD −0.63 (95% CrI −0.90 to −0.36)** — because **psychodynamic psychotherapy versus waitlist in the same network is −0.62 (−0.93 to −0.31)**. The network's authors put it without hedging: psychodynamic therapy was effective, "although its effects were similar to psychological placebo". Only two classes in the entire network cleared their own matched placebo — individual CBT (**−0.56, 95% CrI −1.00 to −0.11**) and the SSRIs/SNRIs (−0.44, −0.67 to −0.22).

This is not an artefact of one network. In depression, non-directive supportive therapy achieves **g = 0.58 (0.45–0.72)** against control conditions, and its apparent inferiority to other psychotherapies, **g = −0.20 (−0.32 to −0.08)**, **disappeared entirely after controlling for researcher allegiance**. The same work apportions total improvement as **33.3% extra-therapeutic, 49.6% non-specific and 17.1% specific** — a ratio worth holding in mind before crediting a good outcome to a technique.

Two caveats stop this becoming nihilism. First, **not every "supportive" comparator is a bona fide therapy**: one frequently cited trial set about 20 contact hours of CBT against **six 30-minute supportive sessions — three contact hours**, a sevenfold dose asymmetry, which is a low-dose attention control rather than an alternative treatment. The canonical attention placebo in this literature — educational-supportive group therapy explicitly constructed to be of **equal credibility** to cognitive-behavioural group therapy across 133 patients — is a fair test, and both active treatments beat it. Second, when supportive therapy *is* delivered at full dose, CBT still wins: in youth social anxiety, internet CBT (n = 51) versus internet-delivered supportive therapy (n = 52), matched for modules, parent involvement and therapist video contact, gave a masked clinician-severity **between-group d = 0.67 (95% CI 0.21–1.12)** at three-month follow-up — and **one suicide attempt occurred in the supportive arm**, a reminder that "inert comparator" is a statistical description, not a clinical one.

Why all of this bears on every trial in this chapter: across **333 RCTs and 41,480 participants**, waitlist-controlled trials returned **g = 0.95 (0.85–1.04)** against care-as-usual-controlled trials at **g = 0.63 (0.55–0.71)**, p < 0.001, robust to full meta-regression adjustment. The mechanism is not that treatment works better in waitlist trials but that the **waitlist arm under-improves** — pre-post change within waitlist **g = 0.37** versus **g = 0.64** within care as usual. Essentially every positive psychodynamic estimate for social anxiety disorder rests on a waitlist contrast.

### 9.7 Researcher allegiance: how much of the gap does it explain?

Enough to matter, not enough to erase the gap in this disorder.

The best overall estimate — a meta-meta-analysis of 30 meta-analyses with primary-study overlap controlled — puts the researcher-allegiance–outcome association at **r = .262 (p = .002, I² = 28.98%)**, robust across treatment type, population and method of allegiance assessment. It is a **causal** bias, not a reflection of real efficacy differences: restricting analysis to 29 head-to-head comparisons of trauma-focused therapies of genuinely equivalent efficacy — where the "true efficacy" explanation predicts exactly zero — allegiance still predicted outcome and explained **12% of the variance**. It operates most strongly where methodological quality is low, and is partly mediated by differences in the *conceptual quality* of the compared treatments: the preferred therapy tends to be the better-specified one.

For calibration of what an uncontrolled allegiance effect can do, look outside this disorder. In obsessive-compulsive disorder, the 28 of 36 exposure-based CBT trials with suspected allegiance reported **g = 0.95 (0.69–1.2)**; the 8 without reported **g = 0.02 (−0.29 to 0.33)** — no efficacy at all. In hypochondriasis, 11 of 13 trials carried high allegiance risk and CBT's effect halved from **g = −1.32 (−1.75 to −0.90)** against waitlist to **−0.58 (−0.95 to −0.22)** against placebo controls.

Now the specific test. A meta-analytic review of CBT against *bona fide* alternative therapies across 26 studies and 1,981 patients, with allegiance obtained by contacting principal investigators directly, found **CBT superior to psychodynamic therapy at post-treatment and at follow-up, but NOT superior to interpersonal or supportive therapies**. Allegiance correlated with the size of CBT's advantage, **but the advantage remained significant after allegiance was controlled**, and methodological strength was unrelated to it.

Social anxiety disorder is, unusually, a literature in which allegiance controls were actually built in: the pivotal psychodynamic trial separated modalities across clinics with dual-modality investigators at every site; the transfer trial **measured** therapist allegiance and found no difference between arms (p = .108) — and the manual still failed; the canonical group-CBT trial ran a cross-site allegiance test finding "few differences between sites"; the cognitive-therapy-versus-IPT trial found no treatment × site interaction. **The CBT advantage here survives allegiance control, attenuated but present.** A bias of r ≈ .26 sits on top of a real but smaller difference — which is exactly why the placebo-controlled estimate for individual CBT (−0.56) is so much less impressive than the waitlist-controlled one (−1.19).

The two published networks disagree about where psychodynamic therapy sits, and the disagreement is mechanical. The anchor network places it at **−0.62 versus waitlist, level with psychological placebo**; a later Bayesian network of 92 studies places it at **SMD 0.98 (95% CrI 0.61–1.36)**, calling it "the most effective non-CBT treatment", with Clark and Wells cognitive therapy at 1.42 (1.14–1.70). They differ in inclusion — the later network has been criticised for omitting eligible psychodynamic RCTs — and, decisively, in **node structure**: only the earlier one models a **psychological placebo node** at all. A treatment ranked against waitlist and a treatment ranked against a credible placebo are not measuring the same quantity, and the second is the one that decides clinical choices.

### 9.8 What to offer when cognitive therapy is declined or fails

**First, verify what failed.** Manual-faithful delivery in routine practice produced 9% remission where trial delivery produced 26–36%, so a "CBT failure" is often a delivery failure. Establish that the patient received a genuine individual protocol at adequate dose before switching modality.

**If the patient declines cognitive therapy**, manualized short-term psychodynamic psychotherapy is the best-evidenced alternative and a legitimate offer: 26% versus 9% masked-rated remission and 52% versus 15% response against waitlist in the largest trial in the field, equal to CBT at two years, dropout no different from active comparators (logOR 0.10, −0.34 to 0.54), and guideline-recognised as third-line behind individual CBT and behind an SSRI or CBT-based supported self-help. State the trade honestly: roughly ten percentage points of acute remission and about double the cost per remission, for a treatment the patient will actually accept. An accepted third-line treatment outperforms a refused first-line one.

**Prefer guided internet-delivered psychodynamic protocols where available** (guided versus waitlist **d = 1.07**; unguided **d = 0.61**; guided over unguided **d = 0.46**) — the strongest psychodynamic effect sizes in the disorder, though also waitlist-anchored.

**Do not select IPT for social anxiety disorder.** It lost 42.1% versus 65.8% at post-treatment and 31.6% versus 68.4% at one year, tied with supportive therapy, and belongs to the one class in the anchor network that did not separate from waitlist.

**Do not treat supportive therapy as doing nothing.** Credible supportive contact delivers about −0.63 against waitlist — the same as psychodynamic therapy in that network, and more than half of individual CBT's effect. For a patient who will not engage with a structured protocol it is a defensible holding treatment, provided it is named as such and reviewed against a symptom measure rather than allowed to run indefinitely.

**Finally, calibrate.** Even the best arm of the best trial in this disorder remitted 36% of patients acutely. Nothing here changes that. The choice among these therapies moves outcomes by roughly ten percentage points; whether the patient engages at all moves them by fifty.

## 10. Internet-delivered, virtual-reality, brief and stepped-care formats

Every format in this chapter trades something for reach — therapist time, session count, or physical presence — and the trade is never free. An efficacy number without its matching engagement or completion number tells only half the story here: a format nobody finishes is not, at population level, an effective format at all.

### 10.1 Internet-delivered CBT: programmes, modules, and the anchor effect sizes

The best-studied internet programme is a direct online transcription of the Clark and Wells model: video-based safety-behaviour demonstrations, attention-training exercises, a virtual-audience presentation-practice tool, and structured behavioural-experiment worksheets across eight core modules plus additional released modules, with weekly asynchronous therapist feedback by secure message and scheduled phone or video contact. A parallel Australian programme family (the Shyness series) uses a simpler six-lesson structure of psychoeducation, worksheets, a moderated forum, and therapist e-mail contact. Both architectures recur, with local variation, across the field.

Across mood and anxiety disorders together, computerised CBT (64 trials) achieves mean **g = 0.80, NNT 2.34**, low risk of bias, with internet delivery, face-to-face delivery, and bibliotherapy rated "equally beneficial" in direct trials — though no comparisons exist against medication. A Swedish national HTA (52 RCTs) rated internet CBT vs waitlist for social phobia moderate-quality at **d = 0.85 (95% CI 0.66–1.05)**, but rated evidence for non-inferiority, adverse events, and cost-effectiveness low or very low, noting 88% of trials came from just Sweden and Australia. Within SAD specifically, the most recent psychotherapy meta-analysis (66 RCTs, n = 5,560) found overall **g = 0.88 (0.76–1.00)**, NNT 3.8, with delivery format a significant meta-regression moderator. A remote-CBT-for-SAD meta-analysis (31 studies, n = 2,905) found pooled within-group pre-post **g = 1.06 (0.96–1.16)**; by modality, internet CBT **g = 1.08 (0.98–1.19)**, app-delivered **g = 1.19 (0.75–1.64)**, bibliotherapy **g = 0.79 (0.45–1.13)**; between-group, remote CBT scored **g = 0.87** vs passive control but only **g = 0.34 (0.14–0.54)** vs face-to-face CBT — numerically favouring the remote arm. An earlier technology-assisted meta-analysis (37 RCTs, n = 2,991) showed the control-dependent pattern that recurs through this chapter: internet CBT **g = 0.84 vs passive control, g = 0.38 vs active control**. A 2026 Bayesian network meta-analysis of 42 digital-intervention RCTs ranks internet CBT first-line among *digital* options, VR showing the largest effect for quality of life — a ranking among digital formats, not parity with face-to-face individual therapy.

Against this manual's anchor benchmarks, self-help sits closer to full therapy than intuition suggests: vs waitlist, individual CBT scored SMD **−1.19 (95% CrI −1.56 to −0.81)**, supported self-help **−0.86 (−1.36 to −0.36)**, unsupported self-help **−0.75 (−1.25 to −0.26)** — both trailing SSRIs/SNRIs at −0.91 by only a modest margin. The gap is real but modest; it is guidance, not internet delivery per se, that closes most of the remainder.

### 10.2 Guided versus unguided: the central finding

The cleanest number comes from youth. A 2025 meta-analysis of digital interventions for social anxiety in children/adolescents/young adults (21 studies pooled, n = 4,196) found overall **g = 0.508 (0.308–0.707)**, NNT 3.6; the guidance split was decisive — **guided g = 0.825 (0.425–1.224), NNT 2.3 vs unguided g = 0.271 (0.083–0.458), NNT 6.6** (p = .01). SAD-specific content likewise beat transdiagnostic content (g = 0.878 vs 0.195). Durability collapsed with time: 3–6-month g = 0.378, falling to non-significant **g = 0.064 (−0.105 to 0.233) at 12 months**; adherence was reported too inconsistently to pool at all. A meta-review of human support across digital mental health (31 meta-analyses) found 22 of 45 pooled effect sizes significantly favoured support, only 4 favoured unsupported delivery, with support mattering most at higher severity.

Adult RCTs, individually underpowered, split rather than replicate this. A three-arm trial (n = 98, guided vs self-guided computerised CBT vs waitlist) found **77% of guided vs 33% of unguided** completed all six lessons, guided-vs-unguided ES **0.66**, unguided-vs-control ES **0.38 (ns)**. But a ten-week trial contrasting unguided, weekly e-mail guidance, and on-demand support (n = 81) found within-group **d = 1.47** with essentially no between-group separation (d = .00–.36) and no difference in dropout or adherence. A scheduled-vs-on-demand-support trial (n = 99) likewise found **d = 0.02 (−0.37 to 0.43)**. The best-powered recent test — an app-based cognitive-therapy trial (n = 164, DSM-diagnosed, guided app + 8 videoconference sessions vs unguided app vs waitlist, clinician-rated LSAS, 6-month follow-up) — found near-identical efficacy (unguided d = −1.01, guided d = −1.10, **guided-vs-unguided d = 0.04, p = .830**) but starkly different completion: **dropout 9.3% waitlist, 42.6% unguided, 15% guided (χ² = 19.5, p < .01)**. The authors' own reading is the one worth remembering clinically: guidance's value was not making treatment work better for completers, but that "it prevents drop-out." A predictor analysis (n = 245) found baseline symptom intensity, not depression, predicted outcome in both arms, and within the unguided group specifically, higher treatment-credibility ratings predicted greater adherence.

Pooled adherence sharpens without resolving this: across psychiatric disorders (11 RCTs, n = 1,272), adherence ran 70.06% for internet CBT vs 86.68% face-to-face, and within internet CBT, **79.09% guided vs 48.17% self-guided**. An individual-participant-data component network meta-analysis of internet CBT for depression found combining human and automated encouragement cut dropout, OR **0.32 (95% CrI 0.13–0.93)** — the guidance effect may need only active contact, not full therapist time.

What guidance costs to buy is quantifiable. A UK head-to-head trial (below) achieved its full symptom drop for **6.45 therapist-hours** against **15.8 hours** for an equivalent face-to-face course. Comparable figures recur across the literature: a Swedish non-inferiority trial used **189.60 minutes (SD 53.77)** of psychologist time against **499.78 minutes** for group therapy; a development pilot used 20% of face-to-face therapist time; two Australian six-lesson trials logged 127 and 46 minutes of total contact per patient; youth stepped-care trials logged 774 and 175 minutes against 897-minute single-programme comparators.

### 10.3 iCBT against face-to-face and group therapy

Earlier internet-vs-face-to-face SAD trials compared internet delivery against face-to-face *group* CBT — a comparator rated less cost-effective than individual therapy — used different therapists per arm, never assessed face-to-face treatment competence or alliance, and never included a behaviour test. A UK trial fixed all four: 102 adults with DSM-IV SAD (18.4-year mean illness duration, 81% comorbid personality disorder) were randomised to internet cognitive therapy on the Clark and Wells protocol, face-to-face cognitive therapy on the identical protocol, or a 14-week waitlist. Both treatments dramatically beat waitlist (post-treatment **d = 2.20 internet, d = 2.38 face-to-face**); the two active formats did not differ on the primary composite outcome at any timepoint (**d range 0.22–0.31**), and equivalence testing rejected internet delivery being more than 25% less effective than face-to-face. The trial was properly powered for this test: 50 patients per arm gave 82% power to detect d = 0.45 between formats, against 99% power vs waitlist. Diagnostic loss ran 72–91%; there were **zero drop-outs** in either active arm; mediation analysis found the same cognitive-model process variables accounted for roughly **60% of improvement in both formats**. Face-to-face edged ahead only on a behaviour-test self-report (confounded by venue familiarity) and post-treatment depression (not sustained past 3 months).

An earlier Swedish trial tested the same question as formal non-inferiority against face-to-face *group* therapy: 126 patients, 15 weeks, pre-specified margin requiring the LSAS-difference CI to stay above −10 points. At 6-month follow-up, **64% (41/64) of internet-arm patients were responders vs 45% (28/62) of the group arm**, both CIs comfortably inside the margin and numerically favouring internet delivery. A 4-year societal-cost follow-up of the same cohort found both arms improved d = 1.34–1.48 from baseline, stayed within the margin, and were similarly cost-effective, with treatment costs offset by societal savings. Pooled across 11 long-term-effectiveness studies (multiple disorders, not SAD alone), the durability gap was **g = −0.07 (−0.36 to 0.21)** — negligible. A technique-level dismantling trial of video feedback found the self-perception shift numerically larger with face-to-face delivery but no format difference in downstream symptom impact.

Cross-cultural transport has been tested directly. A Hong Kong RCT (n = 44) found adjusted LSAS difference **55.36 points (44.32–66.39), d = 2.41**, **86% vs 5% LSAS remission**, gains held at 3 months. A single-arm Japanese trial (n = 15, 14 weeks plus 3-month booster) found within-group **d = 3.66**, 80% reliably improved, 60% remission, 7% dropout, 94% module completion — a confirmatory RCT is underway.

### 10.4 Adherence, durability, and cost-effectiveness in routine care

Effectiveness cohorts test whether internet CBT works once delivered at scale. A 654-patient outpatient cohort found post-treatment **LSAS-SR d = 0.86 (99% CI 0.74–0.98)**, rising to **d = 1.15 at 6 months**, with adherence and clinic experience both positively associated with outcome. A 446-patient long-term cohort (1–4 years) found improvement continuing from d = 0.8 to **d = 1.2**, with higher baseline severity predicting *faster* improvement — directly rebutting the claim that internet delivery suits only mild cases. A 764-patient predictor analysis found treatment credibility the strongest predictor of adherence and improvement, and — worth flagging for service design — that *more* therapist time per module was **negatively** associated with adherence. A five-year follow-up (n = 80, 89% interview retention) found LSAS-SR falling from 71.3 to **40.3**, d 1.30–1.40, one-year gains unchanged five years out. Two boundary findings temper this picture: internet CBT as add-on to treatment-as-usual shows only low-certainty small depression benefit (g = −0.23) with **no SAD-specific add-on evidence**; and quality-of-life gains across depression/anxiety are modest (overall g = 0.35, only the physical-health domain significant) — symptomatic remission does not automatically buy broader functioning.

Cost-effectiveness data are more favourable than the HTA's "low/very low quality" rating for the domain overall implies. The Swedish cost-minimisation analysis found **€400–654 per patient for internet CBT vs €699–1,134 for group CBT** depending on clinic capacity. A cost-utility analysis of unguided internet self-help for students found incremental QALY **+0.046 (0.024–0.068)** at incremental cost **−€211** over six months, driven largely by reduced work presenteeism exceeding the intervention's own price; from a societal perspective the intervention *dominated* waitlist (93% cost-effective at €0/QALY), though the healthcare-only ICER was €348 per additional symptom-free case, and switching the QALY instrument made the gain non-significant — verdicts here are not robust to instrument choice. The NICE decision-analytic model (28 interventions) ranked individual cognitive therapy most cost-effective, then generic individual CBT, then phenelzine, then **book-based self-help without support** — ahead of most drugs and group therapy, though drug side-effects were not priced in. A five-year Hong Kong Markov model found guided internet CBT dominant over face-to-face CBT (2.9956 QALYs at US$6,101 vs 2.9917 at US$6,246), 90.9% cost-effective at three times local GDP per capita.

### 10.5 Virtual reality exposure therapy

Published protocols run 5–14 sessions, most weekly; one meta-analysis sets its inclusion floor at ≥3 sessions. Sixteen of seventeen trials in one review used head-mounted displays; several of the largest pragmatic trials instead used 360°-video. Per-session exposure duration is inconsistently reported — one review explicitly calls for the field to establish it as a basic parameter.

VRET performs well against passive comparators and considerably less well against active ones — the same control-dependence seen for internet CBT, sharper here. A SAD-specific meta-analysis (22 studies, n = 703) found g = **−0.86 (−1.04 to −0.68) at post**, deepening to **−1.14 at 6 months**, then attenuating to **−0.74 at 12 months**; VRET matched in-vivo exposure at post but was **inferior at later follow-up**, dropout risk equivalent. A cross-disorder meta-analysis (30 RCTs, n = 1,057, 8 SAD) found **g = 0.90 vs waitlist, g = 0.78 vs psychological placebo, g = −0.07 (ns) vs in-vivo exposure**, with larger trials yielding smaller effects — a small-study-effect warning. A SAD-specific meta-analysis (15 RCTs, n = 720) found VR beat waitlist on fear/avoidance at **g = −1.170 (−2.056 to −0.283)** but found **no difference vs CBT**; critically, VR's apparent superiority over control concentrated in trials with **<50 participants and ≤5 sessions** — the signature of an underpowered small-trial effect, not a robust advantage. The tightest test — matching VR and in-vivo for *equivalent exposure time* — found overall **g = −0.20 (ns)** but a **significant effect favouring in-vivo** within the social-phobia subset specifically. A broader review found g = 0.33 (ns) vs non-VR/waitlist pooled but **g = 1.29 (0.68–1.90) vs passive controls alone** — the gap is a function of comparator, not of VR itself. A meta-review of 11 VR-psychiatry meta-analyses concluded medium-to-large effects vs inactive controls but **no significant difference vs standard evidence-based approaches**.

Head-to-head trials sharpen without resolving this. A three-arm trial embedding VR exposure in individual CBT (n = 17) vs in-vivo exposure in individual CBT (n = 22) vs waitlist (n = 20), 14 weekly sessions, found both active arms beat waitlist, and — the clearest positive VR-over-in-vivo finding in this literature — **VR was more effective at post-treatment on the LSAS**, held at 6 months, and rated more practical for therapists. But a smaller trial testing *stand-alone* VRET (virtual social interaction, no cognitive-therapy structure) against in-vivo exposure and waitlist (n = 60) found the reverse: in-vivo additionally beat waitlist on fear of negative evaluation, speech performance, and quality of life where VRET did not, and beat VRET on social anxiety symptoms at post and follow-up; only perceived stress remained significant for VRET at follow-up. Read together, VR appears to add value embedded within full cognitive therapy and to lose it delivered as exposure alone. The largest pragmatic test shows what happens when execution fails ambition: a five-site Danish trial of group CBT with VR vs in-vivo exposure for SAD/agoraphobia, planned at n = 302, randomised only 177; both arms improved with **no significant between-group difference** at post (d = −0.026) or 1 year (d = 0.097), and the authors' own verdict — "due to insufficient recruitment and substantial missing data, no definitive conclusions can be drawn" — should temper enthusiasm generalised from smaller trials.

### 10.6 Smartphone apps, chatbots, and cognitive bias modification

The first dedicated mobile-app trial (n = 150, three arms, 12 weeks) found **PC vs waitlist d = 0.74 (NNT 3.33), app vs waitlist d = 0.89 (NNT 6.00), app vs PC d = 0.07 (ns)**, gains held at 3 months; app usage was more evenly spread through the day. This app-desktop parity recurs in the pooled estimate for application-delivered CBT (**g = 1.19, 95% CI 0.75–1.64**) — a large point estimate on the widest CI of any modality here, from few and small trials. Brief ecological-momentary-intervention apps show both what this format can do and where it fails. An imaginal-exposure app used multiple times daily for one week (n = 82) produced significantly greater reductions than self-monitoring at post and 1 month, with a dose–response relationship to completed exercises. Against that, a 14-day mindfulness EMI app (n = 191, self-reported rather than diagnosed SAD) found **no between-group effects on SAD fear/avoidance, worry, depression, or trait mindfulness (d = −0.13 to 0.15)** despite within-group momentary change — a candidly negative trial worth stating plainly.

Chatbot interventions, meta-analysed across mental-health problems broadly (32 RCTs, n = 6,089), reach specific anxiety symptoms **g = 0.47 (95% CI 0.07–0.86)** — a CI nearly touching zero — with long-term effects **not significant for most outcomes**. **No identified RCT has tested a chatbot in diagnosed SAD**: this figure is borrowed evidence from a broader anxiety category and should be presented as such. Cognitive bias modification has not held up in SAD: attention-bias-modification arms were no better than passive control except delivered in-lab (g = 0.35), and the Swedish HTA rated internet ABM vs sham moderate-quality evidence of **no effect (d = −0.04)** — a clean negative that should not survive by omission.

### 10.7 Bibliotherapy and pure self-help

Two well-designed trials of pure self-help reach opposite headlines, and the disagreement is mechanically explicable. The more definitive randomised 224 patients with **severe generalised** social phobia to pure self-help, self-help augmented by five group sessions, waitlist, or full group therapy: pure self-help beat waitlist on diagnostic status immediately post-intervention but the advantage faded over 3 months, and **symptom severity did not differ from waitlist at any point** — the trial's own conclusion states "pure self-help shows limited efficacy." The augmented arm beat waitlist on every measure and matched full group therapy. A separate trial (n = 235) found the opposite headline: pure bibliotherapy beat waitlist on all outcomes, gains "well maintained a year later," and bibliotherapy augmented with online group discussion matched a fully guided internet programme. The likely mechanical driver is severity: the negative trial deliberately recruited a severe generalised population, precisely where the guided-vs-unguided literature above shows support mattering most, while the positive trial's population was not selected for severity and its augmentation used low-cost online discussion. A small self-help-book trial (n = 21, minimal therapist contact) held benefit to 3 months but the authors position it for mild-to-moderate presentations only. The meta-analytic estimate for bibliotherapy-delivered CBT (**g = 0.79, 0.45–1.13**) is the smallest of the three remote modalities compared earlier — consistent with this severity-dependent picture. Economically, book-based self-help without support still ranks fourth in the NICE model, ahead of most drugs and group therapy — a defensible low-severity option even where its stand-alone clinical case is the weakest in this chapter.

### 10.8 Brief, massed, and single-session formats

This is the thinnest evidence base here, and saying so plainly matters. **No SAD-specific concentrated or massed-format trial exists** — the intensive formats transforming access in OCD, panic, and PTSD have not been tested in SAD; an 8-day intensive PTSD programme achieved within-group **d = 1.38–1.52 with zero dropouts**, but its relevance to SAD is by analogy only. Single-session interventions have been tested in adolescent anxiety broadly, again not SAD-specific, and the results argue against overselling: a four-armed cluster-randomised school trial of a ~45-minute online growth-mindset intervention (n = 731) found anxiety fell in **all four arms including the active comparator**, with no arm separating from control, and a second such trial (n = 104) found intention-to-treat **d = 0.07 (−0.32 to 0.47)** — essentially null. The closest analogue for brief guided self-help substituting for full therapy comes from PTSD: a pragmatic non-inferiority trial (n = 196, ≤3 hours face-to-face contact vs up to twelve 60–90-minute sessions) demonstrated non-inferiority at 16 weeks but was **inconclusive at 52 weeks**; delivery cost **£277 vs £729**, though incremental QALYs (−0.04) meant it was cheaper, not more cost-effective. Whether this transfers to SAD specifically has not been tested.

### 10.9 Stepped care, task-sharing, and blended care

The anchor stepped-care trial randomised 173 SAD/panic patients across nine clinics to a three-step model (psychoeducation → guided internet CBT → face-to-face CBT) vs immediate face-to-face CBT. ITT recovery was **40.0% stepped care vs 43.2% direct face-to-face — not significantly different**; of stepped-care recoveries, **76.5% occurred at the two less therapist-demanding steps**. The headline caution: **attrition ran 41.2% stepped care vs 27.3% direct face-to-face** — a pathway that loses patients between steps has not delivered the access gain it promises them. Comorbid cluster-C personality disorder and lower functioning predicted worse outcome regardless of intensity; patients **without** comorbid depression did better started immediately on face-to-face CBT than on guided self-help, while those **with** did equally well either way. A youth trial (n = 281) testing an analogous stepped model against a single best-practice programme found **no significant outcome differences** at 774 vs 897 therapist-minutes, the first two steps returning the strongest gains. A companion trial found pure low-intensity delivery reaching **49% anxiety-disorder remission vs 66% for face-to-face**, at 175 vs 897 minutes and an A$735 cost difference. A boundary condition from OCD (n = 473): guided self-help's advantage over waitlist did not reach the pre-specified threshold, but early low-intensity access **reduced uptake of subsequent therapist-led CBT from 86% to 57–62%** without compromising long-term outcomes — a stepped structure can succeed by reducing demand even where its head-to-head efficacy case is weak.

SAD-specific LMIC evidence is essentially absent, but the transdiagnostic digital-mental-health literature from LMIC settings is substantial: an 80-study meta-analysis (n = 12,070) found **depression g = −0.61, anxiety g = −0.73**, GRADE-rated high; a 21-study meta-analysis (n = 5,296) found post-test **g = −0.89, NNT 2.91**, anxiety **g = −1.02** — and, contrasting the high-income literature above, **no significant guided-vs-unguided difference** in this LMIC sample. The WHO's Problem Management Plus/Step-by-Step programmes (23 RCTs, n = 5,298) showed distress SMD **−0.45**, positive mental health SMD **0.31**, both very-low-certainty. A New Delhi school pilot of task-shared, non-specialist-delivered problem-solving found a six-week guided-self-help schedule **not feasible**, while a compressed three-to-four-week, less-experienced-counsellor version maintained outcomes with better feasibility. Two large registered trials (N = 1,500 in Colombia/Mexico; N = 720 in the UK) will be the strongest tests yet of whether the high-income guidance premium generalises.

Blended care — digital content interleaved with, not substituting for, face-to-face sessions — shows mean uptake **91%** and adherence **81%** across 29 studies, well above unguided digital-alone completion. Depression outcomes were strong (d = **−1.1**) but anxiety outcomes were not (d = **−0.1, ns**); within anxiety, larger effects tracked a **supplementary** digital role, **≤6** face-to-face sessions, and a face-to-face-to-digital ratio **≤50%** — lean, session-light models outperform content-heavy ones. A direct SAD-relevant blended-vs-face-to-face RCT (n = 114, 15 weeks, 1-year follow-up) found no significant differences in acceptability or effectiveness (post d = 0.15; 1-year d = −0.38) and near-identical modelled costs, though the authors flag their small sample. The Mindable app trial above is itself, in substance, a blended-care finding: its guided arm's value lay entirely in retention, not efficacy.

Several registered trials should move these conclusions within two to three years: a Hong Kong non-inferiority trial (**NCT05292612**, n = 110, readout 2026-09) tests whether coach guidance can substitute for clinician guidance; a Canadian three-arm trial (**NCT06403995**, n = 252, readout 2026-10) compares undirected against directed internet CBT; a Swedish trial (**NCT06942429**, n = 556, readout 2029-11) runs a pragmatic non-inferiority test of stepped vs stratified youth-anxiety care; a phase-4 trial (**NCT07192367**, n = 56, readout 2026-08) compares VRET directly against sertraline; and a planned Pakistani trial (**NCT06562010**, n = 800) would be the first SAD-specific internet-therapy trial in an LMIC setting, though its registry record currently shows internal inconsistencies and should be treated with caution.

## 11. Medication alongside psychotherapy: combination, sequencing and augmentation

Ask a clinician running individual cognitive therapy for social anxiety disorder whether to add a medication, and the honest answer is: it depends which trial is read. Four placebo-controlled combination trials in adult social anxiety disorder have asked essentially the same question — does adding an antidepressant to structured psychotherapy beat either treatment alone — and have returned four different answers: combination clearly superior, combination adding nothing, combination measurably worse at one-year follow-up, and combination that looked fine acutely before reversing once the drug was stopped. This is not noise to average away. Read side by side, the four trials show what actually drove each result, and a genuinely useful clinical pattern falls out of the disagreement rather than being obscured by it.

### 11.1 Four combination trials, four different answers

#### 11.1.1 When combination wins: phenelzine, group CBT, and their combination

A two-site, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial randomised 128 patients (intention-to-treat arms: placebo 27, cognitive-behavioural group therapy [CBGT] 34, phenelzine 35, combined treatment 32) to four conditions and tracked them for 24 weeks. The ordering of effects was monotonic and statistically confirmed (Williams test = 4.97, p < 0.01). At week 12, CGI response rates were placebo 33.3%, CBGT 47.1%, phenelzine 54.3%, combined 71.9% (χ²₁ = 8.76, p < 0.01); remission rates (CGI = 1) were 7.4%, 8.8%, 22.9% and 46.9% respectively (χ²₁ = 15.92, p < 0.01). By week 24 the gap had widened further: response 33.3%, 52.9%, 48.6%, 78.1% (χ²₁ = 12.02, p = 0.001); remission 14.8%, 23.5%, 25.7%, 53.1% (χ²₁ = 10.72, p = 0.001). Combined treatment beat both active monotherapies and placebo at both timepoints, with roughly double the remission rate of either monotherapy alone by week 24. This is the strongest positive combination result in adult social anxiety disorder — but it used an MAOI, not an SSRI, and a group rather than individual psychotherapy format.

#### 11.1.2 When combination adds nothing: fluoxetine and comprehensive group CBT

A five-arm trial screened 722 patients with generalised social phobia and randomised 295 to 14 weeks of fluoxetine alone, comprehensive CBGT (CCBT) alone, CCBT plus fluoxetine, CCBT plus pill placebo, or pill placebo alone, with a blinded independent evaluator rating the Brief Social Phobia Scale and CGI as primary outcomes. CGI response rates (intention-to-treat) were fluoxetine 50.9%, CCBT 51.7%, CCBT+fluoxetine 54.2%, CCBT+placebo 50.8%, and placebo 31.7%. Every active arm beat placebo, but **no active arm differed from any other** — the trial's own conclusion was that "combined treatment did not yield any further advantage." A secondary finding sharpens the null result: fluoxetine and CCBT alone were each *faster* than either combination arm at week 4, meaning the combination bought nothing and may even have slowed early response. This trial used an SSRI, an individual-format-adjacent comprehensive group CBT protocol, and a much larger, better-powered sample than the phenelzine trial.

#### 11.1.3 When combination is worse: paroxetine and cognitive therapy at 12-month follow-up

A four-arm trial randomised 102 patients — 54% of whom also met criteria for avoidant personality disorder, making this an unusually severe sample — to paroxetine, individual cognitive therapy (CT) on the Clark and Wells model, CT plus paroxetine, or pill placebo, with medication continued for 26 weeks. At post-treatment, CT was superior to paroxetine alone and to placebo, but was **not** superior to the combination — an apparent argument for adding the drug. That argument collapsed at 12-month follow-up: CT alone maintained its gains and remained significantly better than both placebo and paroxetine, while the combination, paroxetine and placebo arms no longer differed from one another. Twelve-month recovery rates were CT 68%, combination 40%, paroxetine 24%, pill placebo 4%. The trial's own conclusion states it plainly: "combined treatment provided no advantage over single treatments; rather there was less effect of the combined treatment compared to CT alone." In an SAD population half of whom carried a comorbid personality disorder, adding an SSRI to cognitive therapy did not merely fail to help — it measurably degraded the durable outcome.

#### 11.1.4 When medication's early edge reverses: the general-practice sertraline trial

A pragmatic general-practice trial in generalised social phobia randomised 387 patients across a 2×2 design — sertraline 50–150 mg versus placebo, each with or without GP-delivered exposure therapy — over 24 weeks, double-blind. At the acute endpoint, sertraline outperformed non-sertraline (χ² = 12.53, p < 0.001, OR 0.534, 95% CI 0.347–0.835), while exposure added on top of medication did **not** significantly outperform non-exposure (χ² = 2.18, p = 0.140, OR 0.732, 95% CI 0.475–1.134); pairwise, both combined treatment and sertraline alone beat placebo. Read in isolation this looks like a straightforward medication win. The follow-up, conducted a year after randomisation and 28 weeks after medication had been stopped in 328 of the original 375 evaluable patients, reversed it. Exposure alone kept improving after treatment ended — CGI–Social Phobia severity changed by a further 0.45 points (95% CI 0.16–0.65, p < 0.01), against 0.25 points for placebo (95% CI 0.00–0.48, p < 0.05) — while both sertraline-containing arms (sertraline alone and sertraline plus exposure) showed **significant deterioration on the SF-36 functioning measure relative to exposure alone**. In other words, the arm that looked worst acutely (exposure without medication) was the only one still getting better a year later, and the arms built around the drug that had looked best acutely were the ones that got worse once it was withdrawn.

#### 11.1.5 Reconciling the four — the disagreement is mechanical, not mysterious

These four trials do not describe the same intervention. They differ in the drug (an MAOI in the winning trial, SSRIs in the other three), in the psychotherapy protocol (group CBGT, comprehensive group CBT, individual disorder-specific cognitive therapy, and unstructured GP-delivered exposure — four different things, only one of which is the individual Clark and Wells protocol this manual otherwise treats as the reference standard), in the population (a general specialist-referral sample, a broad multi-site trial, an avoidant-personality-enriched sample, and an unselected general-practice population), and — this is the variable that actually explains the contradiction — in **when medication was stopped relative to the assessment**. The positive trial measured outcomes at 12 and 24 weeks, both on-drug. The null trial measured a 14-week acute endpoint, also on-drug. The negative trial and the reversing trial both measured a follow-up **after** the medication phase had ended, and in both, the medication-containing arms lost their advantage or actively deteriorated relative to psychotherapy alone. The honest summary for a clinician is not "combination sometimes works and sometimes doesn't" but: **acute-phase combination trials tend to show either a benefit or no penalty from adding medication; every trial in this literature that followed patients past medication discontinuation showed the psychotherapy-alone arm holding or extending its gains while the medication-exposed arms lost ground.** That asymmetry, not average effect size, is the finding to carry into practice.

### 11.2 The cross-disorder meta-analytic picture — and social anxiety disorder's conspicuous absence from it

Zooming out from social anxiety disorder specifically, a meta-analysis of 52 studies and 3,623 patients (32 in depression, 21 across anxiety disorders) examined adding psychotherapy to antidepressant medication and found a real, sustained benefit: Hedges' g = 0.43 (95% CI 0.31–0.56), number needed to treat 4.20, an effect that held up to two years of follow-up, with the contributions of drug and therapy behaving as largely independent and additive. The authors were explicit about the diagnostic boundaries of that conclusion: "sufficient evidence that combined treatment is superior" existed for major depression, panic disorder, and obsessive-compulsive disorder. **Social anxiety disorder is not on that list.** This is not an oversight; it reflects exactly the four-trial picture above — a literature too small and too internally contradictory to support a general "add psychotherapy to medication" or "add medication to psychotherapy" recommendation for this specific disorder, even though the analogous recommendation is well supported for several neighbouring conditions. Guideline bodies do not always track this distinction: the WFSBP's primary-care short-form guideline states that "a combination of medication and cognitive behavior/exposure therapy was shown to be a clinically desired treatment strategy" for anxiety disorders generally — a position the SAD-specific combination trials, taken together, do not support. Where a general anxiety-disorder guideline and the disorder-specific trial evidence disagree, the disagreement is mechanical: the guideline synthesises across panic disorder and GAD, where combination evidence is more favourable, and that synthesis does not sit well imported wholesale into social anxiety disorder.

### 11.3 The anchor network's combination estimate is not solid ground to build on

The anchor network meta-analysis itself does report a combination class, and on paper it looks like the best treatment in the whole network: combined psychological-plus-pharmacological treatment scored SMD −1.30 (95% CrI −1.73 to −0.88) against waitlist, nominally beating even individual cognitive therapy's −1.19. This number should not be used to justify combination as a default. It is built from **five different drug-plus-therapy combinations, each studied in exactly one trial, contributing 156 participants in total** to the whole class estimate — a fraction of the 3,312 participants underpinning the psychological-intervention classes or the 4,043 underpinning SSRIs/SNRIs alone. The network's own authors qualify the finding directly: "there was no evidence that combined interventions had greater effects than the leading monotherapies." A class estimate resting on five single-trial combinations, none replicated, is not a basis for a first-line recommendation — it is a placeholder for research that has not yet been done at scale.

### 11.4 Pharmacological augmentation of exposure: the d-cycloserine story

D-cycloserine (DCS), a partial NMDA-receptor agonist given as a single oral dose shortly before an exposure session on the theory that it facilitates the fear-extinction learning exposure depends on, is the most extensively tested augmentation strategy in this literature — and its evidentiary arc has a shape clinicians should recognise: **an effect that shrinks as the trials improve.**

#### 11.4.1 The initial promise

The first two placebo-controlled trials in social anxiety disorder specifically reported real signal. A pilot of 27 patients with public-speaking-focused SAD, given 50 mg DCS or placebo one hour before each of four videotaped exposure sessions, found significantly less social anxiety in the DCS group with controlled effect sizes in the medium-to-large range. A larger replication in 56 patients with primary SAD found DCS superior on symptom severity, dysfunctional cognitions and life impairment, with effect sizes "mostly in the medium range," and produced the first evidence that DCS moderates the relationship between reduced negative self-appraisal and overall improvement. A pooled early meta-analysis of nine trials and 273 subjects across anxiety and OCD populations found SMD 0.46 (95% CI 0.15–0.77, z = 2.89, p = 0.004), with no evidence of publication bias.

#### 11.4.2 The correction

As the evidence base grew, the effect did not hold. A Cochrane review pooling 21 published RCTs and 788 outpatients across OCD, PTSD, SAD, and specific and panic disorders — five of the trials specifically in social anxiety disorder — found no evidence of a difference: adult responders at endpoint, risk ratio 1.10 (95% CI 0.89–1.34, 9 studies, n = 449, graded LOW quality); at 1–12 month follow-up, RR 1.08 (0.90–1.31). A Bayesian multilevel meta-analysis of 23 studies and 1,314 patients with anxiety disorders and OCD returned an overall effect "almost indistinguishable from zero" — g = −0.12 (95% CI −0.27 to 0.02) — while noting that effects were slightly larger specifically in socially anxious patients. Crucially, **study quality and year of publication were significant moderators, and the direction was that higher-quality, more recent studies reported smaller effects** — the signature of a true effect fading as methods tighten, not of noise. A separate 2017 meta-analysis restricted to 20 anxiety-disorder RCTs and 957 participants found overall acute efficacy g = 0.15 (small, non-significant) but, when isolating anxiety disorders specifically from OCD and PTSD, a moderate and significant g = 0.33 (p = 0.03) — a partial exception worth naming, though still modest. The most rigorous synthesis available is an individual-participant-data meta-analysis that obtained raw data for 21 of 22 eligible double-blind trials, representing 1,047 of 1,073 eligible participants, and modelled outcomes on a common rank-transformed scale. Controlling for concurrent antidepressant use, DCS produced a pre-to-post mean difference of −3.62 (95% CI −6.43 to −0.81, p = 0.01, d = −0.25); the mid-treatment effect was not significant (d = −0.14); the follow-up effect was borderline (d = −0.19, p = 0.05). No prespecified patient- or study-level moderator, including antidepressant co-treatment, was associated with outcome. The paper's own verdict: "D-cycloserine is associated with a small augmentation effect on exposure-based therapy."

#### 11.4.3 Why the effect kept shrinking — and the failed rescue

A secondary dosing analysis of the same 1,047-participant individual-level dataset traced the apparent decline in efficacy over roughly 14 years of accumulating trials to three drifting practice variables: fewer doses per course, dosing later relative to the exposure session, and lower baseline severity in more recent samples — none of which were significant moderators in the placebo arm, confirming they are drug-specific rather than generic artefacts. More doses produced better outcomes, levelling off at nine doses; dosing more than 60 minutes before exposure was associated with better outcomes than dosing closer to the session. Modelled optimal administration was projected to increase the pre-to-follow-up effect size by roughly 50% — a meaningful relative gain, but 50% larger than an effect of d ≈ 0.2 is still small in absolute terms. A parallel line of work tested whether DCS's benefit depends on the exposure session actually succeeding: in 145 medication-free adults with generalised SAD receiving 50 mg DCS or placebo before five exposure sessions embedded in 12-session group CBT, DCS produced significantly greater improvement at the next session specifically when end-of-session fear had been *low*, and significantly *less* improvement than placebo when end-of-session fear had been *high* — DCS, on this account, amplifies whatever the session taught, for better or worse. This generated a clean, testable hypothesis: dose DCS only after sessions that go well, and skip it after sessions that end badly. A purpose-built four-arm trial of 152 adults with SAD (mean age 29.24 years, SD 10.16; 55.26% men; LSAS > 60 required for entry) tested pre-session DCS, post-session DCS, a tailored post-session condition (dosed only after low-fear sessions), and placebo across five sessions, with blinded evaluators rating severity to three-month follow-up. Both fixed-dosing conditions beat placebo substantially — presession b = −0.25 (95% CI −0.37 to −0.13, p < 0.001, d = 1.07), postsession b = −0.20 (−0.32 to −0.07, p = 0.002, d = 0.85), sustained to three months (presession d = 0.76, postsession d = 0.72) — and did not differ from each other. But **the tailored condition, the one built specifically on the success-dependence mechanism, showed no advantage over placebo at all**, and both fixed conditions outperformed it. The rescue hypothesis failed its own designed test. This single 152-patient trial's effect sizes (d ≈ 0.7–1.1) sit in sharp tension with the 1,047-participant individual-participant-data meta-analysis (d ≈ −0.25) and the 1,314-patient Bayesian meta-analysis (g = −0.12, crossing zero); the larger, pooled, methodologically more rigorous estimates are the ones to lead with, and this trial should be read as a discrepant recent result rather than as the current best answer.

#### 11.4.4 What to do with this in practice
