The trajectory across fifteen-plus years of trials — medium-to-large effects in small early pilots, shrinking to a small, borderline effect in the largest individual-participant-data synthesis, with quality and recency both predicting smaller effects, and a mechanistically-motivated rescue strategy that failed its own test — is the textbook signature of a true effect approaching zero rather than a robust one obscured by noisy small trials. D-cycloserine is not a routine augmentation strategy for treatment-resistant social anxiety disorder. If it is used at all, the dosing literature supports giving it across multiple sessions (benefit levelling off around nine doses) rather than a single dose, timing it more than 60 minutes before the exposure exercise rather than immediately before, and setting expectations with the patient at "small, uncertain benefit" rather than describing it as a proven accelerant.

### 11.5 Other augmentation strategies

Two other adjuncts have been tested with SAD-specific data, and neither displaces exposure-based psychotherapy as the active ingredient. Attention bias modification (ABM) — computerised training intended to redirect attentional bias away from threat cues — added to CBT was examined across 11 randomised studies and produced small but statistically significant effects on clinician-rated anxiety and on attentional bias itself relative to control conditions, but the effect was **non-significant** for self-reported or parent-reported anxiety and for depression; benefit was concentrated in protocols where ABM was woven into each CBT session rather than delivered at separate timepoints. The finding most relevant to this manual's population is a specific negative moderator: "older participants and patients with social anxiety disorder tended to benefit LESS from the combination of ABM and CBT," on bias-score outcomes specifically. ABM is, at best, a weak adjunct, and weaker still in social anxiety disorder than in anxiety disorders generally. Separately, sleep quality has emerged as a modifiable, non-pharmacological target worth attention alongside any augmentation decision: in 169 adults with generalised SAD receiving 12 weeks of group CBT (independent of DCS randomisation, which did not moderate either relationship), poorer baseline sleep quality predicted slower improvement and higher post-treatment severity, and patients who felt more rested the night after a session showed lower symptom severity at the *next* session, controlling for the prior session's score. Neither of these adjuncts substitutes for exposure-based work; both are refinements around its edges. A related, non-modifiable finding worth noting for expectation-setting: in a secondary analysis of the 152-patient dosing trial above, higher pre-treatment trait positive emotionality predicted faster social anxiety reduction and lower three-month severity independent of baseline depression — a candidate marker for who is likely to respond quickly to exposure therapy without any augmentation at all, though this is a predictor, not an intervention.

### 11.6 Practical sequencing guidance

#### 11.6.1 The patient who arrives already on an antidepressant and remains symptomatic

This is the most common real-world version of the combination question, and it has the single strongest randomised answer in this entire chapter. A prospective randomised open-blinded end-point trial in Japan enrolled adults with social anxiety disorder who **remained symptomatic despite antidepressant treatment** and randomised them to 16 weeks of CBT added to usual care (n = 21) or usual care alone (n = 21). The adjusted mean LSAS reduction was −40.87 in the CBT-plus-usual-care arm against +0.68 in usual care alone — patients left on medication with no psychotherapy added showed essentially zero further change. The between-group difference was −41.55 points (95% CI −53.68 to −29.42, p < 0.0001); response rates were 85.7% versus 10.0% (p < 0.0001); remission rates were 47.6% versus 0.0% (p = 0.0005). This is a far larger effect than any pharmacological augmentation strategy reviewed above, and it directly answers "the SSRI didn't work — now what": **add disorder-specific CBT, do not simply switch or augment the drug.** The trial is small (n = 42), open-label, single-country, and its comparator arm's near-total lack of spontaneous improvement inflates the contrast somewhat — but the direction and magnitude of effect are consistent with everything else in this chapter about individual cognitive therapy's specificity. Before labelling any case "treatment-resistant" on this basis, it is worth confirming what the first course actually consisted of: a Delphi consensus process among 36 multidisciplinary experts produced an operational, staged definition of treatment resistance in anxiety disorders precisely because "failed first-line treatment" is used loosely in practice; a course of generic supportive counselling or a non-specific group programme does not meet the bar that licenses escalation.

#### 11.6.2 Whether to add medication mid-therapy to a partial responder

The trial evidence argues against doing this reflexively. In the personality-disorder-enriched combination trial described above, cognitive therapy alone that had not yet reached remission at post-treatment was not improved by adding paroxetine mid-course — the combination arm was statistically indistinguishable from CT alone acutely, and by 12 months the combination arm's recovery rate (40%) had fallen well below CT alone (68%), while its recovery rate was no longer statistically distinguishable from paroxetine alone (24%) or placebo (4%) — despite that large a raw gap remaining on paper. There is no trial in this literature that shows adding medication to an *ongoing* psychotherapy improves the durable outcome. The two trials that showed a same-outcome benefit from combination (Blanco et al.'s phenelzine trial and, acutely, the Nordahl paroxetine trial) both randomised the combination from the start, alongside the monotherapies — they do not speak to the sequential question of layering a drug onto psychotherapy already underway. Absent trial evidence either way for that specific sequence, the reasoning that follows from what evidence does exist: a partial response by mid-course is not, by itself, sufficient justification for adding medication, because the strongest available data point (Nordahl's 12-month result) suggests doing so can *cost* durable gains rather than add them, plausibly by diluting the patient's attribution of improvement to their own new skills and exposure practice rather than to a pill — a mechanism this literature does not test directly but which is consistent with why exposure-alone arms keep improving after treatment ends while medication-exposed arms do not.

#### 11.6.3 What the evidence says about stopping medication

The clearest data on discontinuation come from the general-practice sertraline trial and its one-year follow-up. Both sertraline-containing arms deteriorated significantly on functioning (SF-36) relative to exposure alone once the drug was withdrawn, while the exposure-alone arm continued to improve for a further 28 weeks off any active treatment. This is a single trial in a primary-care population with unstructured, GP-delivered exposure rather than a specialist cognitive therapy protocol, so it should not be over-generalised to specialist-delivered CT — but it is the only data this literature has on what happens *after* stopping medication in social anxiety disorder specifically, and what it shows is not reassuring for a strategy of "start the SSRI, add exposure, then taper the drug once therapy has taken hold." The taper itself appears to be where the medication arms lose their apparent advantage. Where psychotherapy has been delivered as the primary treatment throughout, the pattern across this whole chapter — cognitive therapy's gains holding or extending at follow-up, medication-containing arms losing ground once the drug is withdrawn — argues for treating a genuine, well-delivered course of individual cognitive therapy as the more durable investment, with medication reserved for access, preference, or partial-response scenarios rather than routine addition.

#### 11.6.4 Where the evidence runs out, and what a clinician must still decide

Several practical questions any real caseload generates are simply not answered by this literature, and pretending otherwise would misrepresent it. There is no trial evidence on how long to persist with a first-line individual course before formally declaring non-response and escalating — the Delphi consensus definition above supplies staging language, not a session count. There is no trial testing whether the "add CBT to antidepressant-refractory patients" result generalises to patients on SNRIs rather than SSRIs, or to patients with comorbid avoidant personality disorder specifically (the Nordahl trial's caution about reflexive addition, notably, came from a sample that was 54% avoidant-personality-disordered, so it may be the single most relevant data point available for that subgroup, but it was not designed to test the question directly). And there is no trial answering whether medication should be started concurrently with psychotherapy when access to timely CBT is the binding constraint — a genuinely common situation in under-resourced settings, where the honest answer is that starting an SSRI while a referral for structured individual cognitive therapy is pending is a reasonable default on access grounds alone, not because the combination trials recommend it, and the plan should include a clear point at which the medication is reassessed once psychotherapy actually begins, given how uniformly this chapter's evidence favours consolidating gains around the psychotherapy rather than the drug. Where the trials are silent, that reasoning — not an invented number — is what should govern the decision.

## 12. Special populations and required adaptations

The evidence base for social anxiety disorder is built overwhelmingly on adults without significant comorbidity, treated in specialty anxiety clinics in high-income settings. Every population below departs from that default in a way that changes the expected effect size, the protocol content, or both. The most consequential departure is developmental: the disorder that responds best to individual cognitive therapy in adults is, in children, the disorder a generic anxiety protocol handles worst.

### 12.1 Children and adolescents

#### The central finding: generic youth-anxiety CBT underperforms specifically for social anxiety

The largest dataset on this question, the Genes for Treatment study, pooled 1,519 children aged 5–18 (94% aged 5–13) across eleven sites in Australia, the UK, Scandinavia, Germany, Switzerland, the Netherlands and the US, all of whom completed a full course of CBT for a primary anxiety disorder — individual (mean 11.8 sessions), group (mean 10 sessions), or guided self-help (mean 7.3 sessions). With severity, comorbidity and modality entered simultaneously in mixed models, children with primary social anxiety disorder had significantly poorer response and lower remission than children with primary GAD at post-treatment and at 3/6/12-month follow-up; the authors state these children were "nearly twice as likely as children with primary GAD to still have a diagnosis at the end of the study." The effect held across every modality — poorer-response coefficients were significant for individual CBT (β = 0.36, 95% CI 0.16–0.56), group CBT (β = 0.54, 0.37–0.70) and guided self-help (β = 0.37, 0.13–0.62), and lower-remission odds ratios likewise for individual (OR 0.41, 0.18–0.93), group (OR 0.16, 0.07–0.33) and self-help (OR 0.08, 0.01–0.84) — and applied wherever social anxiety appeared in the diagnostic profile, not only as the primary diagnosis. A comparably severe comorbid diagnosis, specific phobia, showed the same pattern only at post-treatment and caught up by follow-up; social anxiety did not. Treatment modality, age, gender and parental involvement were not significant predictors once diagnosis was accounted for. The authors' own reading is the one this manual carries forward: the pooled trials delivered a "generic format targeting a heterogeneous group," and they explicitly recommend social-anxiety-tailored content — social skills training, video feedback, attention training, naming Social Effectiveness Training by name.

The Child/Adolescent Anxiety Multimodal Study (CAMS, NCT00052078) corroborates this from a different angle. In this 12-week, four-arm trial of 488 children aged 7–17 with separation anxiety, GAD or social phobia (CBT/Coping Cat, sertraline, combination, or pill placebo, randomized 2:2:2:1), remission rates — which varied across several definitions — ranked combination highest (46–68%), sertraline second (34–46%), CBT alone third (20–46%), placebo lowest (15–27%). Absence of social phobia predicted remission; a principal diagnosis of social phobia specifically predicted worse continuous-severity outcome; and in the most severely affected subgroup (n = 220), only combination separated from placebo on remission (RR 2.85, 95% CI 1.51–5.39, p < .001) — CBT alone did not (RR 1.55, NS) and sertraline alone did not (RR 1.27, NS). This is where the manual's adult spine inverts: individual cognitive therapy tops the adult network, ahead of medication. In severe youth social anxiety, CBT monotherapy failed to beat placebo where medication-containing combination succeeded, and sertraline alone outranked CBT alone on remission across the whole trial. The ordering that governs adult treatment choice does not reproduce in children; closing that gap is what the SAD-specific content below is for. Long-run CAMS follow-up tempers all of this: at a mean of 6 years (CAMELS, n = 288), 46.5% were in remission, acute responders only modestly more likely to be among them (OR 1.83, 95% CI 1.08–3.09), and 48% of acute responders had relapsed; a 4-year-window extension (n = 319) found ~22% stable remission, 30% chronically ill, 48% relapsers, with treatment arm no longer related to outcome at either follow-up. A Norwegian community-clinic replication (n = 139, mean 3.9 years post-treatment, 63% overall loss of principal diagnosis) reproduced the same asymmetry — principal social phobia had lower recovery odds than separation anxiety or GAD — and a separate Norwegian individual-vs-group effectiveness trial found full diagnostic recovery in only 20.5% (group) and 25.3% (individual), a useful floor against which to read remission percentages elsewhere in this section.

#### SAD-specific youth protocols

Social Effectiveness Therapy for Children (SET-C) combines structured social skills training, graduated in vivo exposure, and — its defining element — peer generalization sessions rehearsing skills with non-clinical, same-age peers rather than the therapist alone. Against Testbusters, an active study-skills control, it produced a substantially larger diagnostic-remission rate at post-treatment with gains maintained at six months [unverified — no citable DOI attaches to the specific percentages for this trial]. Its evidence is firmer in a three-arm trial against fluoxetine and pill placebo in ages 7–17: both actives beat placebo on social distress, avoidance and functioning, but SET-C was superior to fluoxetine on each, and was the only treatment to improve social skills, situational anxiety and social competence — outcomes fluoxetine never separated from placebo on. Trajectories also diverged: fluoxetine's effect plateaued by week 8, SET-C continued improving through week 12. A companion mechanistic analysis of the same trial found only SET-C improved, pre-to-post, on pragmatic social behaviour and paralinguistic behaviour (posture, motor movement, facial orientation); fluoxetine-treated children showed no more skill than placebo — direct evidence that anxiolysis alone does not repair the behavioural deficit. A dissemination variant, Pegasys-VR, replaced the hardest-to-arrange component — real peer sessions — with AI-driven avatars, and matched standard SET-C: 60% versus 63% no longer met SAD criteria at post-treatment, with equal anxiety and social-skill gains.

School-based delivery is the other pillar. Skills for Academic and Social Success (SASS) is twelve 40-minute group sessions, at least two individual meetings, four weekend social events with prosocial peers from the students' own schools, teacher meetings to arrange classroom exposures, and two parent psychoeducation meetings — peer generalization transplanted into the school itself. Against a contact-matched attention control delivered entirely in-school, 59% of SASS-treated adolescents lost their SAD diagnosis at post-treatment versus 0% of controls, maintained at six months; parent report, notably, did not discriminate between conditions. A larger follow-up trial found SASS delivered by trained school counsellors statistically indistinguishable from SASS delivered by psychologists, both beating a non-specific counselling control at post-treatment and five months — task-shifting to existing school staff is empirically supported here, with fidelity maintenance the open question.

#### Format, and the youth network inversion

A Norwegian trial of adolescents aged 13–16 with primary social phobia randomized to individual cognitive therapy, group CBT, or attentional placebo found individual therapy significantly better than both comparators at post-treatment and 12 months — but no significant difference between group CBT and the attentional placebo. This is a harder result than the adult literature yields once protocols are pooled; a clinic defaulting to group format for adolescent social anxiety on throughput grounds should read it first. A dedicated meta-analysis of SAD-specific youth psychotherapy (17 RCTs) found SMD −1.13, remission RR 8.99 (NNT 3.3), no dropout elevation (RR 1.00), quality-of-life SMD 0.79, depression SMD −0.39, against considerable heterogeneity and low study quality the authors themselves flag. For calibration, the current Cochrane review of trans-diagnostic youth-anxiety CBT (41 studies, 1,806 participants, explicitly excluding selective mutism) found remission OR 7.85 (95% CI 5.31–11.60, NNT 6.0) versus waitlist, no advantage over active controls or treatment-as-usual, and no durable follow-up gain. The two figures are not directly comparable — different populations, different comparators — but the pattern is consistent: generic youth CBT clears waitlist and little else, while SAD-tailored content produces remission rates and NNTs approaching the adult individual-CBT literature.

#### Parental involvement: not the lever

An overview of 25 systematic reviews spanning two decades found 21 detected no format difference between youth-only, parent-only and family-inclusive CBT, and 22 were judged inconclusive; where a directional pattern was detectable, parent-only delivery performed worse, and the early literature's preference for family-inclusive over youth-only delivery did not reproduce in later reviews. This matches Genes for Treatment directly — parental involvement level did not predict response or remission and did not change the diagnosis effect — though a separate meta-analysis of secondary outcomes (42 RCTs, n = 3,239) found higher parental involvement beneficial specifically at follow-up for general functioning and comorbid symptoms. The practical reading: parental involvement is not the lever that improves child and adolescent social anxiety outcome. Social-anxiety-specific content — skills training, peer generalization, video feedback, in-school exposure — is.

### 12.2 Selective mutism

The base is genuinely small: a meta-analysis identified only five RCTs, 233 participants total, against no-treatment or waitlist, yielding a large weighted effect (g = 0.87) that did not differ by SM-specific versus non-specific outcome measures. The authors name the gap explicitly: no trial has compared an effective SM treatment against a psychological placebo or another bona fide active treatment.

The best-developed protocol combines stimulus fading with defocused communication — the adult deliberately reduces direct verbal demand and eye-contact pressure while widening the circle of people and settings the child is expected to speak across. Its founding RCT (ages 3–9, three months treatment versus waitlist) found a significant time-by-group interaction on the School Speech Questionnaire, speech increasing in treatment and flat on waitlist, with younger children responding better. One-year follow-up found no decline (8 of 24 still met criteria, 4 partial remission, 12 diagnosis-free), with a striking age split: 78% of ages 3–5 versus 33% of ages 6–9 were free of SM. Five-year follow-up (30 of 32 children, mean age 6 at treatment, mean 21 weeks of CBT) found 21 full remission, 5 partial, 4 still meeting criteria; older baseline age, greater baseline severity and familial SM were the significant negative predictors — and 23% of the cohort had gone on to meet criteria for social phobia by year five, a direct empirical link that argues for treating selective mutism as an early-intervention point on the social anxiety spectrum rather than a separate entity.

Integrated Behavior Therapy for Selective Mutism (24 weeks versus 12-week waitlist, ages 4–8) produced 75% blind-evaluator responders, with functional speaking improved by parent and teacher report, though the group difference on words-spoken-at-school specifically was not significant. Intensive Group Behavioral Treatment compresses the same principles into five consecutive days: 50% responders at week 4 versus 0% on a four-week waitlist, with significant gains in social anxiety severity, situational verbal behaviour and global functioning (not yet core SM severity or home speech); by the next school year 46% were diagnosis-free, with independent teacher ratings of less impairment and more classroom speech. A telehealth adaptation of the same five-day model retained 100% of families through all assessments and produced roughly half of children classified as responders at post-treatment and 4-month follow-up, though this remains pilot-scale.

Medication does not substitute for the behavioural work: a small placebo-controlled single-case trial of fluoxetine produced medium-to-large effects on social anxiety and speech measures in five children, but all five still met full SM criteria at study end. The recurring elements across the effective trials — graded stimulus fading, defocused communication, shaping and contingency management for audible speech, delivery in school and home rather than clinic alone, teacher involvement, caregiver coaching, early intervention — are what the evidence supports; medication is at best an adjunct.

### 12.3 Older adults

State this plainly: no randomized trial of psychotherapy for social anxiety disorder specifically in older adults was identified, despite repeated targeted searches. Everything usable is imported by analogy from late-life anxiety more broadly, and the analogy carries its own warning. A meta-analysis of CBT for GAD comparing older against working-age adults (15 studies, 22 comparisons, 770 patients) found a moderate effect in older people (g = 0.55, 95% CI 0.22–0.88) against a large effect in working-age adults (g = 0.94, 0.52–1.36); the between-group difference was not statistically significant, but the authors read the gap as leaving "room for improvement," and flag that no older-adult study used intention-to-treat analysis and that the older-adult protocols tested were standard CBT never actually adapted with gerontological content. A meta-analysis of third-wave mindfulness-based CBT in older adults found anxiety g = 0.58 across ten studies but flagged evident publication bias on that outcome and only one study with an active control. A secondary analysis of home-delivered CBT for depression in rural, medically frail adults ≥65 found significant improvement on anxiety and phobic-anxiety subscales against a minimal-support control — not a SAD-diagnosed sample, symptom-level only. Access compounds the efficacy gap: in PRISM-E, which screened 24,930 primary-care patients ≥65 and randomized 2,022 to integrated co-located mental-health care versus enhanced referral, engagement was 71% versus 49% (mean 3.04 versus 1.91 visits) — but the anxiety subgroup across the whole trial numbered only 70 patients, illustrating how thinly late-life anxiety is represented even in trials built to reach this group.

The manual's recommendation: deliver the standard adult protocol — individual cognitive therapy carries the strongest adult evidence — while expecting, by GAD analogy, a possibly smaller effect than in working-age adults, and stating plainly to the patient that the age-specific evidence base for psychotherapy in late-life social anxiety disorder does not exist. An absence of trials is a finding a clinician needs, not a gap to paper over with adult numbers.

### 12.4 Comorbid autism spectrum disorder

The definitive trial is TAASD (NCT02028247): 214 enrolled, 167 randomized (145 completed), mean age 9.9 years (SD 1.8), ages 7–13, IQ > 70, comparing standard-of-practice CBT (Coping Cat), an autism-adapted protocol (BIACA), and treatment as usual, blinded evaluators. The adaptation keeps every core CBT element and adds explicit perspective-taking training and behaviour-analytic techniques targeting autism's social-communication and self-regulation challenges. On the primary outcome (Pediatric Anxiety Rating Scale), mean scores were 2.13 (SD 0.91) for adapted CBT, 2.43 (SD 0.70) for standard CBT, 2.93 (SD 0.59) for treatment as usual — adapted significantly better than standard (p = .04) and than TAU (p < .001). Positive response (CGI-I) was achieved by 92.4% on adapted CBT, 81.0% on standard CBT, 11.1% on TAU (p < .001 each active versus TAU). CBT works for anxiety in autistic children regardless of adaptation, but the adaptation produced a further, distinguishable gain concentrated exactly where social anxiety lives: internalizing symptoms (adapted-vs-standard difference −0.097, 95% CI −0.172 to −0.023), ASD-associated social-communication symptoms (−0.115, −0.223 to −0.007), anxiety-associated social functioning (−0.160, −0.307 to −0.013). A latent-profile analysis of the same sample found five distinct personality subgroups with response contingent on membership — most did better with adapted CBT, but one subgroup did better with standard CBT on a school-adaptation measure; the adaptation is not uniformly superior for every autistic child, only on average and specifically on social outcomes.

At the meta-analytic level, 26 RCTs (n = 1,251) found CBT for anxiety in autistic children moderately effective (g = −0.70), and 11 RCTs (n = 475) found social skills training smaller effects (children g = −0.35, adults g = −0.34), with high risk of bias in 18 of the pooled trials. Anxiety is pooled across disorders here — there is no social-anxiety-specific effect size, and the −0.70 figure should never be quoted as though it were one. A broader umbrella review of 128 meta-analyses in autism found only "suggestive evidence" for social skills groups, with just 4 of 128 surviving a low-risk-of-bias sensitivity analysis.

Evidence in autistic adults is a twelve-person pilot and should be presented as exactly that: a placebo-controlled MDMA-assisted psychotherapy trial (NCT02008396, 8 MDMA versus 4 placebo across two 8-hour sessions plus non-drug follow-up) found a large placebo-subtracted effect on LSAS at one month (d = 1.4) and six months (d = 1.1), but both confidence intervals crossed zero (−0.074 to 2.874; −0.307 to 2.527) — hypothesis-generating, not an effect estimate.

Clinical rule: offer CBT to autistic children with clinically significant social anxiety without hesitation, and make the autism-specific adaptation (perspective-taking layered onto standard exposure content, delivered by clinicians familiar with behaviour-analytic technique) the default, since it is the arm that moves the social-functioning outcomes this disorder is actually about.

### 12.5 Comorbid depression

In adults, comorbid depression is not a reason to sequence — treat the depression first is not what the evidence supports. An individual-participant-level meta-analysis obtaining raw data from 41 studies (46 conditions, n = 4,381) of CBT and pharmacotherapy for social anxiety disorder — the first analysis of this question at the individual rather than study level — found individuals with higher pre-treatment depression had greater decreases in social anxiety from pre- to post-treatment, though not sustained to follow-up. The effect was modality-specific: depressive symptoms predicted better post-treatment outcome for individual and internet-delivered CBT, not for group CBT or pharmacotherapy. The authors' conclusion: depression does not negatively affect SAD treatment outcome and may improve it in some formats — a finding about symptom severity, not active suicidality or a melancholic/psychotic episode requiring its own management first.

A large multicentre trial of Clark and Wells cognitive therapy (n = 244) found number of comorbid diagnoses negatively associated with end-state functioning and response but not remission, while baseline severity was negatively associated with end-state functioning and remission but not response; personality, attachment style and interpersonal problems moderated marginally or not at all, and greater shame at baseline predicted better response. Up to 37% of post-treatment variance was explained by combined baseline predictors — comorbidity and severity matter, but not in the intuitive direction, and which outcome definition is used changes which predictor appears.

This is where age reverses the rule entirely rather than generalizing across the lifespan. In children, comorbid mood disorder cuts the other way: in Genes for Treatment, comorbid mood or externalizing disorder roughly doubled the likelihood of retaining the primary diagnosis across follow-up, and in CAMS, absence of other internalizing disorders was itself a predictor of remission. Depression is neutral-to-helpful for adult SAD outcome and harmful for paediatric SAD outcome — quoting the adult finding for a paediatric caseload would directly contradict the paediatric evidence.

### 12.6 Comorbid alcohol use disorder

Roughly one in five people with social anxiety disorder develops alcohol abuse or dependence, and the sequencing question has a genuinely counterintuitive answer. The founding cautionary trial randomized comorbid patients to 12 weeks of CBT for alcoholism alone (n = 44) or the same treatment with social-anxiety-focused CBT added alongside it (n = 49). Both groups improved on drinking and social anxiety, but — against the study's own hypothesis — the concurrent-treatment group had worse outcomes on three of four alcohol-use indices, and the added anxiety treatment produced no additional social-anxiety benefit at all. Naively bolting SAD-focused exposure work onto a standard alcohol programme can make drinking worse, not better; this is the finding to foreground before "just adding" anxiety treatment to an addiction programme.

The distinction that resolves the apparent contradiction is structural integration, not timing. A Fully Integrated Treatment trial (FIT, NCT03428490) delivered inside a community substance-use clinic randomized 56 patients to usual care (the Matrix Model intensive outpatient programme) or the same programme with exposure-based SAD treatment woven directly into its sessions. By six-month follow-up, integrated care was superior on drinking days in the past 30 days and on social anxiety severity, though the arms did not differ on quantity consumed per drinking day or on broader alcohol-related problems. A second trial added a three-week stabilization period (abstinence or substantially reduced consumption) before diagnosis and randomization; of 86 screened, 37 (65% of those completing stabilization) were randomized to integrated CBT for alcohol plus anxiety/depression versus usual counselling. Intention-to-treat analysis favoured integrated treatment on days to relapse and days to lapse, with a significant treatment-by-time interaction on percentage days abstinent; critically, the heavy-drinking-days benefit was statistically mediated by change in anxiety symptoms — the drinking improvement ran through the anxiety improvement, not around it.

Read together: concurrent is not integrated, and unintegrated concurrent delivery can actively harm the addiction outcome. What worked either wove exposure work directly into the addiction programme's structure, or stabilized drinking first and then delivered integrated treatment of both conditions together. Expect improvement in drinking frequency and abstinent days rather than in quantity consumed per occasion, and set that expectation with patients explicitly.

### 12.7 Comorbid avoidant personality disorder

Avoidant personality disorder comorbidity marks greater severity and impairment, but it is not a reliable negative moderator of outcome, and one large trial found the opposite of what intuition predicts. A confirmatory factor analysis in 335 patients found a two-factor structure (generalized social phobia and AvPD as distinct, correlated constructs) fit slightly better than one factor; AvPD patients were more severe and more impaired — but showed *more* early change in treatment, not less. A four-arm trial (paroxetine, cognitive therapy, combination, placebo) in which 54% of the sample met AvPD criteria — an AvPD-enriched trial in effect — found cognitive therapy superior to paroxetine and to placebo at post-treatment, and at 12 months remained significantly better than both, with recovery rates of 68% (cognitive therapy), 40% (combination), 24% (paroxetine), 4% (placebo); combination provided no advantage over either monotherapy and was numerically worse than cognitive therapy alone at 12 months. In a population half of whom carried AvPD, individual cognitive therapy alone was strongest, and adding an SSRI degraded the year-out result. The Clark and Wells predictor analysis above found personality, attachment and interpersonal problems marginal moderators at best, and a residential cognitive-versus-interpersonal trial found AvPD did not differentially predict format response — age of onset and treatment expectations were the strongest predictors identified.

The trial built specifically to test format in this comorbidity randomized 154 outpatients with both SAD and AvPD to group schema therapy or group CBT across thirty weekly sessions over roughly nine months — substantially longer than the twelve-to-sixteen sessions typical of standard protocols. Intention-to-treat analysis found no significant difference between modalities on the LSAS or the purpose-built Avoidant Personality Disorder Severity Index at three months or one year, both producing substantial improvement; the one clear difference was retention, significantly better with schema therapy, leading the authors to argue for schema therapy's greater acceptability and to redirect future group-CBT work toward retention rather than efficacy. A subsequent moderator/mediator analysis of the same 154 patients found none — no demographic or clinical moderator, no mediator among emotion regulation, self-esteem, experiential avoidance or schema-mode scores distinguished which patients did better in which arm; the one signal, present regardless of condition, was that self-esteem, average schema-mode score and avoidant-protector-mode intensity at mid-treatment predicted end-of-treatment social anxiety.

Practical synthesis: AvPD comorbidity should change the length and retention strategy of treatment, not the active ingredient. Plan for a substantially longer course — thirty sessions over roughly nine months is the only tested duration in a dedicated trial — favour cognitive therapy's demonstrated 12-month durability over combination treatment, and do not add an SSRI reflexively on the assumption that personality pathology needs pharmacological support. Where retention, not efficacy, is the concern, schema therapy's demonstrated edge is the more defensible reason to choose it.

### 12.8 Cultural adaptation and non-Western delivery

The best transportability evidence is not a trial of a culturally *adapted* protocol — it is a trial of an unmodified Western one delivered by local clinicians elsewhere. A UK-developed, therapist-guided internet cognitive therapy programme for social anxiety disorder (iCT-SAD) delivered by local Chinese therapists in Hong Kong, waitlist-controlled (22 per arm, blind diagnostic assessment), produced an adjusted between-group LSAS difference of 55.36 points (95% CI 44.32–66.39, p < .001, d = 2.41), superior to waitlist on every secondary measure, with 86% remission versus 5% and 73% versus 9% losing the diagnosis entirely — gains held at three months. The authors' conclusion is the one to carry forward: dissemination into a substantially different culture "did not result in a substantial loss of efficacy." The trial is small and waitlist-controlled, in a single Chinese urban setting.

A Japanese single-arm trial of sixteen-week videoconference CBT, followed to twelve months, included eight social-anxiety patients among a broader 25-patient anxiety/OCD cohort; LSAS improved 30.9 points at twelve months, and the intervention was judged cost-effective by formal analysis (delivery cost ¥60,800–81,960 per patient against a ¥189,500 threshold). With only eight SAD patients and no control arm this is supportive, not conclusive, evidence that core CBT content delivered remotely can produce outcomes in the expected range.

Two trials in progress will sharpen this further: a culturally adapted Urdu-language iCT-SAD trial in Pakistan (planned n = 800, primary outcome LSAS change at six months) whose registry record is internally inconsistent — listed as not yet recruiting while carrying start and completion dates already passed — should be flagged rather than cited as a result until resolved; and a Hong Kong non-inferiority trial (target n = 110) testing whether the same programme can be coach-guided rather than clinician-guided without loss of efficacy.

What is missing should be stated as plainly as the older-adult gap: no head-to-head trial of culturally adapted versus unadapted CBT for SAD was identified in any population — the Hong Kong data show transport without adaptation works, not that adaptation adds anything, because nothing has tested adaptation against the unmodified version. No treatment trial for taijin kyofusho, and no Morita-therapy trial for social anxiety, was retrievable. Nothing was identified from South Asia, sub-Saharan Africa, Latin America or the Middle East. This is a literature concentrated in a handful of high-income East Asian and Western settings, and the manual should not imply broader coverage than that.

### 12.9 Perinatal and pregnancy

No trial of psychotherapy for social anxiety disorder specifically in pregnancy or the postpartum period was identified, across repeated targeted searches. The nearest evidence is a prevention trial in which social phobia appears only inside a composite outcome: a cluster-randomized trial of a brief, couple-focused psychoeducational programme for first-time parents across 48 maternal and child health centres in Victoria, Australia (required sample 184 women per arm), whose primary outcome is a composite diagnosis at six months postpartum spanning major depressive episode, GAD, panic disorder, agoraphobia, social phobia, adult separation anxiety, or adjustment disorder — social phobia is one of seven conditions folded into a single endpoint, citable only as evidence that perinatal social anxiety is captured inside broader prevention research, never as a social-anxiety-specific result.

State this without hedging: there is no direct evidence to guide psychotherapy for social anxiety disorder in the perinatal period. The reasonable default is the adult evidence base described throughout this manual — individual exposure-based cognitive therapy first line, guided internet-delivered CBT for its access advantages in a postpartum population — alongside the observation that psychotherapy carries none of the perinatal safety considerations attending an SSRI prescription, itself a reason to favour psychotherapy first line here even absent population-specific outcome data. That absence should be stated to the patient as plainly as it is stated here.

## 13. When the first course fails

### 13.1 What "failure" actually means

There is no validated staging model for treatment-resistant social anxiety disorder comparable to the sequential-trial staging systems used in depression. What exists instead is a set of instrument-anchored definitions that differ from study to study, and a clinician reading "treatment-resistant" into a chart note should first ask which of these definitions is in play, because they diverge substantially. **Response** is usually a proportional symptom reduction — commonly ≥31% reduction on the Liebowitz Social Anxiety Scale (LSAS) in routine-care benchmarking, or a Clinical Global Impression rating of "much" or "very much improved." **Remission** is a stricter, absolute threshold — LSAS ≤30 in the routine-practice literature — and is a different, harder target: in the Child/Adolescent Anxiety Multimodal Study, remission rates were significantly lower than response rates across every arm, and the same dissociation shows up in adults. **Loss of diagnosis** (no longer meeting DSM criteria on structured interview) is stricter still and is the outcome most individual RCTs report — 84% of patients lost the diagnosis after Clark & Wells cognitive therapy at post-treatment against 42% after exposure plus applied relaxation and 0% on a waitlist, in one of the founding trials of the model. **Recovery**, in IAPT-style routine-care terms, adds a functioning threshold on top of remission. A patient who has "responded" by one definition may be nowhere near "remitted" by another, and a clinician comparing a discharged patient's outcome against textbook numbers must first match definitions before concluding the treatment underperformed.

The base rates that calibrate expectations are sobering and worth stating plainly, because a manual that implies remission is the normal outcome sets clinicians up to over-diagnose failure. In the SOPHO-NET trial — the largest head-to-head psychotherapy RCT in this disorder — remission at the end of acute treatment was 36% with cognitive-behavioral therapy and 26% with psychodynamic therapy, against 9% on a waiting list; response was 60% and 52% respectively. In routine, non-trial practice, remission after a manualized short-term psychodynamic protocol was 9–16%, with response 28–33% — a floor far below anything reported from an efficacy trial. Across anxiety disorders more broadly, a 12-year prospective naturalistic follow-up found that social phobia had the smallest probability of recovery of any of GAD, panic disorder, or SAD, and that patients who did recover had a high probability of recurrence. None of this means treatment "doesn't work" — the effect sizes against waitlist are large — but it does mean that failing to reach full remission after one course is common, not exceptional, and the diagnostic work below exists precisely because "failure" is so often really "an incompletely delivered adequate trial" rather than genuine resistance.

**An adequate trial**, for the purposes of this section, means: the correct disorder-specific formulation, delivered at protocol dose, with documented evidence that its core mechanisms actually changed. Each of those three elements is checkable, and checking them — before relabeling the case as resistant — is where session twelve should start.

### 13.2 Five questions before concluding the treatment failed

#### 13.2.1 Was the formulation right?

"CBT for anxiety" is not synonymous with disorder-specific cognitive therapy for social anxiety, and the two do not perform the same. In the pooled Genes for Treatment cohort of 1,519 children who completed a full course of generic anxiety CBT across eleven international sites, those with a primary diagnosis of social anxiety disorder had significantly poorer response and lower remission than those with primary GAD across every delivery modality — individual (β = 0.36), group (β = 0.54), and guided self-help (β = 0.37), all in the direction of less symptom change — and the authors state children with SAD were "nearly twice as likely… to still have a diagnosis at the end of the study." Their own reading is that these were generic, non-tailored protocols, and they recommend SAD-specific content — social skills training, video feedback, attention training. The adolescent evidence makes the same point from the format angle: in a three-arm RCT, individual cognitive therapy beat both group CBT and an attentional placebo, and group CBT did not differ from the placebo at all — a hard finding for any clinician defaulting to a group format for a teenager. A school-based trial found 59% of adolescents receiving a disorder-specific protocol lost their SAD diagnosis against 0% on a credible, structurally matched non-specific control, concluding plainly that "adolescents with social anxiety disorder do not respond to non-specific treatment." Consistent with the spine of this manual, the network evidence shows only individual CBT and antidepressants beat their own matched placebo condition; group CBT, though far better than waitlist, has not been shown to beat one. So the first question at session twelve is not "did CBT fail" but "was this Clark-and-Wells-model individual cognitive therapy for social anxiety, specifically, delivered as such" — and if the answer is generic anxiety CBT, or a group format that was never tested against an active control, the case for calling this a failed adequate trial is weak.

#### 13.2.2 Was the dose adequate?

The best-documented CT-SAD dose in the literature is 12.8 (SD 2.1) weekly 90-minute sessions — 18.4 therapist hours — plus roughly 2.3 booster sessions and, within those sessions, a mean of 13.5 behavioral experiments. Set that against the reality that the same trial's authors cite the IAPT service average as 7.5 sessions: routine UK practice delivers roughly half the protocolised dose. Homework is the other half of dose, and it is not a formality — a meta-analysis of 17 CBT studies (N = 2,312) separated homework quantity from homework quality (skill acquisition, not just completion) and found both mattered, with quantity yielding Hedges' g = 0.79 at post-treatment and 0.51 at follow-up, and quality g = 0.78 and 1.07 respectively, though the quality estimates rest on only three studies and should be read as directionally suggestive rather than settled. A chart that shows twelve sessions logged but shows homework "reviewed briefly," or a treatment that never got past session eight before the referral for "resistance" was written, has not completed an adequate trial by the field's own operational benchmark, regardless of how many months have elapsed.

#### 13.2.3 Were safety behaviours actually dropped — or only discussed?

This is the question most likely to be skipped, because the concept is usually explained early and clinicians assume explanation equals change. It does not. In the largest causal test available — a factorial trial that randomized 464 patients to sixteen combinations of internet-CBT components with each component present or absent — the effect of the exposure component on outcome was mediated specifically by a *reduction in safety-behaviour use*, not by insight into the concept; avoidance of social situations, contrary to the a priori hypothesis, did not mediate the exposure effect at all. The same trial delivered the most direct challenge in the mechanism literature to the model's signature construct: self-focused attention was "consistently identified as an inert mechanism of change" when tested this way. The two single-session procedures that operationalize the formulation in the room — the self-focused-attention-and-safety-behaviours experiment and the video feedback experiment — were shown in a dedicated component study to be effective both at demonstrating to patients the role of self-focused attention and safety behaviours in maintaining the disorder, and at reducing symptoms in their own right. So the diagnostic question is behavioral and specific: can the patient name, session by session, which safety behaviours they used to use and stopped using in a real social situation this week — not whether they can define the term.

#### 13.2.4 Is an unaddressed comorbidity carrying the impairment?

Three comorbidities behave differently enough that a blanket rule is wrong. **Depression** in adults is, on the individual-participant evidence (46 treatment conditions, n = 4,381), *not* a reason to sequence care — higher pre-treatment depression predicted *greater* social anxiety improvement in individual and internet-delivered CBT specifically, though not in group CBT or pharmacotherapy, and not at follow-up. This is the opposite of the pediatric picture, where comorbid mood disorder in the GxT cohort roughly doubled the odds of retaining the primary diagnosis — do not import the adult rule downward. **Alcohol use disorder** is a sequencing trap: bolting SAD-focused CBT onto standard alcoholism CBT produced *worse* outcomes on three of four drinking indices with no social-anxiety benefit in the classic negative trial, while structurally integrating exposure work into the addiction program itself improved drinking frequency and social anxiety directly in a later trial, and stabilising drinking for roughly three weeks before starting integrated work improved relapse/lapse outcomes in another — though in that trial the drinking benefit ran through (was mediated by) change in anxiety rather than a directly demonstrated reduction in anxiety symptom scores themselves. The lesson is not "treat sequentially" or "treat concurrently" but "treat integrated, never bolted-on." **Avoidant personality disorder** splits by setting: in tightly protocolised trials it does not predict cognitive-therapy outcome — in one trial 72% of the sample met AvPD criteria and its presence "did not predict outcome in either treatment," with 82% of those who had it losing the diagnosis by one year — yet in naturalistic and routine-care samples, and in pharmacotherapy, personality-disorder features consistently predict a worse result. If the case in front of you was delivered as a tight, well-dosed individual protocol, AvPD is probably not the explanation for non-response; if it was delivered in routine care with limited session counts, it plausibly is.

#### 13.2.5 Was the treatment actually delivered as specified?

This is the least intuitive item on the list, because the honest answer from the outcome literature is that measured competence and adherence barely track outcome at all. A meta-analytic review across 36 studies found a mean weighted adherence–outcome correlation of r = .02 and competence–outcome r = .07, neither distinguishable from zero. A study of routine Swedish psychiatric care rated both dimensions directly — Cognitive Therapy Scale–Revised competence 40.5 (SD 6.5), 76% above the passing threshold — and found "neither therapist adherence nor competence was associated with patient outcomes," despite patients improving substantially (d = 0.80–1.36). Therapist identity itself explains a small slice of outcome variance in routine services — about 3.2–3.4%, smaller than the clinic-level effect of 8.2% — and even that effect is not well predicted by supervisor ratings of skill. This does not mean competence is irrelevant; it means that among therapists delivering a manualized protocol at a passable standard, further differences in rated skill are a weak lever, and a clinician should not expect chart audits of "fidelity" to reliably explain a stalled case. What the competence data *do* support is a floor, not a gradient: the implementation literature shows that a trained, motivated service can still fail to deliver — a UK CAMHS training program had to abandon its planned RCT altogether because of staff turnover and unfilled posts, delivering the protocol to only twelve adolescents through six trained therapists. If the "treatment" was delivered by a therapist with no disorder-specific training in a service under this kind of strain, competence is worth questioning even though the correlational data are weak; if it was delivered by an experienced clinician in a stable service, look elsewhere first.

### 13.3 Options once genuine non-response is confirmed

#### 13.3.1 More of the same

There is no dose-finding RCT of CT-SAD session number, so "how many more sessions" cannot be answered precisely. What exists is indirect: the one large dedicated trial in a severity-enriched population — 154 outpatients with both SAD and avoidant personality disorder — used 30 weekly sessions over roughly nine months, roughly double the standard protocol length, and produced significant, substantial improvement in both arms it tested. Separately, the authors of the routine-practice remission-floor trial noted that patients with heavy personality-disorder comorbidity and long treatment histories "may need longer treatments." Neither is a controlled test of dose extension as a strategy, but together they are the best available argument that, for a severe or comorbid case that has had an honestly adequate 12–16-session course, extending the same protocol rather than switching it is a defensible next step — better supported, in fact, than most of the switching options below.

#### 13.3.2 Switching psychotherapy modality

No RCT randomizes confirmed CT-SAD non-responders to a second, different psychotherapy — this is a genuine evidence gap, and the temptation to read cross-sectional head-to-head trials as switch evidence should be resisted. What the SOPHO-NET trial shows is that cognitive therapy's acute advantage over psychodynamic therapy on remission (36% vs 26%) had disappeared by 24 months, with both arms converging near 40% remission and 70% response as psychodynamic therapy continued gaining ground after treatment ended — a mechanical explanation (both treatments kept improving, one just started faster), not evidence that switching a CBT non-responder to psychodynamic therapy recovers the gap. Acceptance and commitment therapy performed equivalently to CBT overall, with a moderator worth knowing: patients with lower baseline psychological flexibility improved more under CBT than ACT by twelve months. Mindfulness-based stress reduction likewise matched group CBT on most outcomes, with CBT retaining an edge specifically on reducing avoidance behaviours. None of these trials enrolled non-responders; they are first-line parallel comparisons, and using them to justify a switch after failure is an extrapolation the manual should flag as such rather than present as tested.

#### 13.3.3 Switching to or adding medication

Here the evidence is more direct, and it runs against reflexive combination. Adding paroxetine to cognitive therapy gave no advantage over cognitive therapy alone at post-treatment, and at twelve months the combination arm's recovery rate (40%) was markedly worse than cognitive therapy alone (68%) — combination therapy actively degraded durability in a sample where 54% had comorbid avoidant personality disorder. A separate four-arm trial combining fluoxetine with comprehensive group CBT found no advantage for combination over either monotherapy (CGI response 50.8–54.2% across all active arms). And in the cleanest natural experiment on the *sequencing* question, patients who received sertraline together with GP-delivered exposure therapy, or sertraline alone, showed significant deterioration on general functioning relative to exposure alone by 52 weeks — 28 weeks after medication was stopped — while the exposure-alone group kept improving after treatment ended. Adding a drug to good psychotherapy, in other words, is not reliably additive and has repeatedly looked harmful at follow-up specifically. The one genuinely positive signal in this section runs the other direction, though it comes from a small, open-label trial (n = 42) in which usual care alone produced essentially no change, which inflates the contrast: in patients who remained symptomatic *after* antidepressant treatment, adding sixteen weeks of disorder-specific CBT to usual care produced a between-group LSAS reduction of 41.55 points, response in 85.7% versus 10.0%, and remission in 47.6% versus 0% — the single strongest randomized answer in this literature to "the SSRI didn't work, now what." That trial answers pharmacotherapy failure, not psychotherapy failure; the reverse question — does adding medication rescue a confirmed CBT non-responder — has not been tested in a sequential, post-failure design, only in from-the-start combination trials that argue against it.

#### 13.3.4 Intensifying format

The cleanest test of compressing the schedule without changing modality argues against assuming intensity alone helps: sixteen sessions of group cognitive treatment delivered over three weeks performed no better than a year of SSRI-based treatment as usual, while the same sixteen sessions delivered individually over four months beat both. This confounds format with intensity — it is not a pure dose-density test — but it should temper any assumption that "make it more intensive" is itself the active ingredient; on this evidence, individual format did the work, not compression.

#### 13.3.5 Augmentation of exposure — largely tested, largely negative

D-cycloserine is the paradigm case, and the manual should present the whole arc rather than lead with the early numbers. Two small pilot RCTs (n = 27 and n = 56) reported medium-to-large effects, and a pooled early meta-analysis of nine trials found SMD 0.46. As the evidence base grew and improved, the effect collapsed toward zero: a Cochrane review that pooled five SAD trials among its 21 found "no evidence of a difference" in its adult responder analysis (RR 1.10, 95% CI 0.89–1.34, across 9 studies spanning several anxiety disorders); a Bayesian meta-analysis of 1,314 patients found g = −0.12 (CI −0.27 to 0.02), with more recent, higher-quality studies reporting *smaller* effects; and the most authoritative synthesis — individual participant data from 1,047 of 1,073 eligible participants across 21 of 22 trials — put the true effect at d ≈ −0.25 post-treatment and d ≈ −0.19 at follow-up, with no moderators identified. A mechanistic rescue attempt, dosing only after low-fear ("successful") exposure sessions, was tested head-to-head against fixed pre- and post-session dosing in a purpose-built 152-patient four-arm trial and failed outright — the tailored condition showed no advantage over placebo at all. This is a clean instance of "this was tested and did not work," not "this has never been tested," and the honest recommendation follows directly: D-cycloserine is not a routine augmentation strategy for treatment-resistant SAD. Attention bias modification tells a similar story on a smaller evidence base — small effects overall across eleven randomized studies, with patients carrying a social anxiety diagnosis specifically tending to benefit *less* than other anxious patients from adding it to CBT. What is not augmentation but is a genuinely modifiable target is sleep: poorer baseline sleep quality predicted slower improvement and higher post-treatment severity across three sites, unmoderated by D-cycloserine — worth asking about, though it has not itself been tested as an intervention.

### 13.4 Where the evidence runs out

Several of the questions a clinician most wants answered at session twelve have never been tested, and the honest response is to say so rather than default to a plausible-sounding recommendation. No RCT has randomized confirmed, adequately-dosed CT-SAD non-responders to a second, different psychotherapy modality — everything cited above on modality comparison is a first-line parallel trial, not a switch study. No RCT has added medication specifically after a documented failure of adequate individual CBT — the combination evidence available is all from-the-start co-administration, and it argues against the strategy without directly testing the sequential version of it. There is no dose-finding trial establishing an optimal CT-SAD session count, so "extend the course" rests on analogy from a severity-enriched comorbid trial, not a controlled comparison of dose lengths. And for older adults, there is no RCT of psychotherapy for social anxiety disorder specifically at all — guidance for a non-responding older patient is extrapolated from generalized anxiety disorder data (where CBT's effect in older adults, g = 0.55, is directionally but not statistically smaller than in working-age adults, g = 0.94, and where no study used an intention-to-treat design), not derived from disorder-specific evidence. These are not failures of this manual's search; they are gaps in the field's evidence base, and a clinician facing a genuinely resistant case should weigh the options in Section 13.3 in that order — extend dose, then consider a documented-gap switch or augmentation with expectations set accordingly — rather than treat any of them as more settled than the trials actually show.

## 14. Outcome, durability, and what actually predicts it

### 14.1 How much durability evidence actually exists

Before promising durability, a warning about the evidence itself: the anchor network meta-analysis behind this manual's headline efficacy figures excludes long-term data altogether — its comparisons are all post-treatment. Any case that psychotherapy's benefit endures has to come from a thinner, separate literature, and it does not support the confidence with which "CBT's gains last" is usually asserted. The most direct attempt to quantify it — 69 RCTs, 4,118 outpatients across anxiety-related disorders, its own trials "mainly of low quality" — reports SAD Hedges g of 0.34–0.60 from post-treatment through 6–12 months, but at ≥12 months g = 0.42 from only three studies (versus ten for GAD, five for PTSD; panic non-significant, OCD had no eligible data). Relapse was reported in just 6 of the 69 trials, mostly for panic disorder. This is the field's central caveat: the "psychotherapy for SAD is durable" claim rests on three controlled comparisons past one year, and almost no trial defines relapse as an event at all. An earlier 108-trial meta-analysis states the asymmetry from the drug side: pharmacotherapy's durability "could not be assessed at all" for lack of follow-up data, while the moderate psychological gains that were measured continued — with no matched drug arm running in parallel.

Individual trials mostly agree. CT against fluoxetine-plus-self-exposure and placebo-plus-self-exposure (n = 60) remained superior to fluoxetine through a 3-month booster and 12-month follow-up. CT against exposure-plus-relaxation and waitlist (n = 62) produced 84%/42%/0% loss of diagnosis at post-treatment, persisting at one year, exposure patients more likely to seek additional treatment — an under-used relapse proxy; the same trial reported "therapist effects were small and nonsignificant" (14.8). CT versus IPT versus waitlist (n = 117) showed response widening from 65.8%/42.1%/7.3% at post to 68.4%/31.6% at one year. A 102-patient paroxetine/CT/combination/placebo trial (54% comorbid avoidant PD) found 12-month recovery of 68% (CT), 40% (combination), 24% (paroxetine), 4% (placebo) — combination conferred no advantage, an early instance of the spine's point that medication addition can attenuate durability.

Two trials push further out. A 67-patient intensive-group-versus-individual CT cohort, recontacted at five years (72% participated), found 75% (36/48) had sought no further treatment, both arms improving further to five years. An internet-CBT cohort at 89%/80% retention showed LSAS-SR falling from 71.3 (95% CI 66.1–76.5) to 40.3 (35.2–45.3), Cohen's d 1.30–1.40, one-year gains fully sustained. The longest naturalistic follow-up — 70 exposure-treated outpatients, median 6 years, 45 remitted — found 6/45 (13%) relapsed, survival estimates 98%/85%/85% in remission at 2/5/10 years; the authors' own caveat is that one patient in three could not complete or benefit from treatment, so 85% is conditional on remission, not intention-to-treat. And SOPHO-NET's 24-month follow-up (n = 495, CBT vs psychodynamic therapy) shows an acute advantage dissolving: remission 36% vs 26% at post, but by 24 months response ≈70% and remission ≈40% in *both* arms — the initially worse therapy caught up rather than the better one pulling ahead.

### 14.2 Psychotherapy durability versus medication discontinuation

There is no RCT randomizing SAD patients to psychotherapy versus continued versus discontinued pharmacotherapy with ≥2-year blinded relapse ascertainment. What exists is adjacent. The most-cited "psychotherapy is more durable" datum comes from the classic phenelzine-versus-CBGT trial: acute responders entered a 6-month maintenance phase then a 6-month treatment-free phase. Relapse *during* maintenance, while still on treatment, did not differ between arms; only in the treatment-free phase did phenelzine show a trend toward greater relapse, concentrated in generalized-subtype patients — a trend in a small responder subsample the original authors explicitly asked to be replicated. The clean randomized-withdrawal benchmark: of 437 patients entering a 12-week acute phase, 323 acute responders were randomized double-blind to continue paroxetine (n = 162) or switch to placebo (n = 161) for 24 weeks; relapse was 14% versus 39% (OR 0.24, 95% CI 0.14–0.43, p < .001). An open-label moclobemide cohort (93 patients, 59 completing 2 years) found 88% deteriorated after ≥1 month drug-free, alcohol abuse the strongest predictor of poor outcome (93.5% correct classification), absence of avoidant PD a positive predictor. A three-arm moclobemide/CBT trial to 24 months reported relapse "significantly lower" in CBT-containing groups than moclobemide-alone, without numeric percentages in the record.

The cleanest natural experiment for the spine's medication-addition warning sits inside the Blomhoff trial (n = 387, sertraline vs placebo × GP exposure vs medical care): sertraline beat non-sertraline acutely (OR 0.534, 95% CI 0.347–0.835), exposure did not separate (OR 0.732, 0.475–1.134) — but 28 weeks after medication stopped (week 52, n = 328), exposure-alone kept improving (CGI-SP change 0.45, 95% CI 0.16–0.65, p < .01) while sertraline and sertraline-plus-exposure deteriorated on SF-36 relative to exposure alone. Put together — 39% relapse in 24 weeks off an SSRI, 13% relapse over a median six years after exposure, 88% deterioration within a month of moclobemide withdrawal — the comparison is real but not randomized against itself: different populations, definitions, follow-up windows. Indicative, not causal.

### 14.3 The serious challenge to the standard durability claim

Everything above is compatible with a less flattering story: SAD drifts toward and away from its own baseline over years regardless of treatment, and within-group "gains maintained" data cannot separate an enduring effect from spontaneous remission and regression to the mean. Structurally, almost none of the long-term literature carries a comparison group forward — the five-year cognitive-therapy and five-year internet-CBT follow-ups are single-arm extensions measured against the *same* group's earlier score, and the 69-RCT meta-analysis's own headline caveat is this exact problem restated as a study count: relapse, the outcome requiring an actual comparison, is reported in just 6 of 69 trials. The one place a genuinely inactive comparator *was* followed past active treatment — the Blomhoff placebo arm at week 52 — did not deteriorate; it kept improving (0.25 point CGI-SP change, 95% CI 0.00–0.48, p < .05), smaller than but directionally identical to exposure's continued gain. A patient on an inert pill with no manualized therapy still improved on its own timeline. And the phenelzine-versus-CBGT trial supplying the field's favourite durability datum found relapse during the still-on-treatment maintenance phase did not differ between arms at all — the psychotherapy advantage appeared only as an unreplicated treatment-free-phase trend in a small subsample.

This fits the field's natural-history baseline: a 12-year prospective naturalistic follow-up found social phobia had the smallest recovery probability of any anxiety disorder studied, and patients who did recover had high recurrence — comorbid MDD and substance use lowered recovery and raised recurrence further. That is a chronic, fluctuating course in which spontaneous improvement and relapse both happen with no treatment attributed — exactly what a no-comparator "stable at five years" finding cannot rule out as the real explanation. None of this makes CT-SAD's randomized advantage over waitlist illusory. It means the *durability* claim specifically — that CBT's effect resists erosion better than medication's — rests on evidence that cannot currently distinguish an enduring psychological change from the disorder's own tendency to wax and wane, because the design that could separate them (psychotherapy vs continued vs withdrawn medication vs untreated natural history, blinded, ≥2 years) has not been run in SAD.

### 14.4 Moderators of response

**Baseline severity and comorbidity.** The tightest test (SOPHO-NET's CT arm, n = 244) found all pre-treatment variables together explained up to 37% of post-treatment LSAS variance — most of outcome is unaccounted for at intake. Baseline severity predicted worse end-state functioning and lower remission but *not* response; comorbid-diagnosis count predicted worse end-state functioning and response but *not* remission. This dissociation is the most clinically useful and most routinely collapsed finding here: a severe or comorbid patient is not less likely to *improve*, but is less likely to reach a genuinely healthy end-state — so the intake conversation should set expectations ("much better," not "symptom-free") and plan boosters, not restrict eligibility. Attrition could not be predicted at all; the authors concluded that beyond severity and comorbidity "most patients share a similar likelihood of treatment success." An 87-patient CBT-vs-ACT-vs-waitlist trial adds perceived control and extraversion predicting improvement and comorbid depression predicting worse outcome across both arms, plus one treatment-specific moderator: lower baseline psychological flexibility favoured CBT over ACT by 12 months.

**Comorbid depression is a maintenance moderator, not an acute-response moderator.** A naturalistic 219-patient CBGT cohort (pre–post ES 1.23 social anxiety, 0.94 depression; 44% generalized vs 37% specific-subtype reaching ≥50% improvement, 44% vs 87% reaching normal-range functioning) found comorbid MDD at intake did *not* predict poorer acute response but *did* predict symptom aggravation over the following year, while non-depressed patients kept improving. At intake: comorbid depression is not a reason for pessimism about therapy's course, but a flag to plan maintenance once treatment ends. A 173-patient stepped-care trial adds a counter-intuitive allocation signal — patients *with* comorbid depression did equally well on immediate face-to-face CBT or guided self-help, while patients *without* it did significantly better with immediate care — and found comorbid cluster-C PD, low social functioning and high impairment predicted worse outcome generally. A 1,249-patient UK IAPT cohort found personality-disorder screening scores independently predicted worse outcome and reduced recovery, unconfounded by severity or session count.

**Avoidant personality disorder is genuinely split.** In the tightest RCT (72% AvPD at baseline, n = 99), AvPD did not predict outcome in either format, and 82% (49/60) of AvPD patients reassessed at one year had lost the diagnosis; a 102-patient paroxetine/CT trial (54% AvPD) likewise found CT's superiority held regardless. Against this, the naturalistic exposure follow-up found remission-persistence higher without a personality disorder, the moclobemide cohort found AvPD-absence predicted better outcome, and the IAPT and stepped-care cohorts above found cluster-C features predicted worse routine-care outcome. Reading: AvPD is a real prognostic marker in naturalistic, routine-care and pharmacotherapy samples, but not a moderator inside protocolized CT-SAD trials — which also reverse the diagnosis in roughly four of five patients within a year. At intake: don't triage AvPD away from structured CT-SAD; do flag it for closer monitoring in routine or medication-only pathways.

**Attachment, temperament, expectancy, alliance.** In SOPHO-NET (n = 412), attachment did not predict dropout or differ by arm, though the highest attachment-avoidance quartile (ECR-R > 3.87) benefited more from CBT than PDT at post and follow-up; a companion analysis (n = 495) found CBT specifically reduced attachment anxiety and avoidance while PDT did not, a difference gone by 12 months. High baseline harm avoidance predicted poor outcome across *all* treatments in a temperament substudy. Age of onset and treatment expectations were "the most powerful predictors of post-treatment outcome" in an 80-patient residential trial, ahead of any diagnostic variable. The tightest data come from the 102-patient iCT-SAD trial's week-2 betas: treatment credibility (iCT β = −0.373, CT β = −0.342, both p = .01) and patient-rated alliance (iCT β = −0.470, CT β = −0.459, both p = .001) predicted outcome in both formats; therapist-rated alliance predicted outcome only in the internet arm (β = 0.349, p = .015; not CT, ns) — therapists rated the online alliance weaker (71.3 vs 77.1, p < .001) though patients did not; within iCT, frequency of practising to a virtual audience independently predicted outcome (β = −0.420, p = .003), a monitorable dose-like signal.

**Homework compliance.** A 17-study, 2,312-client meta-analysis separating quantity from quality found post-treatment effects similar (quantity g = 0.79, 95% CI 0.57–1.02; quality g = 0.78, 0.03–1.53) but diverging at follow-up (quantity g = 0.51, 0.28–0.74; quality g = 1.07, 0.06–2.08, k = 3) — a "quality matters more for durability" signal, suggestive but resting on a wide-CI, three-study estimate.

**A negative worth stating.** The largest therapygenetics GWAS to date (n = 2,724) found no SNPs, no obtainable heritability estimate, and no polygenic overlap with psychopathology, personality or learning traits — there is currently no usable genetic predictor of psychotherapy response.

### 14.5 Mediators: what actually moves the needle, and the temporal-precedence problem

This literature has four methodological grades, and confidence should scale with the grade. Grade 1 measures mediator and outcome at the same timepoints and cannot show which moved first. Grade 2 separates mid-treatment mediator from post-treatment outcome. Grade 3 tracks mediator trajectories session by session. Grade 4 randomizes the presence or absence of the component that supposedly works through the mediator — the only design that manipulates the cause.

Grade 1: the foundational mediation study (n = 90, CBGT vs cognition-free exposure group therapy vs waitlist) found estimated social cost mediated change in *both* active arms equally at post-test — not treatment-specific — but only CBGT kept improving from post-test to six months, tied to the pre–post social-cost reduction: the mediator becomes specific only for durability, not acute change. A 50-patient uncontrolled study found estimated probability, estimated cost, and acceptance of emotions carried significant indirect effects (specificity untestable, no control arm). A 75-patient CBT-vs-waitlist trial found reappraisal self-efficacy gains mediated outcome, sustained to one year; a 108-patient CBGT-vs-MBSR-vs-waitlist trial found shared mediators (reappraisal frequency, mindfulness skills, attention shifting) alongside two mediators unique to CBGT — reappraisal self-efficacy and reduced avoidance behaviours.

Grade 2 is the iCT-SAD/CT-SAD trial's process composite (negative social cognitions, safety behaviours, negative assumptions, self-focused attention): mid-treatment process change mediated the treatment-to-outcome path in both formats, accounting for roughly 60% of improvement in either arm — the strongest design here because it achieves real temporal separation.

Grade 3, a 50-patient CBT-vs-ACT trial with weekly mediator measurement, found both theorized mediators declined nonlinearly in both arms (ACT steeper early, CBT steeper late); the *curvature* of experiential-avoidance decline predicted outcome in ACT only, while negative-cognition curvature predicted outcome in *both* arms — a common factor, not CBT-specific.

Grade 4 is the single most important result in this section: 464 SAD patients randomized across 16 combinations of internet-CBT components (psychoeducation, cognitive restructuring, attention training, exposure), each present versus absent for exactly half the sample — the only randomized-cause design here. Psychoeducation and exposure replicated as the most active components; knowledge gain mediated psychoeducation, reduced safety-behaviour use mediated exposure, and exposure's effect ran through reduced safety behaviours then reduced negative social cognitions. Avoidance of social situations did *not* mediate the exposure effect despite being independently associated with outcome. And: "self-focused attention was consistently identified as an inert mechanism of change" — a direct empirical challenge, under a randomized cause, to the Clark and Wells model's most distinctive maintenance factor. This does not make self-focused-attention procedures clinically useless, but it undercuts the causal story told about why they work.

Procedure-level evidence still supports the components: single-session tests of the self-focused-attention-and-safety-behaviours experiment and the video-feedback experiment both achieved their didactic aims and reduced symptoms; single-session imagery rescripting produced reductions in trait social anxiety equivalent to cognitive restructuring via different pathways, and shifted the validity of memory-derived negative core beliefs about self and others — but not about the world — one week later. The standing demonstration of why temporal precedence matters is a 137-patient CBGT-vs-mindfulness/acceptance-group trial: both reduced self-focused attention and safety behaviours equally, and mediation supported *both* directions at once — safety behaviours mediating self-focused attention's effect on anxiety, and vice versa — in both arms. When one dataset supports two mutually reversible causal orderings, the design cannot adjudicate, and most SAD mediation work shares this design.

### 14.6 Dose-response, session count, and guidance intensity

There is no dose-finding RCT of session number for CT-SAD.
