The most precise therapist-time accounting comes from the 102-patient iCT-SAD/CT-SAD/waitlist trial: face-to-face delivered 12.8 (SD 2.1) weekly 90-minute sessions (18.4 hours) plus boosters (2.8 hours); internet delivered 12.4 short calls (2.8h) plus 2.5 video calls (1.3h) plus messaging, totalling ~6.8h to week 14. By end of treatment, internet patients achieved a 45.5-point LSAS drop after 6.45 therapist-hours versus 15.8 hours for face-to-face — 2.45× more change per hour. Effect sizes versus waitlist were large in both (d = 2.20 internet, 2.38 face-to-face), no significant format difference at any timepoint (d 0.22–0.31), and equivalence testing rejected >25% inferiority for internet delivery. Loss of diagnosis ran 72–91% and IAPT recovery 59–82% with no format difference, zero dropouts in either active arm versus 9% deterioration on waitlist. The paper's own underdelivery benchmark: routine IAPT services average 7.5 sessions against CT-SAD's protocolized allowance of up to 14.

Guidance intensity gives genuinely mixed answers by outcome. An 81-patient unguided-vs-email-guided-vs-on-demand trial found large within-group effects (d = 1.47 primary) but essentially no between-group difference (d .00–.36) and no difference in dropout. A newer 164-patient guided-app-vs-unguided-vs-waitlist trial found equivalent LSAS effect sizes (d = −1.10 vs −1.01, between-arm d = 0.04) but starkly different dropout (9.3% waitlist, 15% guided, 42.6% unguided) — the authors' own reading: guidance's value is retention, not effect size. Combination pharmacotherapy gets its cleanest negative from a 295-patient fluoxetine/CBGT/placebo/combination trial: CGI response 50.9%/51.7%/54.2%/50.8% (active arms) versus 31.7% (placebo) — every active treatment beat placebo, combination added nothing, and "many patients remained symptomatic after 14 weeks." A companion analysis of that trial's refusals found comorbid depression the leading exclusion and unwillingness to take medication the leading reason for declining participation — a generalizability finding bearing directly on the effectiveness gap below, since such trials under-recruit exactly the medication-averse, depression-comorbid patients who fill routine caseloads.

### 14.7 Sudden gains

Reported rates are inconsistent enough that no single "sudden-gain rate" should be quoted for SAD. The earliest, largest-sample study (107 patients, 967 session intervals) found only 22 intervals met criteria, peaking near session 5; cognitive change did not precede the gains, leading the authors to question their clinical significance altogether. A 67-patient CT-vs-IPT trial found 22.4% had a sudden gain, with lower post-treatment and follow-up anxiety, similar frequency/timing across arms but lower resulting symptoms in CT — cognitive change again did not precede the gain; instead the gain *produced* subsequent cognitive change, the reverse of the theoretical prediction. The largest rates come from the 99-patient iCT-SAD/CT-SAD RCT: 64% (CT) and 51% (iCT) had sudden gains, preceded specifically by reductions in negative social cognitions and self-focused attention (not by prior depression reduction) and by rated evidence of "generalised learning" in the preceding sessions, with no CT-vs-iCT difference. A 45-student trial comparing CBGT with a deliberately non-specific comparator found 22.2% had sudden gains with no overall outcome advantage — but *greater* improvement when gains occurred in the non-specific arm, and most CBGT gains occurred before specific techniques were even delivered, undercutting a specific-technique account. Across four internet-delivered samples (SAD n = 175, plus GAD/panic/MDD), sudden gains were equally prevalent under guided and self-guided delivery, clustered at weeks 2–3, and were associated with improved outcome in SAD and MDD but not GAD or panic — directly refuting a therapist-required-for-sustained-gains theory. One trial found *pretreatment* symptom variability (measured during waitlist) predicted who would go on to have a sudden gain, correctly classifying 83–84% of patients — a state marker present before therapy began.

### 14.8 Therapist effects, competence, and adherence

SAD-specific data is almost nonexistent: the field's only explicit statement, from the 62-patient CT-vs-exposure trial, is that "therapist effects were small and nonsignificant," with no ICC reported. The one detailed competence measurement comes from the iCT-SAD/CT-SAD trial's deliberate cross-over design (three experienced therapists, each treating a third of each arm, so therapist is not confounded with format): blinded expert ratings of a randomly selected videotape from every face-to-face patient scored a mean 4.92 (SD 0.47) on the 0–6 Cognitive Therapy Competence Rating Scale for Social Phobia, no session below 4 — "good" to "excellent" throughout; internet-arm competence could not be assessed at all, as no measure existed. Whether less-experienced routine-service therapists can replicate this remains untested by the authors' own statement.

General (non-SAD) evidence on rated adherence and competence is a near-uniform null: a 36-study meta-analysis found mean weighted adherence–outcome r = .02 and competence–outcome r = .07, both non-significant. A Swedish routine-care study (29 therapists, 85 patients; competence 40.5 via standardized-patient role-play, 76% above threshold; patient improvement d = 0.80–1.36) found "neither therapist adherence nor competence was associated with patient outcomes." Multilevel variance-partitioning studies converge on a small range: a three-level model (26,888 patients, 462 therapists, 30 clinics) found clinic effects (8.2% unadjusted) exceeding therapist effects (3.2%), therapist variance essentially unchanged after adjustment (3.4%) while clinic variance shrank to 1.9%. A larger cross-classified model (1.39 million episodes, 55 clinics) found similarly small clinic (2–5%) and neighbourhood (1–2%) effects, deprivation explaining most neighbourhood but not clinic variance. In low-intensity care, ICCs were 0–1.3% with therapist effects not detectably different from each other; and across 5,828 patients and 158 therapists, the gap between high- and low-performing therapists *grew* with treatment duration (interaction coefficient 0.071, p < .001) — a therapist effect negligible in brief protocols that becomes material in longer ones. Overall: therapist effects sit at roughly 0–5% of variance, smaller than clinic effects, near-zero in low-intensity formats, growing with length, unpredicted by rated adherence or competence — and for SAD specifically this whole evidence base is borrowed from other conditions.

Whether non-specialists can deliver CT-SAD at all remains largely untested. A UK CAMHS training programme for an adolescent adaptation had its planned head-to-head RCT become infeasible from staff turnover and unfilled posts; the resulting training case series (8 therapists, 12 adolescents) found 9/12 good outcomes at an estimated £1,861 per person, with real implementation challenges reported. A single-arm Japanese transportability trial (n = 15) reported a very large within-group effect (d = 3.66), 80% reliable improvement, 60% remission, 7% dropout; a confirmatory two-arm RCT (n = 60) has since been registered. The dose-anchoring trial is best read as an implementation result: the same three therapists delivered both formats, isolating format from skill, and the internet format strips out the features services find hardest to deliver — bringing therapist time into the range of routine-service averages, though whether less-experienced therapists can reproduce it is explicitly unanswered.

### 14.9 The effectiveness gap: routine care against efficacy trials, and cost-effectiveness

The best-evidenced general statement about transportability comes from a 56-study benchmarking meta-analysis of anxiety CBT (not SAD-specific): pre–post effects uniformly large, only 6 of 56 studies controlled (those favoured CBT with large effects), effectiveness-study results falling within the efficacy-trial range, and a real but "quite small" inverse relationship between clinical representativeness and outcome. The single best-evidenced statement this manual can make about the effectiveness gap generally: it exists, runs in the expected direction, and is small.

The SAD-specific test is cleaner still: the identical 12-session CBGT protocol delivered in a research unit (n = 71) versus a community clinic (n = 94) — community patients more severe, more comorbid, more often male, medicated and less educated — showed no difference in degree of improvement, completer or intention-to-treat. But a national routine-care dataset shows a gap large enough that both findings must be held together: 565 patients receiving therapist-guided internet CBT across four Norwegian hospitals achieved social-anxiety response of 26.6% and remission of 35.6% — against loss of diagnosis of 72–91% and IAPT-defined recovery of 59–82% in the expert-delivered trial anchoring this section. The comparison is non-randomized and uses different definitions, but the order-of-magnitude size of the gap is the honest framing of the implementation problem, larger than the "small" transportability gap above would predict. That Norwegian cohort still showed real secondary benefit — sick leave fell 3.2 days post-treatment and 7.7 days at 6 months, EQ-5D-5L utility rose 0.11–0.12 — at a program cost of $1,030 per patient (SD 452, range $636–$2,152 by site). A pragmatic non-inferiority trial inside UK IAPT (n = 510, not SAD-specific) offers a directly relevant caution: two therapies were non-inferior at 6 months, but a significant CBT advantage emerged by 12 months (adjusted difference 1.73, 95% CI 0.26–3.19) — invisible at the shorter horizon most routine-care trials use.

The cost-effectiveness case for individual cognitive therapy rests explicitly on the durability assumption this section has spent three subsections complicating. A NICE decision-analytic model spanning 28 SAD interventions, built on a 101-trial, 13,164-participant network (individual CBT SMD −1.19, 95% CrI −1.56 to −0.81 vs waitlist, falling to −0.56, −1.00 to −0.11, vs psychological placebo; SSRIs/SNRIs falling from −0.91 to −0.44 against matched controls), ranked individual cognitive therapy most cost-effective, then generic CBT, then phenelzine, then unsupported bibliotherapy. Its authors state their results were "driven by limited evidence suggesting superiority of psychological interventions over drugs in retaining long-term effects" — the load-bearing input is exactly the durability claim examined above — and note drug side effects were not modelled, which would only strengthen CBT's advantage if included. A 30-month economic evaluation alongside SOPHO-NET found the probability CBT was more cost-effective than psychodynamic therapy rose from 65% at zero willingness-to-pay per anxiety-free day to 83% at €10 and 96% at €30 — the longest economic horizon here, though CBT-vs-PDT rather than CBT-vs-medication. Cost benchmarks worth holding together: guided internet CBT in Norwegian routine care, ~$1,030/patient; adolescent CT-SAD via UK CAMHS training, ~£1,861/person; within one RCT, internet delivery matching face-to-face CT-SAD's LSAS reduction using 6.45 therapist-hours against 15.8.

## 15. Controversies, the live pipeline, and what would settle them

A textbook chapter that ends with a tidy summary of "what works" would misrepresent this literature. Social anxiety disorder is treated with a psychotherapy — individual cognitive therapy on the Clark and Wells model — whose supporting evidence is simultaneously the strongest in the field and among the most contested on methodological grounds. This closing section takes the contest seriously: what the biggest specific threats to validity actually cost in effect-size terms, what is currently enrolling and what it will settle, and what trials still do not exist.

### 15.1 The waitlist problem, quantified — and what it actually indicts

The single most load-bearing number in this domain comes from Mayo-Wilson and colleagues' network meta-analysis built for NICE: 101 trials, 13,164 participants, 41 interventions collapsed into 17 classes, published and unpublished, spanning 1988 to September 2013. Because it is a network, every class can be read against two different reference conditions simultaneously — a waitlist and a psychological or pill placebo — and the comparison is damning in a very specific way. Individual CBT falls from an SMD of −1.19 (95% credible interval −1.56 to −0.81) against waitlist to −0.56 (95% CrI −1.00 to −0.11) against psychological placebo: a 53% reduction in apparent effect from switching the comparator alone. SSRIs and SNRIs show almost exactly the same shrinkage, from −0.91 against waitlist to −0.44 against pill placebo — a 52% fall. But the network's more important finding is not the shrinkage; it is what survives it. Among the ten classes for which a placebo-controlled estimate is reported here, individual CBT and the antidepressants (SSRIs/SNRIs) were the *only* two that beat their matched active placebo — the network's other three pharmacological classes (MAOIs, benzodiazepines, anticonvulsants) carry no reported placebo comparison to check this against. Group CBT (−0.92 vs waitlist), exposure plus social skills training (−0.86), supported self-help (−0.86), unsupported self-help (−0.75), and psychodynamic psychotherapy (−0.62) each posted a large, clean effect against waitlist and then failed to separate from psychological placebo. And even individual CBT's surviving effect is narrow: its placebo-controlled credible interval, −1.00 to −0.11, comes close to touching zero.

This reframes what the waitlist critique should be read as saying. It is not a reason to disbelieve individual cognitive therapy specifically — that treatment is the one class that clears the higher bar. It is a reason to be skeptical of every other psychological modality's apparent superiority to doing nothing, because none of the other five classes has been shown to beat an active comparator.

Why does the waitlist inflate at all? Furukawa and colleagues' network meta-analysis of 49 RCTs and 2,730 participants in acute depression found that people assigned to literally no treatment did better than people assigned to a waiting list — an odds ratio of response favoring no-treatment over waitlist of 2.9 (95% CI 1.3–5.7) — and titled the paper around the idea that the waiting list functions as a nocebo condition. This must be reported with the authors' own caveats attached, because they are severe: evidence quality including publication bias was rated less than ideal, none of the pre-planned sensitivity analyses restricted to high-quality studies could actually be run, and the significant difference did not survive the post hoc adjustment for publication bias. The nocebo mechanism is plausible and the SAD-specific magnitude gap above is real and independently documented; the specific claim that a waiting list is actively harmful is evidence that failed its own robustness check.

Two further syntheses corroborate the scale of the control-condition effect without adding the nocebo claim. Cuijpers and colleagues' unified series of meta-analyses — 375 trials, 423 CBT-versus-control comparisons, 32,968 patients across eleven disorders, uniformly extracted with Cochrane risk-of-bias 2 — reports SAD falling in the g 0.5–1.0 band overall, but states plainly that waitlist-controlled comparisons produced g greater than 0.94 while care-as-usual comparisons produced 0.22 to 1.13: the type of control condition, not the treatment, is doing most of the work in separating effect sizes across the literature. Scholten and colleagues' individual-patient-data meta-analysis — 1,246 patients pooled across twelve studies, mean age 35.3 (SD 10.9), 59.2% female — is the best patient-level estimate available for CBT in SAD specifically: LSAS change of b = −20.3 (95% CI −24.9 to −15.6), Cohen's d = −0.95 (95% CI −1.16 to −0.73), moderated by baseline severity (very severe SAD d = −1.13, mild SAD d = −0.54). Its own inclusion criterion, however, was randomized trials comparing CBT with being on a waiting list — the d of −0.95 is a waitlist-anchored number by construction, not an estimate of CBT's advantage over credible care. The most recent conventional SAD-specific meta-analysis, de Ponti and colleagues (66 RCTs, 5,560 participants, 98 comparisons, search to January 2024), lands at g = 0.88 (95% CI 0.76–1.00), I² = 74%, NNT 3.8 — and states outright that there was significant risk of bias across the included trials and significant moderation by delivery format, recruitment strategy, target group, and session count.

### 15.2 Researcher allegiance

Dragioti and colleagues took 30 Cochrane meta-analyses covering 240 RCTs and compared allegiant against non-allegiant trials directly: summary relative odds ratio 1.31 (95% CI 1.03–1.66), I² = 53% — allegiant investigators reported larger effects. Four qualifiers matter. The effect was significant for every psychotherapy modality except CBT itself. It was significant only when treatment integrity of the delivered therapy went unassessed. It was strongest specifically where the investigator had developed the preferred treatment and also supervised or trained the therapists delivering it. And standard risk-of-bias tools found no quality difference between allegiant and non-allegiant studies at all — the bias is invisible to the instruments normally used to catch it.

Is this a real effect, or does it merely reflect that allegiant investigators happen to back the better treatment? Munder and colleagues tested this directly by restricting analysis to comparisons where the true-efficacy hypothesis predicts zero allegiance-outcome association — 29 head-to-head comparisons of different versions of trauma-focused therapy for PTSD, already known to be roughly equivalent. Allegiance still significantly predicted outcome and explained 12% of the variance. The true-efficacy explanation is not supported here; allegiance should be treated as causal, not epiphenomenal.

That said, the allegiance literature has a genuine negative replication that must sit alongside it. Schünemann and colleagues re-examined the German Scientific Board's corpus of humanistic-versus-other-psychotherapy comparisons — a dataset where the majority of studies showed *non*-allegiance toward the humanistic arm and only about half the humanistic interventions were bona fide treatments, i.e. the biasing conditions were abundantly present — and found allegiance a significant moderator in only 2 of 5 outcome comparisons, bona fide status in only 1 of 5. Their own conclusion states there was no clear evidence of allegiance or treatment quality impacting outcome in that re-examination; type of active control and disorder group (anxiety disorders specifically) emerged as moderators instead.

Why this matters most acutely in SAD: every trial establishing individual cognitive therapy's superiority over group formats — Stangier's 2003 individual-versus-group comparison, Mörtberg's 2007 three-arm trial, Stangier's 2011 CT-versus-IPT trial, and Ingul's 2014 adolescent trial — was run by Clark or by Clark-trained investigators, with Clark himself a co-author on most. This is precisely Dragioti's maximum-allegiance configuration: developer of the preferred treatment supervising the therapists who deliver it.

### 15.3 Blinding — the impossibility, what has actually been tried, and a genuine surprise

No SAD psychotherapy trial blinds participants or therapists to their assigned treatment; that is simply not achievable when one arm sits in a waiting room and the other does exposure exercises with a therapist. The field's universal substitute is the independent, blinded outcome assessor: Stangier's 2011 CT-versus-IPT trial used independent masked evaluators for its CGI-Improvement primary outcome; the SOPHO-NET trial used LSAS raters blind to group assignment; a VR-exposure registry protocol (NCT05302518) states its actual ceiling explicitly — participants are blind to their treatment assignment only until their first treatment session, and not at any assessment after that. That single visit is what "blinding" concretely means in this field. Secondary devices exist — attentional-placebo arms, psychological-placebo arms within network analyses, and waitlist-plus-blinded-rater three-arm designs such as Arad and colleagues' 105-participant trial comparing gaze-contingent music reward therapy against an SSRI and a waitlist — but none of them blinds the participant to whether they are receiving an active intervention.

The received wisdom is that this unblinding inflates psychotherapy's apparent advantage whenever it is compared head-to-head with medication. Cuijpers and colleagues tested this directly across 35 trials and 3,721 depressed patients comparing psychotherapy with pharmacotherapy, splitting by whether a placebo arm (and hence any blinding) was present. Trials with a placebo condition found no significant difference between the two treatment types (g = −0.07, NNT 25); trials without one found a small but significant difference — favoring pharmacotherapy (g = −0.13, NNT 14). The moderator test between the two sets was not significant, but the direction is the opposite of the received story: if anything, unblinding modestly favored the drug, not the therapy. This is one of the rare places a well-motivated methodological worry has been directly tested and failed to run the expected direction.

The deeper problem is that blinding is barely ever *measured* anywhere in psychiatric trials, medication trials included. Lin and colleagues searched double-blind antidepressant RCTs published 2010–2020 and found that of 154 eligible studies, only 11 (7.1%) reported any blinding assessment whatsoever. Pooling those with five older studies gave agreement between patient guesses and truth of κ = 0.21 (95% CI 0.14–0.28) and assessor guesses of κ = 0.17 (95% CI 0.05–0.30) — both low — and blinding success was not associated with effect size (meta-regression β = 0.06, p = 0.09). The honest framing is not "psychotherapy trials are uniquely unblinded"; it is that almost nobody in psychiatry checks whether blinding actually held, and the one subfield that checked found no evidence that blinding failure moved effect sizes.

### 15.4 Publication bias and small-study effects

The only direct empirical measurement of psychotherapy publication bias comes from outside SAD but is methodologically decisive. Driessen and colleagues tracked every NIH grant that funded a psychotherapy RCT for major depression from 1972 to 2008 and chased down what happened to each one: 13 of 55 funded trials that began enrollment (23.6%) never produced any publication at all. Published comparisons showed g = 0.52 (95% CI 0.37–0.68, k = 20); unpublished trials showed g = 0.20 (95% CI −0.11 to 0.51, k = 6) — a confidence interval that includes zero. The combined estimate, g = 0.39, is a 25% reduction from the published-only figure, and the authors are explicit that even this is a floor, since outcome-reporting bias within trials that did publish could not be quantified.

SAD-specific funnel-plot evidence is thinner but consistent in direction. Wersebe, Sijbrandij, and Cuijpers's meta-analysis of group CBT for SAD (11 RCTs, pooled effect 0.53, 96% CI 0.33–0.73, NNT 3.24) states there was "some indication of publication bias" but provides no trim-and-fill-adjusted estimate. Walder and colleagues' meta-analysis of digital mental health interventions for youth social anxiety (22 studies, 21 meta-analyzed) is the one place a SAD-adjacent adjusted estimate actually exists: overall Hedges' g = 0.508 (95% CI 0.308–0.707) before adjustment, g = 0.506 (95% CI 0.308–0.707) after — essentially unchanged, showing no meaningful small-study inflation in that particular subfield. On the pharmacotherapy side, Stein, Ipser, and van Balkom's Cochrane review of 36 RCTs and 4,268 participants states a funnel plot provided evidence of publication bias, with pooled non-response RR 0.63 (95% CI 0.55–0.72) and LSAS weighted mean difference −15.56 (95% CI −17.95 to −13.16) — the funnel-asymmetry critique applies to the drug literature in this disorder too, not only to psychotherapy. A useful methodological analogue from outside SAD: Cuijpers and colleagues' EMDR meta-analysis found only 4 of 27 studies at low risk of bias, and EMDR's apparent superiority to other therapies (g = 0.36, 95% CI 0.14–0.57) disappeared entirely when restricted to the low-risk-of-bias subset. No sensitivity analysis of that kind — asking whether individual cognitive therapy's advantage survives restriction to low-risk-of-bias trials — has been run for SAD.

### 15.5 Risk of bias: the honest picture

There is no single published figure for the proportion of SAD psychotherapy trials rated at high risk of bias across the field. What exists is fragmentary and consistently unflattering. de Ponti's 2024 meta-analysis states there was "evidence for significant risk of bias in the included trials" without a retrievable percentage. Cuijpers's 2025 unified series concludes "the quality of the evidence is limited," again without a SAD-specific breakdown. The one retrievable numeric breakdown comes from Walder's youth digital-intervention review: 16 of 22 studies (73%) some concerns, 3 of 22 (14%) high risk, 3 of 22 (14%) low risk. Van Dis and colleagues' long-term-outcome meta-analysis across six anxiety-related disorders (69 RCTs, 4,118 outpatients) describes the literature as "mainly of low quality," reports SAD-specific Hedges' g of 0.34–0.60 from post-treatment through 6–12 months and g = 0.42 at 12 months or beyond — drawn from only three studies — and notes relapse rates were reported in a mere 6 of the 69 RCTs, predominantly for panic disorder rather than SAD. Olthuis and colleagues' Cochrane review of therapist-supported internet CBT (38 studies, 3,214 participants, SAD the largest single diagnostic block at 11 trials) graded every effect estimate low or very low quality and states directly that "blinding of participants and personnel, and outcome assessment tended to have a high risk of bias" and that "adverse events were rarely reported." That last point deserves its own sentence rather than a subordinate clause: the harms of psychotherapy for social anxiety disorder are not small in this literature — they are essentially unmeasured.

### 15.6 Is "CBT" one treatment? Is individual cognitive therapy genuinely superior, or is that an allegiance artefact?

The field's own taxonomy already answers the first question in the negative. Mayo-Wilson's network split "CBT" into distinct classes precisely because they behave differently — individual CBT at −1.19 against waitlist, unsupported self-help at −0.75 — and de Ponti's meta-regression found delivery format, recruitment strategy, target group, and session count all significantly moderating outcome across 98 comparisons. Two further analyses test the coherence of the "CBT" label directly. Cuijpers and colleagues pooled 42 trials using generic anxiety instruments and found BAI point change of 13.33 (95% CI 10.58–16.07) for panic disorder against 5.92 (4.64–7.20) for SAD and 6.06 (3.96–8.16) for GAD — panic significantly larger (p = 0.001), robust to adjustment for baseline severity, with the authors flagging high risk of bias in most included studies. The same nominal treatment, applied to different disorders, yields radically different effect sizes. Montero-Marin and colleagues' 50-study, 2,801-patient comparison of CBT against relaxation therapy found an overall advantage for CBT (g = −0.27, 95% CI −0.41 to −0.13, NNT 6.61) but no significant difference for social anxiety disorder specifically, and the moderators that together explained 82% of the variance were age, hours of therapy, quality of the intervention, treatment format, and disorder type — not the CBT-versus-relaxation label itself. In SAD, "CBT" does not separate from relaxation on the field's own numbers, and what predicts outcome is dose and delivery quality.

The counter-evidence deserves equal weight. Kindred and colleagues' 25-study analysis of long-term CBT outcomes for SAD found post-treatment effects of g = 0.74 for social anxiety, and — critically — moderation was non-significant for treatment model, format, number of sessions, treatment duration, or booster sessions. Protocol heterogeneity is visible in some analyses and invisible in others; the honest statement is that it has never been assessed by direct coding of what was actually delivered session to session, only by comparing labels.

The individual-cognitive-therapy-versus-generic-CBT question is best adjudicated trial by trial. Stangier's 2003 comparison (71 randomized, 65 assessed post-treatment, 59 at six months) found individual cognitive therapy superior to the group version of the identical treatment protocol at both endpoints. Mörtberg's 2007 three-arm trial (n = 100) found individual cognitive therapy superior to both an intensive group format and treatment-as-usual, with the group and treatment-as-usual arms not differing from each other. Ingul's 2014 adolescent trial found individual cognitive therapy superior to both group CBT and an attentional placebo — but its arms held roughly 19 participants each, and the finding that group CBT did not beat attentional placebo in that trial is badly underpowered and should not be read as evidence that group CBT is inert. Stangier's 2011 trial (117 randomized) found post-treatment response of 65.8% for cognitive therapy against 42.1% for interpersonal psychotherapy and 7.3% for waitlist, sustained at one year (68.4% versus 31.6%). And the largest trial of them all, SOPHO-NET — 495 patients randomized across CBT, psychodynamic therapy, and waitlist, with blinded LSAS raters — found remission of 36% for CBT, 26% for psychodynamic therapy, and 9% for waitlist, with CBT superior to psychodynamic therapy for remission but *not* for response (60% versus 52%), and its own authors describing the between-group effect sizes as small. Its 24-month follow-up is the number that most complicates the superiority story: response converged to roughly 70% and remission to roughly 40% in both arms, with no significant between-treatment differences after six months.

Four things have to be held together here. The individual-cognitive-therapy-superiority finding is real and replicated across four independent samples with blinded assessors. It is also produced entirely by one research lineage — Stangier, Mörtberg, and Ingul are all Clark-co-authored or Clark-trained, which is exactly Dragioti's maximum-allegiance configuration, and there is no head-to-head trial of the Clark and Wells model against Heimberg-style cognitive-behavioral group therapy run by an investigator group with balanced or opposing allegiance. Ingul's underpowered arms should not be cited as proof group CBT is inert. And the advantage that does replicate is an acute-phase advantage: SOPHO-NET, the field's largest trial, shows it dissolving by two years. A working-alliance analysis within one of these samples (n = 54) found alliance stronger in individual than group therapy but generally unrelated to outcome — which undercuts the most obvious non-specific explanation (better therapeutic relationship in a one-on-one room) without resolving the allegiance question.

Economic modeling built on the same evidence base still ranks individual cognitive therapy first. Mavranezouli and colleagues' NICE decision-analytic model across 28 interventions, NHS/PSS perspective, ranked individual cognitive therapy first, generic individual CBT second, phenelzine third, and book-based self-help without support fourth — while noting explicitly that the ranking was influenced by limited evidence for psychotherapy's superiority in retaining long-term effects, and that drug side effects were not modeled. The granular economics from inside SOPHO-NET make the trade concrete: unadjusted incremental cost-effectiveness ratios of €3,615 per responder for CBT against €4,958 for psychodynamic therapy, and €5,788 per remitted patient against €10,733; CBT was cost-effective at 97.5% probability only above a willingness-to-pay of roughly €16,100 per responder. And the SOPHO-NET economists state the waitlist-contamination problem outright, from inside a SAD trial: the waiting-list condition is assumed to represent untreated patients, "although the expectation to start treatment in the near future probably affects symptom severity and health care utilization" — an admission that belongs equally in section 15.1.

### 15.7 The treatment gap and implementation failure

The sharpest finding in this entire chapter is not about efficacy at all. Chapdelaine and colleagues' "Dialogue" project screened 14,833 people across 67 Quebec primary care clinics, interviewed 1,956, and identified 289 meeting DSM-IV criteria for SAD. Of those 289, 86.2% had consulted for mental health reasons in the past twelve months — these were not people outside the health system. Yet only 23.6% had SAD detected by a healthcare professional in that period, and only about two in five received pharmacotherapy or psychotherapy meeting guideline-based adequacy indicators (antidepressants were the most common treatment given). The most important number in the whole study is this: detection of major depression predicted adequate treatment (OR 4.651, 95% CI 2.559–8.453) and detection of another anxiety disorder did too (OR 2.957, 95% CI 1.555–5.625) — but detection of social anxiety disorder itself did not (OR 1.420, 95% CI 0.696–2.899). Recognizing this specific disorder, in patients already engaged with the health system, did not raise the odds of them receiving evidence-based care.

The population-level gap is larger still. Suhas and colleagues' analysis of India's National Mental Health Survey — 34,802 adults across 12 states, MINI 6.0.0 structured interview — found current SAD prevalence of 0.47%, disability in work for 63%, social life for 77%, family life for 68% of cases, comorbidity in 58%, and a treatment gap of 82%: more than four in five people meeting criteria received no treatment at all. The authors flag their own finding of higher SAD odds among males as anomalous against most Western epidemiology, a caution against importing this prevalence estimate uncritically elsewhere.

What scaled delivery can achieve when it is actually implemented is documented in the Norwegian Prompt Mental Health Care program, the Norwegian adaptation of IAPT. Knapstad and Smith's secondary analysis of that randomized trial (70:30 allocation to the program versus treatment-as-usual) found 61.3% of all clients (n = 472) scored at caseness for SAD, with sustained between-group effects at six and twelve months — SPIN-9 avoidance and physiological discomfort d = −0.60 (95% CI −0.94 to −0.26), ATQ-SA cognitions d = −0.45 (95% CI −0.70 to −0.20). The implementation datum sits alongside it: only one in five of these clients had sought help in the past twelve months before the program existed. And efficiency, not just access, is the mechanism that may actually close the gap: the internet-delivered cognitive therapy trial for SAD (section 15.6, and detailed with full operational parameters elsewhere in this manual) achieved the same symptom change as face-to-face cognitive therapy using 6.45 hours of therapist time against 15.8 hours — more than doubling clinical benefit per therapist hour, with the underlying cognitive-model process variables mediating 60% of improvement in either format. That the mechanism transfers out of the trial is corroborated in routine practice: Yim and colleagues followed 193 patients treated with internet cognitive therapy inside NHS Talking Therapies (146 analyzable) and found 70 sudden gains occurring in 57 participants — a 39% occurrence rate — associated with larger LSAS reduction at both end of treatment and three-month follow-up.

This creates a genuine, unresolved tension the field has not reconciled. The most cost-effective treatment by NICE's own model is individual cognitive therapy — the most therapist-intensive option on the menu. The treatment-gap data say the binding constraint is exactly therapist hours and detection-to-treatment conversion, not the existence of an efficacious protocol. Those two conclusions pull in opposite directions.

### 15.8 The registry sweep: the live pipeline

#### What is currently enrolling, and when it reads out

ClinicalTrials.gov currently lists 81 SAD-psychotherapy-family trials as recruiting, not-yet-recruiting, active-not-recruiting, or enrolling, against 209 that have completed. Ordered by planned size, the trials most likely to move this field between 2026 and 2029 are a mix of mechanism studies and genuinely comparative designs. The largest, NCT05467683 (Jasper A. Smits, RECRUITING, n=600, primary completion 2026-12-01), is testing CO₂ reactivity as a biomarker of non-response to exposure-based therapy across OCD, PTSD, GAD, SAD, and panic disorder — a prediction study, not a treatment comparison, but the largest single mechanism trial in the pipeline. NCT06942429 (Region Skåne, RECRUITING, n=556, primary completion 2029-11-05) is a pragmatic non-inferiority trial of stepped versus stratified care in youth anxiety, comparing internet-delivered CBT against personalized in-person CBT — the largest ongoing comparative-modality trial and the one most directly relevant to the therapist-hours question in section 15.7. NCT05858346 (VA Office of Research and Development, RECRUITING, n=264, primary completion 2027-04-01) compares Transdiagnostic Behavior Therapy against disorder-specific CBT for veterans with SAD and comorbid PTSD symptoms — the closest thing in the pipeline to a genuine transdiagnostic-versus-specific head-to-head. NCT05683223 (Boston University, RECRUITING, n=240, primary completion 2027-06-30) is notable for combining neural markers with a sequential design across group CBT, individual CBT, and sertraline for non-responders — but because non-responders are stepped rather than randomized between formats, it will not deliver an unbiased group-versus-individual comparison, which the field still lacks outside the allegiance-saturated lineage described in section 15.6. NCT07192367 (Emine Kanmaz, RECRUITING, n=56, PHASE4, primary completion 2026-08) is one of the very few direct randomized comparisons of virtual reality exposure therapy against sertraline — a genuine psychotherapy-versus-pharmacotherapy design, rare in this literature since the 2000s, reading out within the year. A cluster of smaller mechanism trials worth naming for what they specifically test: NCT06678295 (University of Washington, RECRUITING, n=160, primary completion 2028-09-30) is testing self-focused attention as an early-warning indicator of CBT non-response in SAD and body dysmorphic disorder; NCT07666776 (Tel Aviv University, RECRUITING, n=90, primary completion 2026-10-30) is testing whether the attentional-bias-modification effect survives an active placebo control, which speaks directly to whether that mechanism is real or an artifact of comparator choice.

Three records in the registry are internally inconsistent enough to flag rather than cite as expected readouts. NCT06562010 (Universiti Putra Malaysia, culturally adapted internet cognitive therapy for SAD in Pakistan) registers a planned n of 800 with a primary completion date of 2025-02-05 — but its status is still NOT_YET_RECRUITING. An 800-participant trial cannot complete in the time window its own record specifies while never having started; this should not be treated as a trial that will actually deliver 800 participants of data. NCT06352463 (Leiden University, combined parent-child treatment for children with SAD) is RECRUITING with an estimated n of 9 across three sites — an enrollment failure recorded as an active trial, not a trial nearing its planned size. NCT06214039 (Vilnius University, VR exposure for public speaking anxiety) is TERMINATED with only 37 of a calculated target of 86 enrolled, and its record still carries a future primary completion date of 2026-02-14 despite the termination — a registry bookkeeping artifact, not a live readout to expect.

#### The publication-bias flags: completed and silent

Of a sample of nine substantial completed SAD-psychotherapy trials checked individually for posted results, five had none. The most consequential is SoREAL (NCT03845101, Mental Health Centre Copenhagen), a five-site, 302-participant assessor-blinded superiority trial comparing group CBT with virtual-reality exposure against group CBT with in-vivo exposure for SAD and agoraphobia — the largest VR-versus-in-vivo exposure trial ever mounted in this disorder. Its own pre-registered dissemination policy commits to publishing "regardless of the magnitude or direction of effect." Its primary completion date was 2024-04-09; the record has since lapsed to UNKNOWN status with no results posted. If VR exposure had shown a clear advantage over in-vivo exposure, that result would very likely already be in print. NCT01654510 (University of Michigan, systems-level group CBT versus vocational services for unemployed adults with SAD, n=165) completed in March 2012 and has posted no results roughly thirteen years later. NCT05138068 — SAMATI, the MDMA-assisted therapy trial for generalized SAD discussed in section 15.9 — completed 2025-08-12 with no results posted at the time of this sweep. NCT05302518 (Region of Southern Denmark, biosignal-guided adaptive VR exposure versus in-vivo exposure, n=80) completed 2025-01-04, roughly a year unposted. NCT03428490 (UCLA/NIAAA, integrated CBT for comorbid SAD and alcohol use disorder, n=56) completed 2021-04-30, roughly five years unposted. The pattern across all five is not random: completed-but-silent trials cluster precisely in the comparisons where a null result would be commercially or academically unwelcome — VR against the established in-vivo standard, MDMA, and integrated-care models competing against standard practice. With 209 completed trials in the registry and result-posting the exception rather than the rule, this field's null-result reservoir is effectively invisible.

#### Terminated, withdrawn, and what stopped them

Thirteen psychotherapy or psychotherapy-augmentation trials in the registry are marked TERMINATED, and the pattern in why is informative rather than random. Bias-modification paradigms fail hardest: NCT03764644 (Erasmus Medical Center, web-based attention bias modification plus CBT for childhood anxiety including SAD, n=55) was stopped because interim results showed high futility to continue — a rare case where the registry states the actual reason a mechanism trial died. Augmentation pharmacology bolted onto exposure fails almost as consistently: NCT01574014 (Bern, hydrocortisone-augmented group CBT, n=66) terminated for recruiting problems; NCT02099825 (Washington University, d-cycloserine plus mifepristone, n=15) terminated for COVID and staffing issues; NCT03743571 (University of Nevada Reno, tDCS-augmented exposure therapy, n=33) terminated for COVID-related distancing requirements, though it did post results. The starkest single case is NCT02790736 (New York State Psychiatric Institute, propranolol reconsolidation blockade for fear of public speaking, PHASE4) — terminated at n=6 for COVID-19 restrictions. This is the *entire* registered evidence base for reconsolidation blockade in social anxiety: there is no adequately powered completed trial of this mechanism in the disorder. NCT03683823 and NCT03135990 — VR trials at UT Austin and Weill Cornell, enrolling 21 and 19 participants — were both terminated by COVID-19 disruption in 2020. NCT03465137, a neuroscience-mechanism trial at Stanford (n=32), terminated in 2021 for an unrelated reason stated in the registry: the principal investigator left the institution, not COVID-19. Four terminated trials stopped at n ≤ 6.

Two large phase 3 programs terminated for business rather than scientific reasons: NCT05011396 (PaliSADe-2, VistaGen Therapeutics, 15 sites, terminated at n=228) and its companion open-label safety study NCT05030350 (terminated at n=481) — both testing the neuroactive nasal spray PH94B (fasedienol), both stopped by the sponsor for business reasons unrelated to any safety signal. Two large late-phase terminations in the same program, in the same year, is a pipeline collapse worth naming rather than treating as background noise. The cannabinoid pipeline for SAD collapsed even earlier and more completely: NCT04086342 (Canopy Growth, CHI-902, PHASE2b) and NCT05429788 (Receptor Life Sciences, inhaled CBD RLS103) both withdrew with zero participants enrolled, the former for pipeline changes and the latter for lack of enrollment. A waitlist-controlled smartphone CBT trial, NCT04622930 (Massachusetts General Hospital), was withdrawn before starting at all. And one trial remains SUSPENDED rather than closed: NCT03240003 (New York State Psychiatric Institute, gaze-contingent music reward therapy, n=60 estimated), halted for lack of funding with a primary completion date still nominally listed as 2026-12-01.

### 15.9 Novel augmentation, reported honestly: MDMA, ketamine, and the pharmacological adjuncts

The registered psychedelic-and-SAD literature is small enough to state as a single fact: crossing SAD against MDMA, psilocybin, ketamine, or "psychedelic" in the registry returns five records total, two of them MDMA-assisted therapy, one ketamine, one an fMRI observational study nested inside the MDMA program, and one a single-subject psychoanalysis observational record. No psilocybin trial with SAD as an indexed condition exists in the registry at all. That is a genuine absence, not a null finding, and any claim about "psychedelics for social anxiety" beyond MDMA specifically is unsupported by the registered evidence.

The MDMA evidence itself must be reported at exactly the size it is. Danforth and colleagues' trial (registered as NCT02008396) enrolled 12 participants total — 8 to MDMA, 4 to inactive placebo — and this is the *entire* double-blind evidence base for MDMA-assisted therapy in social anxiety. The population was autistic adults with marked-to-very-severe social anxiety, not a general SAD clinical sample, selected further by a registered inclusion requirement of at least two years of college-level or vocational education and willingness to withdraw psychiatric medication for at least five half-lives plus a week. The design was dose-escalation across two eight-hour experimental sessions roughly a month apart, each bracketed by three preparatory and three integration non-drug sessions, with the primary outcome being LSAS change from baseline to one month after the second experimental session. The result: p = 0.037, placebo-subtracted Cohen's d = 1.4 (95% CI −0.074 to 2.874); at six months, p = 0.036, d = 1.1 (95% CI −0.307 to 2.527). Both confidence intervals cross zero. A "very large" point estimate with an interval that includes no effect whatsoever is exactly what a sample of twelve produces. The trial's own registered title describes it as a "Phase 2 Pilot Safety Study… Dose Finding," its stated primary objective was collecting safety data on tolerability, and it was not powered or designed to estimate efficacy. Its authors' own conclusion was that the findings support expansion into larger samples — not that they establish efficacy. Anything stronger than "a twelve-participant dose-finding safety pilot in a highly selected population produced a large but statistically fragile signal" overstates what exists.

The follow-on trial, SAMATI (NCT05138068), is a randomized, open-label, delayed-treatment-control phase 2 study in moderate-to-severe *generalized* SAD, sponsored by Jason B. Luoma with MAPS and Portland Psychotherapy as collaborators — three preparatory sessions, two MDMA medicine sessions, six integration sessions across roughly sixteen weeks, with the delayed arm waiting the same sixteen weeks before crossing over. Its primary outcome is LSAS assessed by a blinded independent rater at post-treatment and twenty-six weeks later, with a substantial secondary mechanism panel targeting shame, thwarted belongingness, and self-concealment. There is an unresolved discrepancy in the record that should be flagged rather than smoothed over: the published protocol specifies 20 participants (10 immediate, 10 delayed), and the registry's own detailed description repeats "each of the 20 subjects" — yet the registry's enrollment field reads 90. The trial is marked COMPLETED with a completion date of 2025-08-12, has posted no results, and no results publication was found in PubMed at the time of this sweep. Until a paper appears, SAMATI's actual analyzable sample size and effect estimate are simply unknown. It is also worth noting the design is open-label, meaning expectancy is uncontrolled by construction in a treatment whose subjective effects during the session itself are unmistakable to the participant.

Ketamine has one adequately designed trial and it should be read as a genuine proof-of-concept, not more. Taylor and colleagues (registered as NCT02083926) randomized 18 adults with DSM-5 SAD to a double-blind, placebo-controlled crossover of ketamine 0.5 mg/kg over 40 minutes versus normal saline, with a 28-day washout and 14-day follow-up. The blinded clinician-rated outcome moved: LSAS Time × Treatment F = 2.6, p = 0.01, with 33.33% of ketamine-treated participants meeting a greater-than-35% LSAS reduction within two weeks against 0% on placebo (p = 0.025). The patient-rated continuous outcome did not move at all: VAS-Anxiety Time × Treatment F = 0.4, p = 0.95 — a flat null. This dissociation between blinded clinician rating and self-report is worth naming explicitly rather than averaging away; it is exactly the kind of discrepancy a single small trial cannot resolve. No follow-on ketamine trial for SAD appears anywhere in the registry.

The most useful context for both of these pilots is the cautionary tale sitting right next to them: d-cycloserine augmentation of exposure therapy is a *completed, adequately powered* literature, and it is flatly null. Four large trials completed — 169 participants at Massachusetts General Hospital, 169 at Boston University, 152 in Smits's dose-timing study, 48 at NIMH — feeding into Ori and colleagues' Cochrane synthesis of 21 RCTs and 788 outpatients, of which 5 were in social anxiety disorder specifically. Adult treatment response at endpoint: RR 1.10 (95% CI 0.89–1.34), n=449 across 9 RCTs, low quality. Follow-up to twelve months: RR 1.08 (0.90–1.31). Children and adolescents at endpoint: RR 1.01 (0.78–1.31), also low quality. The authors attribute the null partly to the high efficacy of exposure-based therapy alone — a ceiling effect that will doom any augmentation trial layered on top of a treatment that already works well. A mechanistically compelling story, four adequately powered trials, nothing. It is the standard the MDMA and ketamine pilots should ultimately be held to before either is treated as more than promising.

The remainder of the augmentation shelf is thin and mostly negative. Two oxytocin trials completed (NCT05968651, n=121, University of Washington; NCT01856530, n=60, Hofmann) with no pooled SAD efficacy estimate locatable. Propranolol reconsolidation blockade has already been covered above — a single phase 4 trial terminated at n=6. Yohimbine augmentation of group CBT completed at n=40 (NCT00958880). And beyond the cannabinoid collapse already described in section 15.8, the SSRI/SNRI comparator literature itself — 33 SSRI and 6 SNRI studies pooled — shows response RR 1.62 and LSAS mean difference −9.65 for SSRIs, RR 1.57 and LSAS mean difference −11.72 for SNRIs, with dropout not significantly different from placebo, and the meta-analysts' own statement that most findings rested on low quality of evidence. Novel augmentation in this disorder, across every mechanism tried so far, has either produced a null on an adequately powered sample or a promising-but-fragile signal on a pilot too small to trust.

### 15.10 What would settle the open questions

Six trials do not currently exist, and each open controversy above traces to a specific one of them.

**A head-to-head of individual cognitive therapy against Heimberg-style cognitive-behavioral group therapy, run by an investigator group with no allegiance to either.** Every trial establishing individual therapy's superiority shares the Clark lineage. What is needed is a multi-site randomized superiority (or non-inferiority) trial, blinded independent raters, manualized fidelity checks on both arms, conducted by a coalition of centers with no developmental stake in either protocol, ideally pre-registered with a data-sharing agreement that removes the option to quietly not publish a null. Absent this, the individual-versus-group superiority claim will remain, correctly, a claim about one research lineage rather than a claim about the treatments.

**A component-randomized dismantling trial of individual cognitive therapy itself**, isolating video feedback, attention retraining, behavioral experiments, and safety-behavior withdrawal as separately randomizable modules, powered to detect which components carry the acute effect and which carry the two-year effect that SOPHO-NET shows converging across treatments. The field has never run a factorial dismantling large enough to separate "the protocol" from "its most active ingredient" — the closest existing analogue is a small factorial dismantling of exposure-only versus exposure-plus-cognitive-restructuring components in adolescent speech anxiety, far too small and narrow to answer the adult SAD question.

**An adequately powered, blinded-rater, non-inferiority trial of internet-delivered cognitive therapy against face-to-face cognitive therapy in adult SAD**, powered specifically to distinguish "internet delivery is equivalent" from "internet delivery is merely not proven inferior on an underpowered sample" — the existing internet-versus-face-to-face literature (Cochrane's pooled RR 1.09, 95% CI 0.89–1.34) is graded low quality across the board, and the field is making large resource-allocation decisions on it. Given that a single existing trial already shows internet delivery achieving equal outcomes at 41% of the therapist time, this is the single highest-value trial the implementation-gap problem needs and does not have.

**A trial testing whether treatment-gap closure interventions — not treatment protocols — change outcomes at a population level**, randomizing primary care clinics to a detection-plus-automatic-referral pathway for SAD specifically (mirroring what already exists for depression) against usual detection, with adequate treatment receipt and LSAS-verified remission as co-primary outcomes. Chapdelaine's finding that detecting SAD does not raise the odds of adequate treatment is an observational association from one cohort; nothing has tested whether an intervention aimed at closing exactly that gap actually closes it.

**A low-risk-of-bias-restricted re-analysis of the entire SAD psychotherapy literature**, applying the sensitivity analysis that dissolved EMDR's apparent superiority when restricted to low-risk trials, to ask whether individual cognitive therapy's advantage over group formats and over psychodynamic therapy survives the same restriction. This does not require a new trial, only the systematic-review labor to run it — and its absence, given how directly analogous the EMDR precedent is, is a genuine gap rather than a resource constraint.

**An adequately powered, blinded-rater trial of MDMA-assisted therapy in a general (non-autistic, non-selected) SAD population**, sized to produce a confidence interval that does not span zero — something neither the twelve-participant pilot nor, on current information, SAMATI's genuinely uncertain analyzable sample appears able to do. Until that trial exists and reports, MDMA-assisted therapy for social anxiety disorder remains a promising direction with a fragile evidence base, not a validated treatment, and this manual should not be read to imply otherwise.

- German SPIN cut-off 25 (representative n=2,043, clinical n=164) — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2007.08.011](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2007.08.011)

- GSAD/AvPD structural analysis (n=335): two-factor fit; AvPD patients more severe but showed more early change in treatment — [doi:10.1002/da.20349](https://doi.org/10.1002/da.20349)
- Guidance on persistent negative self-evaluations and therapist drift — [doi:10.1017/s1754470x22000496](https://doi.org/10.1017/s1754470x22000496)
- Guided and unguided self-help for SAD, pure bibliotherapy beats waitlist, gains maintained 1 year — [doi:10.1192/bjp.bp.108.060996](https://doi.org/10.1192/bjp.bp.108.060996)
- Guided vs unguided internet ACT with smartphone app (N=152): social anxiety d=0.70 vs waitlist, no guided-vs-unguided difference — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2016.09.012](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2016.09.012)
- Guided vs unguided internet psychodynamic therapy (N = 181): guided d = 1.07 (0.72–1.43), unguided d = 0.61 (0.25–0.98), guided over unguided d = 0.46 (0.11–0.80), no difference at 6 or 12 months — [doi:10.1038/s44184-024-00063-0](https://doi.org/10.1038/s44184-024-00063-0)
- GxT (N=1,519): social anxiety disorder predicts significantly poorer CBT response and remission than GAD across individual, group and guided self-help modalities; parental involvement not predictive — [doi:10.1016/j.jaac.2015.03.018](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jaac.2015.03.018)
- HARP 65-week predictors: mean illness duration 18 years, no variable predicted outcome — [doi:10.1097/00005053-199405000-00008](https://doi.org/10.1097/00005053-199405000-00008)
- HARP 8-year sex-stratified: 38% women / 32% men complete remission, women more agoraphobia, men more SUD — [doi:10.1176/appi.ps.52.5.637](https://doi.org/10.1176/appi.ps.52.5.637)
- Head-to-head comparison of SIAS/SPS short forms: SIPS and SIAS-6/SPS-6 best supported (n=3,607+283) — [doi:10.1037/a0037063](https://doi.org/10.1037/a0037063)
- Head-to-head RCT: inhibitory retrieval vs habituation exposure, null on primary interviewer-rated outcomes, modest self-report advantage (N=89, 77 SAD) — [doi:10.1037/ccp0000970](https://doi.org/10.1037/ccp0000970)
- Highly shy vs normatively shy: generalized SP gradient, but nongeneralized SP equal at 14% vs 14% — [doi:10.1016/s0887-6185(02)00124-x](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0887-6185(02)00124-x)
- Historical guided-exposure-with/without-cognitive-restructuring trial for severe social phobia, the anchor for the dismantling literature (no abstract in PubMed, no numbers asserted) — [doi:10.1037//0022-006x.56.2.251](https://doi.org/10.1037//0022-006x.56.2.251)
- Historical origin of taijin-kyofusho in 1930s Japan — [doi:10.1007/s40656-021-00392-9](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40656-021-00392-9)
- Hofmann mediation trial (N=90, CBGT vs exposure group therapy without cognitive intervention vs waitlist): no difference at post-test, only CBGT continued improving to 6 months, estimated social cost as mediator — [doi:10.1037/0022-006x.72.3.392](https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-006x.72.3.392)
- Home-delivered CBT for depression improved anxiety/phobic-anxiety subscales in rural older adults (secondary analysis, not SAD-diagnosed) — [doi:10.1080/07317115.2017.1288670](https://doi.org/10.1080/07317115.2017.1288670)
- Homework compliance and CBGT outcome: first/late-period compliance predicted lower post-treatment anxiety, middle-session compliance linked to worse fear of scrutiny, locus of control added nothing — [doi:10.1016/0005-7967(96)00014-9](https://doi.org/10.1016/0005-7967(96)00014-9)
- Homework compliance meta-analysis: quantity g=0.79/0.51, quality g=0.78/1.07 (post/follow-up) — [doi:10.1016/j.beth.2016.05.002](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.beth.2016.05.002)
- Hong Kong SIAS-6/SPS-6 construct validation (n=925) — [doi:10.1080/07853890.2023.2210842](https://doi.org/10.1080/07853890.2023.2210842)
- Hong Kong therapist training and dissemination case series — [doi:10.2196/13446](https://doi.org/10.2196/13446)
- Hungarian SIAS-6/SPS-6 IRT validation: fit indices, ω=.87/.88, test–retest r=.90/.92, clinical vs control d=1.4/1.3, revised SPS-6 cut-off recommendation — [doi:10.1186/s12888-021-03174-6](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12888-021-03174-6)
- IAPT cohort (n=1,249): personality-disorder screening score predicts worse outcome, unconfounded by severity/sessions — [doi:10.1016/j.brat.2015.07.006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.brat.2015.07.006)
- iCBT as add-on to treatment as usual, low-certainty small benefit, no SAD-specific evidence — [doi:10.1016/j.jad.2022.11.036](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jad.2022.11.036)
- iCBT effect on quality of life across depression/anxiety, overall g=0.35 — [doi:10.1016/j.invent.2023.100654](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.invent.2023.100654)
- ICBT vs CBGT for SAD, non-inferiority RCT, responder rates and LSAS CIs — [doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0018001](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0018001)
- ICD-11 classification structure: lifespan grouping, focus-of-apprehension organizing principle, essential-features format — [doi:10.1002/da.22530](https://doi.org/10.1002/da.22530)
- ICD-11 field study reliability: SAD kappa 0.88, most reliable diagnosis of 1,806 patients across 13 countries — [doi:10.1002/wps.20524](https://doi.org/10.1002/wps.20524)
- ICD-11 timeline (WHA approval May 2019, reporting from 2022) and culture-related guidance per disorder — [doi:10.1002/wps.20611](https://doi.org/10.1002/wps.20611)

- Imagery rescripting pilot, own-control design, n=11 — [doi:10.1016/j.beth.2007.04.003](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.beth.2007.04.003)
- Imagery rescripting vs cognitive restructuring (N=60): equivalent effects, differing pathways — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2016.03.009](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2016.03.009)
- Imagery rescripting vs imaginal exposure vs supportive counselling (N=33): distinct memory-content changes; only rescripting updated negative core beliefs — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2019.102169](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2019.102169)
- Imagery rescripting without cognitive restructuring vs reading task (N=14): improved memory and image distress, fear of negative evaluation and social interaction anxiety at 1 week — [doi:10.1016/j.brat.2012.03.004](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.brat.2012.03.004)
- Imagery rescripting, within-session and one-week change, n=14 — [doi:10.1016/j.jbtep.2007.07.003](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbtep.2007.07.003)
- Impaired theory of mind predicts reduced expression of fear of negative evaluation — [doi:10.1037/abn0000724](https://doi.org/10.1037/abn0000724)
- Implicit and explicit self-esteem improved in both cognitive and psychodynamic therapy — [doi:10.1080/10503307.2013.802824](https://doi.org/10.1080/10503307.2013.802824)
- Individual CT vs group CBT vs attentional placebo in adolescents (n=57): individual CT superior at post and 12 months; group CBT not different from placebo — [doi:10.1159/000354672](https://doi.org/10.1159/000354672)
- Individual exposure plus group social skills training (SET-C model) with or without parent education, n=55 children 8–14: no difference between active arms, both beat waitlist, maintained at 1-year follow-up, comorbidity did not worsen outcome — [doi:10.1016/j.brat.2015.02.001](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.brat.2015.02.001)
- Individual vs group CBT effectiveness trial in Norwegian community clinics: full diagnostic recovery only 20.5-25.3% — [doi:10.1016/j.brat.2014.03.007](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.brat.2014.03.007)
- Individual vs group delivery of the same Clark & Wells cognitive therapy, n=71 randomized (65 post, 59 at 6 months): individual superior to group at post-treatment and 6-month follow-up — [doi:10.1016/s0005-7967(02)00176-6](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0005-7967(02)00176-6)
- Individual-participant meta-analysis: neither subclinical fears nor SAD diagnosis predicts incident AUD — [doi:10.1007/s00127-018-1569-z](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00127-018-1569-z)
- Individual-participant-data meta-analysis of D-cycloserine: 21 of 22 trials, 1,047 participants, d≈-0.25 post-treatment — [doi:10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2016.3955](https://doi.org/10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2016.3955)

- Inhibitory learning approach to exposure: the eight optimization strategies, worked social phobia case (worksheet loop, hierarchy ordering by learning need, expectancy-violation wording, post-exposure-only cognitive work, forbidden mid-exposure distraction, desirable in-session variability, safety-signal removal, occasional reinforced extinction/"shame attacks", retrieval-cue rules, cognitively prepared video feedback) — [doi:10.1016/j.brat.2014.04.006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.brat.2014.04.006)

- Integrated Behavior Therapy for Selective Mutism pilot RCT: 75% responders by blind evaluation — [doi:10.1016/j.brat.2013.07.003](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.brat.2013.07.003)

- Intensified weekend-group task concentration training vs cognitive therapy for fear of blushing — [doi:10.1002/cpp.1975](https://doi.org/10.1002/cpp.1975)

- Intensive group cognitive treatment (16 sessions/3 weeks) vs individual cognitive therapy (16 sessions/4 months) vs SSRI treatment-as-usual, n=100: ICT superior to both, IGCT equal to TAU despite lasting only 3 weeks — [doi:10.1111/j.1600-0447.2006.00839.x](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0447.2006.00839.x)
- Internet acceptance-based CBT with vs without therapist support (n=42): equivalent outcomes, attrition 20% vs 50% — [doi:10.1177/0145445517694457](https://doi.org/10.1177/0145445517694457)

- Medical-student screen-positive meta-analysis: pooled 45.8%, cautionary datum — [doi:10.1186/s40359-026-04874-y](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40359-026-04874-y)

- Meta-analysis of dropout in individual psychotherapy for GAD (45 studies, 2,224 participants): weighted mean dropout 16.99% — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2017.10.001](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2017.10.001)
- Meta-analysis of dropout/engagement in internet-delivered anxiety trials (140 trials, N=11,021): 46% eligible, 98% started, 81% completed modules, 21% dropout — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2022.102638](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2022.102638)
- Meta-analysis of enduring effects of psychological treatments for anxiety disorders (93 RCTs, 152 psychotherapy arms): gains maintained to 24 months but medication and placebo arms also remained stable — [doi:10.1192/bjp.2018.49](https://doi.org/10.1192/bjp.2018.49)

- Meta-analysis of group psychotherapy across anxiety disorders, 57 studies, 3,656 participants: g=0.92 vs no treatment, g=0.29 vs common-factor controls, g=0.24 (n.s.) vs individual, g=−0.05 (n.s.) vs pharmacotherapy, researcher allegiance a significant moderator — [doi:10.1080/10503307.2020.1729440](https://doi.org/10.1080/10503307.2020.1729440)
- Meta-analysis of group psychotherapy for SAD, 36 RCTs, 2,171 patients: CBGT vs waitlist g=0.84 (specific symptoms) and g=0.62 (general psychopathology); superior to common-factor controls; no difference vs individual psychotherapy or pharmacotherapy on direct comparison — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2016.02.005](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2016.02.005)
- Meta-analysis of pre/post-therapy neuroimaging: psychotherapy and pharmacotherapy affect different regions, no amygdala difference among groups (12 studies, 295 subjects) — [doi:10.4088/jcp.15r10226](https://doi.org/10.4088/jcp.15r10226)
- Meta-analysis of psychological interventions for SAD in youth (17 RCTs): SMD -1.13, remission RR 8.99, NNT 3.3, dropout RR 1.00 — [doi:10.1007/s00787-018-1189-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00787-018-1189-x)
- Meta-analysis of psychotherapy in autism: CBT for anxiety in children g=-0.70 (not SAD-specific); SST g=-0.35/-0.34 — [doi:10.1017/s0033291722003415](https://doi.org/10.1017/s0033291722003415)
- Meta-analysis of remission in CBT for adult anxiety disorders (100 articles): overall mean remission 51.0%; SAD and OCD lowest of all anxiety disorders — [doi:10.1016/j.cpr.2018.03.002](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cpr.2018.03.002)
- Meta-analysis of response rates for CBT for anxiety disorders (87 studies): 49.5% post-treatment/53.6% follow-up; rates vary by definition; blinded assessors yield higher rates — [doi:10.1016/j.cpr.2015.08.004](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cpr.2015.08.004)
- Meta-analysis of VRET attrition (46 studies, N=1,057): 16% overall, no difference vs in-vivo exposure — [doi:10.1016/j.janxdis.2018.06.006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.janxdis.2018.06.006)
- Meta-analysis: amygdala/insula hyperactivity across SAD, phobia and PTSD, more frequent in SAD/phobia; prefrontal hypoactivation PTSD-specific — [doi:10.1176/appi.ajp.2007.07030504](https://doi.org/10.1176/appi.ajp.2007.07030504)
- Meta-analysis: behavioural inhibition confers OR 7.59 for developing SAD; ~15% extreme BI, ~half develop SAD (7 studies) — [doi:10.1016/j.jaac.2012.08.002](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jaac.2012.08.002)
- Meta-analysis: post-event rumination correlates r=0.45 (95% CI 0.40–0.50) with social anxiety, significantly moderated by measure and task — [doi:10.1016/j.jpsychires.2024.03.013](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpsychires.2024.03.013)

- Metacognitive belief change uniquely predicts improvement in interpersonal problems beyond social-phobic cognitions and symptoms (n=52) — [doi:10.1111/sjop.12943](https://doi.org/10.1111/sjop.12943)
